What the Mountain Keeps

What the Mountain Keeps

Part I — The lamp without a flame

Chapter 1 — The borrowed pot

The good pot was on the table, full of somebody else’s supper.

Aevra stood in the doorway with her tool bag against her knee. Through the steam she could see the glossy brown skins of onions, a shoulder bone, the end of the wooden spoon Maeven had been asked repeatedly not to leave standing in anything hot. Her aunt had given them that spoon. Its handle was splitting. Aevra had spent the morning telling a customer that a thing could remain useful for years after its first crack, and did not want the argument repeated to her over supper.

“That was for tomorrow,” she said.

“The meat?”

“The pot.”

Maeven looked down at it as though it had arrived without her assistance. She had flour along one forearm and a red mark at the bridge of her nose where she had pushed her spectacles up with the wrong part of her hand.

“Tomorrow you shall have it again.”

“With the onions still in it?”

“Depends how long you stand there.”

Drevik rose from the bench. He had put on the embroidered waistcoat their surface relatives wore for visits, a good piece of work, too warm for this room. He caught its lower edge on the table as he stood and set three cups trembling.

“Stay where you are,” Aevra told him. “I’m only going to complain.”

His father occupied the chair nearest the flue. Someone had folded a blanket under him to bring his feet level with the low stool. When he had arrived three weeks earlier, he had said a stool would make him feel like an invalid. Now he tapped it with one slipper until Drevik pushed it into the right place.

“You’re late,” he said.

“The stair at the cooper’s was worse than he said.”

“They always are.”

“He’d cut into the cheek to get a barrel round the turn.”

“Was there nobody to tell him?”

“There was me. Last autumn.”

Her uncle laughed, then pressed the folded cloth at his belt against his mouth. The cough was brief. Aevra set down her bag before he could notice her noticing it.

Kharveth’s evening service bell sounded through the open door. Its lower note came up the stairwell; the higher traveled through the flue behind her uncle’s chair and seemed to strike within the room. From the gallery outside came the trundle of a handcart and a woman calling for a child to leave the rail alone. The child called back that he wasn’t touching it. Aevra’s younger sister, seated at the other end of the table, leaned out and said he certainly was.

“You have my blue bowl,” Aevra told her.

“I brought it back.”

“Empty?”

“Would you rather I’d kept it?”

Aevra kissed Maeven where the flour had reached her cheek, took the washing basin from its hook and went to the small water recess. The day’s grit lay in the folds of her fingers. She had a strip of scraped skin along the right thumb, where a young man had moved a stone after being told to wait. He had apologized so fervently that she had eventually apologized herself. She was still annoyed about it.

Hot water came slowly through the household pipe. She used the first cold run to wet the cloth around her neck, then rubbed behind her ears, over her forehead, into the short curls flattened by her work cap. The room smelled of meat and bread, with the sharper mineral breath of the gallery underneath. Maeven had opened the upper shutters to let the cooking steam out. Beyond them, the lights of the opposite houses climbed in staggered rows toward the dark ribs of the chamber. A neighbor had hung pale washing above a yellow window. It turned gently whenever the main air doors opened far below.

Drevik’s father had asked for those shutters to remain open even when he was cold. He liked to look across at people moving in their rooms. At home on the surface, Drevik said, he had watched the road.

Aevra dried her hands and reached for the stool beside Maeven.

The familiar room had settled around her before she had looked at any of its joins. As a child she had felt a moved partition for days, an irritation she could not locate by touching her skin. She still disliked the recess behind her uncle’s chair, though she had inspected it herself and knew it was sound. Maeven liked that recess. She said the flue made the room feel open at the back.

“Other side,” Maeven said. “Your uncle can hear you better there.”

Her place had a plate already set. This irritated her more than the pot. Drevik slid the bread toward her with the discreet concentration he gave anything involving somebody else’s convenience.

“Have you eaten?” she asked him.

“A little.”

“He has inspected a little,” his father said. “I told him the bone wouldn’t improve by examination.”

“I was cutting it for you.”

“You cut yours that small as well. He thinks I won’t notice.”

Maeven gave Aevra a generous helping. The onions had almost dissolved where they lay against the meat. There was pepper in the sauce, more than they usually used. Aevra tasted it and stopped looking for something to be cross about.

“Where did you get the shoulder?”

“Your sister brought it.”

“In my bowl?”

“It was wrapped.”

Her sister smiled and took another slice of bread.

They ate with the door still open. A boy from the next stair came to return a baking peel and stayed for the heel of the loaf. Maeven asked whether his mother had found the missing clasp. He said she had, in the pocket she had already searched twice, but he was not supposed to say where. Aevra offered him an onion. He declined with the offended courtesy of a guest much older than himself.

At the center of the table, the family lamp smoked.

It was a low bronze thing with two mouths for the wicks and a carrying bail folded over the reservoir. Her uncle had brought it from the surface wrapped in a shirt. Aevra remembered it looking larger when she was a child. Beside it stood a shallow cup of clean oil and the little hooked pick Drevik used to lift the wicks without dirtying his fingers.

A bright bead gathered beneath the lamp’s left side, swelled, and dropped onto the wood.

Aevra reached across for the empty dish beside her sister.

“Not that one,” her uncle said.

“It’s leaking.”

“I can see that. It shouldn’t sit in a dish.”

“It shouldn’t sit in the table either.”

Drevik put down his knife. “I’ll take it away.”

“Leave it. We’ve only just lit it.”

Aevra lifted the lamp by the bail, careful of the heat gathering in the hinge, and slipped the dish underneath. Her uncle watched with his mouth drawn in. One wick flickered close to extinction before recovering.

“The seam’s opened again,” Drevik said.

“When was it last done?”

“Before we came up.”

“By whom?”

“Me.”

“Ah.”

He smiled reluctantly. “You needn’t make that noise.”

“I hadn’t finished making it.”

“The old metal’s thin. I didn’t want to chase the split farther round.”

Aevra looked at the dark line beneath the lip. The repair was neat. It was also failing. She passed the lamp back to its dish without offering to improve it.

Her uncle caught the pick between two fingers and lowered the nearer wick. His hand shook enough that Drevik moved, then stopped when his father glanced at him.

“That’s better,” the old man said.

The flame was barely above the bronze mouth. Aevra could not see how it was better, but the lamp no longer smoked.

Her sister was wrapping a portion of meat in a covered bowl. She had left the bone out and added several pieces of softened onion.

“For next door?” Maeven asked.

“For the upper stair. I said I’d bring it while it was warm.”

“How is she?”

“Sleeping badly. Her brother’s there.”

Maeven nodded. “Take the small loaf as well. There’s another in the cloth.”

Drevik looked from one to the other. “The woman whose husband—”

“Yes,” Aevra’s sister said.

“They haven’t brought him out?”

“No.”

“Is it still moving?”

“The whole face is sitting on the second support. They aren’t opening it again.”

Drevik’s knife lay across his plate. “Ever?”

“Not for him.”

Her sister tightened the cord around the bowl. She did it twice before the knot held. Her thumb was clumsy; she had burned it the day before.

“Was he alone?” Drevik asked.

“He was the last.”

Aevra knew what had happened. She had seen the man on the morning he died, buying a hot cake from the woman outside the lower lift. He had been annoyed because the cake had no filling in its middle. He had broken it open to demonstrate, holding the two halves up while the seller denied having any way to look inside them.

“His brother’s bringing the older boy back,” she said. “From the foothills. They expect him tomorrow.”

“Does the boy know?”

“They sent word ahead.”

Maeven reached over the table and put the remaining good pieces of meat into the bowl. Aevra began to object, then took the spoon and scraped in the sauce herself.

Drevik sat very still. He had done that when they were children and she had told him how a hand could be caught in a hoist. He had wanted to know which part of the hand. She had told him, delighted to possess knowledge he did not, and he had gone away without speaking to her for an afternoon.

Her sister lifted the bowl. “She likes the carrots you do.”

“I can make them tomorrow,” Maeven said.

“Not too sweet.”

“I know.”

The boy with the baking peel had gone. Aevra’s sister took the food out after him, carrying it in the folded end of her shawl. Drevik watched her cross the gallery and turn up the narrow stair. In the window beyond, a woman moved back to make room for her.

“Is that your last loaf?” he asked Maeven.

“For tonight.”

“I could go down.”

“There’s enough.”

“I wouldn’t mind.”

“Then bring bread in the morning. Warm if you can.”

He nodded and picked up his knife. Aevra offered him the pot. This time he took a proper helping.

After supper, they cleared the table around the lamp. Her uncle protested when Maeven tried to move his chair away from the flue, then admitted he was hot. Aevra loosened the back of his collar. His neck had become shockingly narrow. She could feel the knobs of his spine through skin soft as the underside of her wrist.

“Too much meat,” he said.

“You ate three bites.”

“They were large bites.”

“Drevik cut them.”

“Very large for him.”

She laughed, and he leaned his head back briefly against her arm.

Maeven had kept two baked apples in the warm recess. Aevra ate hers standing while they argued about whether the morning visitor had meant to leave a pair of door fittings or collect them. Maeven made handles and hinges in the workshop across the gallery. People carried a broken thing into her room and expected the memory of having put it down to constitute an order. Aevra did much the same with her tool repairs and was told so before she could express an opinion.

Her uncle fell asleep. Drevik spread a cloth over his knees and sat at the table, working the lamp’s pick through a little wad of fiber. Outside, the household lights began to diminish. The lower gallery remained busy; late shift workers were returning from the waterworks, and someone had begun singing in an aggressively cheerful voice.

Aevra carried the dishes to the recess. Maeven followed with the good pot.

“There is another,” she said quietly.

“It’s too wide for my carrier.”

“I mean supper. For tomorrow. I put some by before they came.”

Aevra turned, wet hands hanging over the basin.

Maeven kissed her, quickly, on the mouth. “You are very easy to feed and very difficult to tell that you have been fed.”

“I was going to say thank you.”

“Then don’t let me prevent it.”

Aevra put her hands on Maeven’s waist, leaving two damp marks. Maeven laughed and pushed her away with an elbow.

From the table, her uncle said, “Do you think it’s there?”

Aevra looked through the open recess door. He had woken without moving his head. Drevik held the cleaned pick in his lap.

“What?”

“The proper one.”

“The lamp?”

“It was put beside Orrvek. They didn’t throw everything away when they closed the road.”

“No.”

“I should like to see it.”

Drevik bent forward. “I know.”

“You say that as though I’d asked to see my mother.”

“I didn’t mean—”

“It’s a thing. A bronze thing. Somebody could pick it up.”

The upper air door opened. Across the chamber, the neighbor’s pale washing lifted and subsided.

Aevra turned back to the basin before Drevik answered. She washed the spoon, testing the split with her thumbnail, and put it aside where Maeven would find it in the morning.

Chapter 2 — Work on the bell

Drelset brought the bell in a barrow intended for mortar and tipped river mud across Aevra’s clean working boards.

“Don’t,” she said, too late.

He looked down. “There was a cloth.”

“Underneath it?”

“That would have been the place.”

The bell was small enough for one person to lift, though an inconvenient shape to carry. Its mouth had filled with black grit and a length of pale root. Water ran out through the clapper hole when Drelset rolled it on its side. A broken hanging strap remained bolted to the crown. Along one edge the bronze had been rubbed bright by something harder.

Aevra put her bread on the high shelf.

“Where did you get it?”

“Lower catch.”

“Which lower catch?”

“The one I’m pointing at when I say lower.”

“Drelset.”

“Below the third settling stair. Zerla’s people pulled the screen this morning. It was in with a dead sheep and most of a tree.”

“A sheep?”

“Looked like one. I didn’t bring it for you.”

The workshop occupied a long bay beside the public lifting shaft. Daylight did not reach this level, but three large lamps hung behind polished sheets of metal above the benches. The sheets gave everything two shadows. At the far end, a pair of apprentices were facing replacement stones for a stair. Their hammers struck in an uneven alternation that kept losing its rhythm whenever they spoke.

Aevra took a wooden scraper from the bench and worked the compacted grit out of the bell’s mouth. It smelled of stagnant water. The root had caught around the clapper pin; the pin itself was newer than the bronze around it.

She rubbed it with her thumb.

“Did the waterworkers touch this?”

“Only to get it off the screen.”

“And you?”

“I rang it.”

“Of course you did.”

“It’s a bell.”

She lifted the remaining root clear. “What did it sound like?”

“A bell.”

The apprentice at the nearest bench laughed. Drelset leaned on the barrow handles with a pleased expression. He was taller than Aevra by enough to mention it when she asked him to reach something. His beard was trimmed close to keep it out of his work, but he had grown two narrow points below the corners of his mouth. She disliked them and suspected this contributed to their continued existence.

The clapper was a length of forged iron with a swollen end. It had been filed where the suspension passed through it. The file marks lay in bright, regular sweeps beneath a thin covering of dark water stain. Aevra tilted the bell toward the lamp.

“That’s been done lately.”

“The river does clean things.”

“Not with a file.”

Drelset stopped smiling. He came round the bench and looked where she pointed.

There was a washer above the clapper loop, a crude piece cut from sheet metal. Its edge had been turned over to stop it biting into the loop. The washer was still flat where the bell would have worn it hollow with long use.

“How lately?” he asked.

“I don’t know.”

“Not a hundred years.”

“Not unless it spent them hanging still.”

One of the apprentices put down his hammer. The sudden loss of the alternating noise made the other look up.

“Can I see?”

“Finish the corner first,” Aevra said.

A cooper arrived carrying the split top step of his stair.

“You said you’d look at this.”

“I looked yesterday.”

“Yes, but now I’ve brought it.”

She closed her eyes for a moment.

The cooper had shaved another piece from the damaged cheek in order to remove the step. He stood it beside her bench and pointed to the clean cut as if presenting evidence in his favor. Aevra made him put it down flat. They discussed, again, how much of the stair could be removed before the remaining stair stopped being one. Drelset took the bell to the wash trough while she drew two lines on the cooper’s piece with a chalk stub and told him neither line was an invitation to cut.

When the cooper had gone, promising not to do anything until the following morning, Drelset had the bell clean.

The royal mark lay on the shoulder beneath the broken strap: three raised bars within an arch, the middle bar cut shorter. Below it, scarcely the breadth of a fingernail, was a little divided square.

Aevra knew the arch. She had seen it on boundary stones from the disused road and on a cup somebody kept in a cupboard because it was supposedly valuable.

“Outhren,” Drelset said.

“Possibly.”

“Who else used it?”

“Someone copying Outhren.”

The apprentice had drifted nearer. “If the road’s open, I’ll take a house.”

“You can’t afford your mother’s room,” Drelset said.

“They’ll be cheaper there.”

“Damp.”

“I like damp.”

“You complain when the washing’s out.”

Aevra sent the apprentice to fetch Drevik from Maeven’s room. Then she sat on the edge of the bench, bell between her boots, and struck it gently with the wooden scraper.

The sound was surprisingly sweet. Its first clear note dwindled into a little trembling burr where the rim had taken a blow. She struck it again, with her ear turned toward the damaged part. The burr was old; the fresh clapper would not mend it.

Drelset heard it too. “Somebody liked it enough to keep.”

“Yes.”

“Could have come in a load of salvage.”

“Yes.”

“Or somebody down there.”

She looked at the new washer. “Could.”

Drevik arrived with bread wrapped in a cloth. He stopped at the threshold, taking in the barrow and the muddy boards, and set the bread on the only clean corner.

“Maeven says that’s for everyone except Drelset, because he still has her punch.”

“I returned it.”

“To whom?”

Drelset thought. “I will return it.”

Aevra pointed to the small square under the royal mark.

Drevik crouched. He was very careful about the knees of his trousers. The divided square had one line carried beyond its edge, ending in a shallow hook. He touched the hook without rubbing it.

“That’s on the drawing.”

“Which drawing?”

“The lamp. At the bottom of the page, beside the porch.”

“Can you fetch it?”

“My father has it.”

“Then ask him.”

Drevik glanced at the bell again. He left the bread without remembering to unwrap it.

While he was gone, Aevra measured the bell’s mouth and crown and wrote the dimensions on a scrap. The hanging strap had been broken recently. Its remaining half was older iron, heavily pitted except where it had lain against the bronze. She tried to imagine the force needed to part it. A fall could do it. So could something pulling the bell from where it hung.

She set that thought aside until she knew more about the river.

Drelset took a piece of bread. “You’re going to look.”

“I’m going to ask.”

“Then look.”

“If the catch has changed, the roadworkers will already have a job for me.”

“They’ve got jobs for you this side of the catch.”

“Thank you. Put the cooper’s step on the list.”

Before Drevik returned, a young woman from the screen crew came looking for the barrow. She stopped when she saw the cleaned bell.

“It comes up well.”

“You found it?” Aevra asked.

“I was there. Zerla.”

Aevra recognized the name from messages that arrived with wet corners. “Did it come through the new opening?”

“We couldn’t see. The water was thick.”

“Was the tree caught first?”

“The tree was yesterday. This was behind it when we lifted the screen.”

Zerla described the work with both hands, placing the screen and the winch in the air between them. The bell had caught by its strap. She had thought it was part of a broken lamp bracket until they hauled it clear. There had been strips of woven material in the same catch, pale and soft with water, which they had left in the waste pile.

“Can you get one?”

“Perhaps. They’ll have cleared it by now.”

“Ask.”

The girl nodded, then glanced at Drelset. “I need the barrow.”

He looked down at the muddy boards and began moving the tools out of it.

Aevra asked one more question. “Did you hear anything through the opening?”

“Water.”

“Nothing else?”

“There was plenty of water.”

Zerla took the barrow away. Aevra liked the answer.

Drevik returned with a flat wooden case tucked under his arm. His father had come with him.

The old man had insisted on his coat and then become too hot on the stairs. He stood breathing through his mouth while Drevik unfastened it. Aevra fetched the chair from her small office. Her uncle waved her away, looked around for somewhere to sit, and accepted the chair without referring to the sequence.

“Show me.”

Drevik laid out the drawing.

The sheet had been copied on good rag paper, folded only once, with worn patches at the fold where earlier hands had tried to reinforce it. It showed an oval lamp from the side and from above. A porch was drawn beneath, out of scale, its pillars ending in a dark horizontal line. Several letters stood beside each feature. One corner contained the divided square.

“That’s it,” her uncle said.

Aevra placed the bell beside the page. The marks matched in their proportions, including the extended lower stroke.

“It may be the maker,” Drevik said. “Or the household. The note only says it’s found on the porch fittings.”

“Orrvek’s people,” his father said.

“It could be the people who made things for them.”

“The same people, probably.”

Aevra asked how old the copy was. Drevik knew. He knew which preceding copy had been damaged, who had made the present one and why the porch drawing was poor. Aevra listened with growing respect and some impatience. She needed the point at which any of it connected to something living.

“Is there a route written down?”

“On the other side.”

He turned the sheet with both hands.

The route began at a stair she knew. Beyond that came three names she did not recognize, a distance expressed in old survey lengths, and an instruction concerning an upper and lower gate.

“The stair is still there,” she said.

Her uncle gripped the arms of the chair. “Then you could go.”

“To the stair.”

“And after it?”

“I haven’t looked.”

“You could look.”

Drevik was staring at the bright clapper pin.

A woman in a waterworks coat arrived while the old man was still explaining why the route would have been properly made. She had a leather satchel flattened by papers and a damp patch on one sleeve where she had leaned against something wet. Aevra recognized Thaereth by the long, old burn along the side of her neck. They had worked together twice, both times on closures somebody else had objected to.

“Is this the thing you’ve stolen?” Thaereth asked Drelset.

“Borrowed.”

“From a screen holding back half a forest.”

“They said I could.”

“I know. They’ve sent me to get it before you hang it over a door.”

She examined the bell, then the drawing. When the apprentice said he had already chosen a house in Outhren, she told him to choose one with a roof.

Aevra showed her the washer.

The woman’s thumb moved over it once. “Where exactly did it come out?”

“Third settling stair,” Drelset said.

“Above or below the new break?”

He did not know. Thaereth took a folded plan from her satchel and spread it on the bench after first moving the bread. The plan showed the catch and the feeder tunnels in a blunt waterworker’s hand. One branch had been marked with a broad stroke. Another, finer line crossed it at an angle.

“This is the break,” she said. “The screen crew were here.”

Drelset pointed. “Then below. It was lying on that side.”

Thaereth’s finger remained on the paper. The joking expression had left her face.

Aevra bent closer. The fine line was an old passage. Where it met the feeder, somebody had drawn a filled rectangle.

“Closed?”

“Supposed to be.”

“How long?”

Thaereth folded back the edge of the plan to read a note. “Long enough.”

“That isn’t a length.”

“No.”

The old man pushed against the chair arms, trying to stand. Drevik helped him before he could refuse.

“Someone’s down there,” he said.

Thaereth looked at him, then at the fresh clapper pin.

“Someone has worked on a bell,” she said. “I can put that in front of them.”

She took the bell away herself, wrapped in the cloth Drelset had left in the bottom of the barrow. Drevik followed her to ask when she would know. His father remained by the bench, looking at the empty place beside the drawing.

Aevra brought him the last slice of bread. He took it without tasting it for a while.

At the other end of the workshop the hammers began again. Drelset swept the mud off the boards, found Maeven’s punch beneath his apron and put it in Aevra’s hand.

Chapter 3 — The version at home

His father wanted the lamp cleaned before breakfast.

Drevik had brought warm bread, as promised, and a little pot of soft cheese bought from a woman beside the upper entrance. His father smelled the bread, asked whether it was fresh, and then said they had better see to the lamp before anybody came to use the table.

“It will still be there after we’ve eaten.”

“I may not feel like doing it after we’ve eaten.”

“You don’t have to do it at all.”

His father looked toward the window.

Drevik spread the old cloth.

The room Maeven had found for them belonged to a cousin away on work. Two beds stood against the inner wall, with a narrow shelf between them. The cousin’s spare boots remained beneath one bed. Her cups hung on pegs above the water jug, each with a pale clean crescent on the hook where it usually rested. Drevik had been using the smallest.

His father disliked the room’s draught and liked its view. Every morning Drevik adjusted the shutters until the contradiction became tolerable. This morning a man was repairing a rail across the chamber, his little work lamp moving slowly along the bars. Below him, the public gallery had begun to fill. The first smell of baking reached them whenever the air door opened.

Drevik set the bread near his father’s hand and unwrapped the lamp.

The leaking oil had made a dark oval in the cloth. He blotted it with a scrap before lifting the lid. Inside, old sediment lay against the reservoir’s lower wall. He could clear it, but the split was outside the bowl and the metal around it had been worked so often that the next repair might start another leak. He had told his father this. His father had listened with the distracted politeness he used when someone explained why a simple request had become difficult.

“Your mother used the fine ash,” the old man said.

“I’ve got it.”

“Not much.”

“I know.”

She had also said that. Not much ash, not much water, do not let the rag become so wet that it dripped inside the hinges. Drevik could hear her instructions over the noise of the street outside their old shop: iron tires on stones, a human hawker shouting the price of fruit, the two girls from next door arguing in Viterran while they carried washing out behind the house. His mother had understood their argument perfectly and pretended she could not when their father asked what had been said.

At Kharveth, people sometimes asked Drevik whether they had kept the proper household observances on the surface. He never knew whether they wanted to hear yes or no.

The human woman next door had once told him he could stop keeping a place in his work for a customer who had died. He had never managed to explain that the waiting did not feel like remembering the man. The unfinished clasp remained present whenever he reached for a file, even on days when he had not thought of its owner. Eventually the man’s daughter brought a different box, and Drevik fitted it there. Only then had his hand stopped finding the space on the bench.

He had washed the hands of their dead. He had repaired buckles for marriages, fitted the little plates carried by people going far from home and sat up through wakes where the language changed as the night went on. At one, a human neighbor had sung the wrong song with such affection that nobody corrected him. Drevik’s mother had joined the last verse, getting half of it wrong herself.

His father picked up a piece of bread. A smear of cheese reached the beard below his lip.

“Leave that edge,” he said. “You’ll take the mark off.”

“The mark’s on the other side.”

“The dent, then.”

Drevik turned the lamp. “This dent wasn’t on the first drawing.”

“It’s an old dent.”

“I know. I remember you dropping it.”

His father’s eyes narrowed. Then he began to laugh, quietly enough that the laugh did not become a cough.

“Your mother told you.”

“I was there.”

“You were under the table.”

“Still there.”

He had been under the table because the room was crowded and he wanted to see the shoes. There had been a wedding that day. Somebody had leaned across for the lamp; his father had caught the bail with his sleeve. The sound when it struck the floor had stopped the conversation in the room. His mother had looked first at the hot oil on the boards, then at Drevik, who had drawn his feet up onto the chair rung.

For years he had remembered the accident chiefly as the occasion on which his father had used a word forbidden in their house.

Now he followed the dent with the edge of his cloth. The copy had acquired its own injuries, none of them shown on the careful paper they had taken to Aevra’s workshop.

“Where did the first drawing come from?” he asked.

“You know.”

“I know who copied this one. Before that.”

“From people who were there.”

“Was it made before the road closed?”

His father chewed for a while. “It must have been.”

“That isn’t written on it.”

“Then write it.”

“I can’t just write it because it must have been.”

“You write plenty of things down that you weren’t there to see.”

Drevik set the cloth aside. “I don’t mean it’s wrong.”

His father turned toward him so sharply that the bread fell from his fingers onto the blanket.

“I have not asked you to go into a mountain that isn’t there.”

“No.”

“Or to fetch a dead man to tell us all how well we’ve done without him.”

“I didn’t say—”

“Every time I mention it you get that face. The face you use with customers who’ve brought you something beyond mending.”

Drevik looked down at the lamp.

His father waited. Outside, the man working on the rail moved his light to the next upright. The bread on the blanket had left a small grease mark.

“I don’t know if they’ll let us go,” Drevik said.

“Ask.”

“Aevra is asking.”

“Then help her.”

He lifted the bread and put it back on the plate. His father’s hand covered his for a moment, dry and warm, the fingers much lighter than he remembered.

“I should like something that was there,” the old man said.

Drevik nodded. He could give no sensible reason why that sentence made him angrier than the rest.

He finished cleaning the lamp after his father had gone back to bed. The outer metal came up to a quiet luster where fingers usually touched it. He left the deeper dark around the hinges and did not test the split again. When Maeven came to see whether they needed hot water, he asked her where the family singer lived.

“Which family?”

“Ours.”

“Oh. The one who knows the words, or the one who keeps singing them?”

He smiled despite himself. “The words.”

She gave him directions to an old woman three galleries over, in rooms above a brewer’s store. The woman had worked at several family wakes and was said to remember verses that other people only pretended to remember. Drevik took the drawing in its case and left the repaired bell’s measurements with his father in case Aevra called.

The singer answered the door wearing a heavy apron over a fine blouse. Her fingers smelled of hops. When he explained why he had come, she looked at the paper case and told him she did not want to be recorded while she was working.

“I only need the words about Orrvek’s porch.”

“Then you’ll have to wait until I’ve finished this.”

“This” was a sack of dried herbs from which stems had to be picked. She put a bowl in front of him and sat opposite. He removed his waistcoat before beginning. She approved of the lining and disapproved of the way one button had been replaced.

“My mother did that.”

“Then she was in a hurry.”

He liked her immediately.

As they worked, she asked about his father, his father’s cough, the journey up and the people still living on their street. She knew two of the families and remembered a marriage Drevik had attended as a child. When she began the porch verse, it came out between one question and the next, without a preparatory solemnity that would have told him to pay attention.

He asked her to repeat it.

“You weren’t listening.”

“I was.”

“Then what did I say?”

He gave her the first two lines. She repeated the rest slowly. He wrote them on his own paper, keeping the old drawing away from the herb dust.

The porch had a low inner lintel. The verse mentioned a figure with a bent head beside it, and a small basin to the right. There was also something about a singer waiting at the lower gate. Drevik knew that part, but not the line she put after it.

“Surely she tells him to wait?”

“She tells him to take his boots off.”

“In that verse?”

“In the one I’m singing.”

“The copy we have says wait.”

“Yes, well, somebody decided that sounded better at a wake.”

He hesitated over the paper. “Which is older?”

“Neither of us.”

She laughed at her own answer and shook more herbs into his bowl.

Drevik asked about the rest of the song. The singer knew several versions and refused to arrange them in the order he wanted. In one, the singer’s beloved had been taken below. In another, they had gone willingly and sent the singer back for a coat. There was a version suitable for children and another she sang for him only after making him shut the door.

The indecent verse was very funny. He laughed, then thought of his father alone in the borrowed room and felt he had stayed too long.

“Take him some of these,” she said, tying a small bundle. “For the water, not the lamp. Tell him that. He once put something of mine in a lamp and smoked half a room out.”

“He won’t remember.”

“He will if you tell him I remember.”

By the time he left, the middle service bell had sounded. Drevik hurried down the public stair, using the outer side as people coming up took the rail. A board at the next turn read WAIT FOR THE LOWER BELL. He stopped.

Others stopped behind him. A woman carrying a basket of hinge blanks asked if somebody had fallen.

“No. The bell.”

“What bell?”

He pointed.

“That’s for when the lift’s running.”

Drevik looked down through the open center of the stair. The lift was stationary several levels below, its empty platform visible in a square of light.

“I couldn’t see.”

“You can from there.”

He could. He had only to take one more step.

Someone behind the woman sighed with an extravagance Drevik might have admired in another circumstance. He moved aside. The traffic passed, and he went down after it with his paper case pressed too tightly beneath his arm.

At the lower landing he almost collided with Aevra.

“Where have you been?” she asked.

“Finding the porch verse.”

“Any use?”

“Possibly. There’s a low lintel. A basin.”

“Good. Thaereth wants to see us tomorrow.”

“Us?”

“You, if you mean to come.”

He had imagined this invitation often enough that its ordinary delivery left him momentarily without an answer. Aevra was looking over her shoulder at a man who had been trying to catch her attention from the lift.

“Yes,” Drevik said.

“You’ll have to carry your own things.”

“Yes.”

“And do what you’re told on the closed road.”

“I can do that.”

She studied him for a moment. “How much have you walked below the occupied galleries?”

He thought of the woman with the basket. “I’ve walked.”

“That wasn’t what I asked.”

“Some. Not far.”

“All right. We’ll see what the route is.”

The man at the lift called again. Aevra swore softly, squeezed Drevik’s shoulder and went to him.

Drevik returned to the borrowed room. His father was awake, looking at the bell measurements. He had drawn a line beneath one figure with a trembling hand.

“That can’t be right,” he said.

“It’s what Aevra measured.”

“Then the old measure’s different.”

“It probably is.”

Drevik sat on the edge of the bed and told him about the appointment. His father’s face changed. The effort he had been putting into seeming merely interested fell away, and he looked both older and more like the man Drevik remembered from the shop.

“I’ll go if they allow it,” Drevik said. “I’ll look.”

His father nodded several times before speaking.

“Take the lamp with you.”

“The copy?”

“To compare.”

“It’s heavy.”

“You carried it all the way here.”

Drevik took the little bundle of herbs out of his pocket and laid it on the shelf. “These go in water.”

“I know what they go in.”

“She said you would say that.”

His father began to laugh. This time the cough caught him in the middle of it. Drevik held the cup while he drank, and waited until the shaking had stopped before asking which cloth they should use to wrap the lamp.

Chapter 4 — Permission for the road

Thaereth’s office had a ceiling too low for the cupboard someone had installed in it. The cupboard leaned forward from the wall. Aevra had noticed it on her previous visit and now had to look at it again while the officer found the right key.

“It will come down,” she said.

“The cupboard?”

“Eventually.”

“Put it in writing.”

“I did.”

Thaereth stopped trying keys and looked at her.

“Three years ago,” Aevra said.

“Before me.”

“It won’t know.”

Drevik stood beside the door with his paper case. He wore his working coat today, which Aevra found encouraging. His boots had been cleaned with an enthusiasm likely to last until the first muddy step.

Thaereth opened the cupboard and took out a flat tray. The bell lay on a cloth inside it, with its broken strap separately wrapped. She put the tray on the table and sat opposite them. There were damp marks on her sleeves again. Aevra wondered whether the woman ever took her coat off.

“The waterworkers say the level below the third stair has dropped since the new break. There’s a ledge exposed on the eastern side. It runs toward your old porch road.”

“Accessible?”

“For a survey. They’ve been as far as the first narrowing.”

“Air?”

“Moving. They didn’t test beyond.”

“Water still falling?”

“Slower now.”

Aevra pulled the plan toward her. The exposed ledge had been drawn in red. Beyond it, the older route continued as a dotted line. Whoever had copied the old distances had added question marks rather than pretending the figures agreed.

“I want to go before it rises again.”

“So do I,” Thaereth said.

Aevra looked up. “You’re coming?”

“I’m responsible for the closure report.”

“Already?”

“For deciding whether it needs one.”

The officer took another sheet from the tray. Its headings were printed, with spaces beneath for location, observed air, remaining access and persons unaccounted for. Aevra had seen such forms at work, though not one with a separate place for a crown mark.

Drevik was reading upside down. “Why does that say unaccounted for?”

“Because that is sometimes what there is to report.”

“But you haven’t found anyone.”

“I haven’t filled it in.”

Aevra turned the sheet over. On the back was a list of stores issued for earlier surveys. Several entries had been crossed through. One line gave a number of lamps; the returned number beside it was smaller.

“Is this the same road?”

“No. That was the west sump approach.”

“To Outhren?”

“To where we thought we might reach it.”

“When?”

Thaereth named the year. Aevra remembered it chiefly for a bad winter and the failure of a lifting chain in the northern works. She did not remember an expedition.

“How many?”

“Ten went. Four came back.”

Drevik sat down without waiting to be invited.

“Were you one?” Aevra asked.

“No. I closed the approach afterwards.”

Thaereth set the form aside, clearing room for the current plan. Aevra watched the paper as if moving it might change what had been written on it.

“What took them?”

“First water. Then bad air in the high chamber where they got out of it. The four who came back stayed at the lower opening. They thought the others would return when they’d found a way up.”

“Could they hear them?” Drevik asked.

“For a while.”

Aevra’s gaze went to the red ledge on the plan.

“This is another approach.”

“Yes.”

“And recent work on the bell.”

“Yes.”

Thaereth was not making it difficult for her to go. That annoyed Aevra, who had arrived ready to insist.

The officer laid out the proposed commission. Eight people. Food for ten days beyond the last staffed station, with a reserve held separately. Oil and wicks issued against a written list. Return to the staffed station by the tenth day after departure unless a runner had brought a new arrangement. No attempt to enter flooded workings. No authority to dismantle supports serving inhabited galleries. If the road proved open, they were to establish what they could without committing the group to a passage from which they could not return.

Aevra ran a finger down the last condition.

“You can write that about any stair.”

“I can.”

“It won’t tell us when to stop.”

“No. We will have to do that.”

Thaereth turned the plan so that the inhabited galleries lay nearest her. Aevra would have put the unexplored stair there. Thaereth kept a finger on a bedroom mark while asking about the reserve oil. Aevra had to follow the lines to bring them to mind.

Drevik asked whether the crypt was included. Thaereth turned to his family paper.

“Accessible memorial property may be recovered with the household’s permission, provided there is no current claimant and the removal does not damage the structure.”

“There won’t be a current claimant.”

“We don’t know that.”

He colored. “I mean it’s ours.”

“Then you should be able to explain that if there is one.”

Aevra touched his sleeve before he could answer. “It’s a lamp. We don’t have to settle it here.”

The officer slid the paper toward him. He read it this time the right way up. His thumb rested over the line concerning damage while his eyes went to the place for his name.

They went from the office to the stores court, where the proposed company was waiting with varying degrees of patience. Drelset sat on a stack of folded carriers, pretending not to be sitting on things clearly marked to be kept clear. Beside him stood a broad man with one shoulder lower than the other. A woman in a stained work apron had taken a lamp apart on the counter. Two others argued about whether a roll of cloth counted as medical stores when it had been cut from a sail.

Thaereth introduced them without ceremony.

Baerun, the broad man, moved loads for the waterworks and knew the capstans along the maintained road. Ildret tended lamps on the lower service route. Kouven dressed injuries and worked in the receiving rooms by the lift. Zerla ran messages and small stores through the waterworks, and knew the new ledge better than anyone else present.

“Have you all worked together?” Aevra asked.

“No,” said Ildret.

“I have with him,” Baerun said, pointing to Drelset.

Drelset looked surprised. “When?”

“The west stair. You put a wedge where I was going to put my hand.”

“You had your hand in the wrong place.”

“There you are. We know each other.”

Zerla was young enough to find this funny and old enough not to laugh while Baerun was looking at her. She had tied her hair close to her head, with one bright thread in the braid. Aevra recognized the thread as the sort sold in the market for festivals and felt relieved by it for no reason she could have explained.

They gathered around a long bench. Thaereth stated the purpose of the survey. Ildret asked about flame requirements, Kouven about the number of people they might find, and Baerun about whether the road would take a loaded sledge.

“We don’t know,” Thaereth said to the second and third questions.

“Then I’ll bring the folding frame,” Baerun said. “It’ll carry a load or a person.”

“Who carries the frame?” Drelset asked.

“You, if you keep sitting on the carriers.”

Drevik set his paper case on the bench. He explained the porch and the family mark. The company listened courteously until he reached the history of the drawing, at which point Zerla began to examine the stitching on her sleeve. He noticed and shortened it.

“You mend these things?” Ildret asked, pointing to the lamp on the paper.

“Yes.”

“Good. You can help with the shutters if they catch.”

He looked pleased. Aevra had been about to say he was there for the memorial and stopped herself.

Stores were brought out: closed oil vessels, spare wick, leather water bottles, rope, iron dogs, short timbers, dried food, a packet of salt, clean cloth sealed inside waxed wrapping. They were going to carry this weight, eat these hard strips of meat and sleep under these covers. Aevra lifted a timber and found it sound but wet.

“Not these.”

“They’re what we have,” the storekeeper said.

“They’re heavy before we’ve done anything to them.”

“Then take fewer.”

Drelset went to the rack behind him and pulled out a shorter piece with a split at one end. Aevra began to object, but he set it across the bench and showed her where it could be cut. Three usable lengths from something the storekeeper had been keeping for an undefined future job.

“That will do,” she said.

“I know.”

The words came out more sharply than she expected. He was looking at the timber, not at her.

She let him mark the cuts.

Kouven wanted more clean cloth than the storekeeper would issue. Thaereth authorized it after hearing what he intended to use it for. Ildret rejected two lamps and took a third whose glass had been replaced with horn. Drevik asked why. She put both in his hands, one after the other, and told him to feel the difference in weight and in how the shutters closed.

Aevra found she was smiling.

This she knew: people choosing tools, distrusting stores, showing off a little in front of strangers. The expedition would begin on an old road, and they had brought someone who knew the new part of it. Nobody here looked like the subject of a warning song.

At the end of the afternoon, Thaereth took the signed papers back to the office. The bell remained locked in its tray. Aevra and Drelset stayed to cut the timber, while Drevik carried the copied measurements home.

“Three days?” Drelset asked.

“To leave. If the stores are ready.”

“I’ll be ready.”

“Tell whoever needs telling.”

“I have.”

He set the saw and drew it back once, making a shallow line before putting his weight into the cut. Aevra held the timber. Sawdust gathered on the toe of her boot.

“You don’t have to come because I am,” she said.

He stopped.

For a moment she thought he would laugh. Instead he looked directly at her.

“I’m coming because there’s shoring to do.”

“All right.”

“And because I want to see it.”

“So do I.”

He began sawing again. She kept her hand steady until the piece came free.

When the timber was cut, Aevra returned to Thaereth’s office for a copy of the stores allowance. The officer was standing on a stool, removing the upper shelves from the leaning cupboard.

“Stay there,” Aevra said.

“I’m trying to get down.”

“Then give me that first.”

She took the stack of old covers and put it on the floor. Thaereth descended carefully. Behind the cupboard, an iron fastening had drawn almost out of its hole. Aevra pushed the empty upper half back while the officer placed a block under its front feet. They stood looking at it.

“I’ll send someone,” Aevra said.

“I’ve written it down.”

“Send me the paper.”

Thaereth found the allowance on her table. There was a small cup beside it, cold drink with a skin on top. Aevra thought of asking whether she had eaten and decided it would sound as if she were trying to make the woman like her.

“Were they people you knew?” she asked instead.

“The west approach?”

“Yes.”

“One. I knew his wife better.”

“Did you tell her?”

“No. His brother did. I told her why we couldn’t open it.”

Thaereth folded the allowance and pressed the crease with her thumb. She did not offer an account of the meeting. Aevra had enough experience of those conversations to imagine several versions and no right to choose one.

“You think we should go?” she asked.

“I think we should look at the approach while there is one to look at.”

“That isn’t the same thing.”

“It is the thing I can agree to.”

Aevra took the paper. On her way out she turned back.

“I’ll tell my cousin to leave the lamp here if it’s too much to carry.”

“Let him carry it. If he tires, he can choose what to put down.”

Outside the office, Aevra unfolded the allowance. Thaereth had authorized the extra cloth, two replacement straps and a little more oil than the original issue. The additions had been made before their meeting in the stores court.

At home, Maeven had the good pot empty, scrubbed and drying beside the flue. Aevra stood looking at it while her spouse explained that she would need the household stool back from the visiting uncle because a customer was collecting it in the morning.

“It’s sold?”

“It was always sold.”

“I thought it was ours.”

“You think anything you’ve sat on twice is ours.”

Aevra put her coat down. “They’ve allowed it.”

Maeven nodded. She had a hinge in one hand and a file in the other.

“How long?”

“Ten days out from the last station. Less, perhaps.”

“Perhaps.”

“I’ll leave the cooper’s measurements with Drelset’s brother.”

“And the north window?”

“I can do it before we go.”

“You said that last week.”

Aevra took the file from her hand and laid it on the table. “I’ll do it tomorrow.”

Maeven looked at her for a while, then put the hinge down beside it.

“Come and eat first,” she said. “I’ve kept it warm long enough.”

Chapter 5 — Things packed

The lamp would not fit in the case with its bail raised.

Drevik had known this before trying it. He had brought the lamp to Kharveth in the same case, with the same folded cloth, and had spent part of the journey worrying that the hinge would work through the lining. Now he wanted the case to contain a small roll of tools, the family drawing, two changes of stockings and the lamp, and could not persuade any of those things to become smaller.

His father watched from the bed.

“Put the tools underneath.”

“They’ll press on the bottom.”

“Wrap them.”

“They are wrapped.”

“Then there’s no difficulty.”

Drevik removed the tools and put them in his ordinary pack. The lamp settled into the case. Its bail folded down with a small metallic click.

He had emptied the oil into a household vessel, wiped the reservoir and let it stand open overnight. Even so, the cloth smelled faintly of warm oil. He placed a fresh piece around the weak seam and put a thin board beside it to prevent pressure from the case wall. His father reached out to touch the lid before he closed it.

“Don’t polish it on the road.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You’ll get bored waiting somewhere and decide to improve it.”

Drevik smiled. “I’ll try to find something else to do.”

The two days since the commission had filled with little tasks. He had copied the useful portions of the family drawing and given the old sheet back to his father. The original would remain dry at home. Aevra had helped convert the old distances into present measures, marking the uncertain lengths in a different hand. Drevik had bought thread, found that his good walking coat rubbed beneath the pack strap, and asked Maeven to move a buckle.

He had also told his father that there might be nothing to recover.

That conversation had gone badly enough that he did not intend to repeat it before departure.

A woman from the next room came to ask whether the old man wanted broth at midday. He said he did not. She told Drevik she would bring some anyway and asked where he kept the cup with the wide handle. His father listened with theatrical resignation until she mentioned that her daughter would sit with him in the afternoons.

“The little one?”

“She’s taller than you.”

“She was little.”

“She’ll bring her mending. You needn’t entertain her.”

“I can manage to sit in a room.”

When she had gone, he asked Drevik whether there was still cheese. The cheese had been refused at breakfast because he was not hungry. Drevik cut it and put it on the plate without comment.

The final stores check was held in the lamp room below the public gallery. Ildret had arranged the traveling lights on a bench, each with its shutter open. The room smelled of boiled wick and a bitter cleaning paste. A trough along the wall held pieces of horn under weights. Some had dried to a translucent yellow; others were still cloudy and soft.

Drevik liked the room. There were small, comprehensible things to do.

Ildret handed him a lamp whose shutter had begun sticking at one corner. “Show me what you’d do with that.”

He examined the hinge before reaching for a tool. The frame had been distorted by a blow. He braced it on a folded cloth and eased it back a little, tested it, then stopped.

“Again?” she asked.

“It will spring if I go much farther. I’d rather dress the edge.”

She nodded and gave him a fine file.

They worked without speaking for a while. Ildret was a compact woman with a square, almost expressionless face until something amused her. Her hair had been cut close beneath the work scarf. Oil had stained the seams of her fingers dark, and one thumbnail was growing back ridged after an injury. Drevik recognized the care with which she used that hand. He had done the same after dropping a vice screw on his own.

When the shutter moved freely, she filled the lamp halfway and made him carry it across the room.

“By the bail.”

“I am.”

“Let it swing. Don’t try to hold the whole thing still.”

He loosened his grip. The lamp settled beneath his hand. A small projection at its base kept the reservoir away from his leg when he walked.

“Now stoop.”

He did. Oil moved audibly inside.

“Not that far. If you have to crawl, close it and give it to somebody before you go.”

“Does the shutter put it out?”

“No. It shades it. This does.”

She showed him the separate little cap that could be lowered over the wick. He tried it twice. The second time the cap caught, and he had to lift it and begin again.

“It sticks?”

“You’re pushing sideways.”

He corrected his hand. The flame vanished. A narrow thread of smoke emerged through the upper vent.

Ildret relit it and made him do it again until the movement felt ordinary. Then she showed him how the lamp fastened to a carrying hook when both hands were needed. The catch had to be pressed fully home and turned. Drevik pressed it, heard a click and assumed the turn was for release. When he tried to unfasten it later, it came away more easily than he expected.

Ildret had gone to answer a question. He looked at the hook, then at her back, and fitted it again. This time he turned it until he met resistance. He could ask her when there were fewer people in the room.

Baerun arrived carrying the folding frame on his good shoulder. He put it down with a noise that made Ildret look up sharply.

“Mind the lamps.”

“I am minding them.”

“You’re making me mind them.”

He apologized, awkwardly and with no joke after it. Drevik liked him better for that.

The frame was built of short wooden lengths joined by iron plates, with lashings to hold either supplies or a person. Baerun spread it on the floor and demonstrated how the ends could be raised for carrying. When he reached across himself, his left shoulder stopped before the rest of him did.

Drevik watched the movement, then looked away.

Their stores were divided into loads on the floor. He moved a bundle of light cloth from his own pile to Baerun’s and took a packet of metal fittings in its place. The hauler noticed but said nothing until the others had gone to weigh their packs.

“Those will sit badly against your back.”

“I’ve got a board in the pack.”

“Let me see.”

Drevik opened it. Baerun placed the fittings lower and closer to the center, then tightened the inner cord.

“There. You won’t spend the day being hit in the kidneys.”

“Thank you.”

Baerun touched his shoulder and went to argue with Drelset about rope.

By the end of the afternoon, Drevik’s pack contained tools, clothing, his share of food, the medical cloth Kouven had asked him to carry and a small tin of the cleaning ash he had almost left behind. The separate lamp case would ride against the front of the pack, where he could reach it without unloading everything. He stood with the full weight and discovered that the arrangement pulled him backwards.

Aevra changed two straps and had him stand again. Better. Not comfortable.

“You can leave that tin,” she said.

“It weighs nothing.”

“Everything weighs something.”

He took out the tin, considered it, and put it back. She did not argue.

He went back by the upper market, taking the long way because he wanted air and did not want to say so at the lamp room. Near the entrance a human woman had spread repaired coats over a rail. One had a lining of densely woven blue cloth. Drevik stopped to touch it, thinking of his father’s cold knees.

“That one’s sold,” she said.

“I only wondered about the lining.”

“Good, isn’t it? Better than the coat.”

He asked where it came from. She named a town he knew, and they spent several minutes establishing that they had both bought thread from the same ill-tempered seller. Her accent belonged to the mixed road speech of his own street. His answers grew quicker. He did not have to consider whether a word would sound oddly chosen.

She had a remnant of the lining beneath the table. He bought it, though it was dearer than he wanted, and sat on a low wall to fold it around the small packet of things he was taking home.

Kouven found him there.

“You’re going the wrong way.”

“I’m going home.”

“The others thought you’d gone to the lower stores.”

“No.”

The orderly sat beside him without asking. He carried a paper cone of roasted nuts. He offered it. Drevik took two.

For a while they watched the traffic through the outer arch. A mule objected to the paving and had to be led backwards over the place it disliked. The driver and a boy argued about which of them had taught it this habit.

Kouven laughed. “I had one do that with a man on it.”

“An injured man?”

“A man who said he couldn’t walk.”

“Could he?”

“Afterwards.”

Drevik waited for more, but Kouven was attending to a nut caught in his teeth.

“Do you go on many of these?” Drevik asked eventually.

“Surveys? No. I usually have the people brought to me.”

“Then why this one?”

“They asked. My next room turn was with a woman who dislikes the way I fold bandages. I thought a change might improve us both.”

Drevik could not tell how much of that was a joke.

Kouven offered the nuts again. “I’ve worked away before. Not that road.”

“Neither have I.”

“No. You said.”

Had he? Drevik had been so occupied with avoiding the admission that he could not remember making it.

He showed Kouven the cloth he had bought. The man rubbed it between finger and thumb and asked whether it was for padding a tool. Drevik explained about his father. Kouven nodded, saying nothing about how long the old man might live, and asked whether there was enough left over to line a glove.

They walked back together. At the inner turn Drevik glanced once more toward the arch. The sky had darkened during their conversation.

At home that evening, his father had more color in his face than Drevik had seen for several days. The neighbor’s daughter had brought a torn purse to mend. He had objected to her first stitch, whereupon she had given him the purse and asked him to do it. He had managed half an inch before returning it. Now they were both pretending that the crooked beginning belonged to her.

Drevik sat with them while Maeven and Aevra moved something heavy in the next room. There was a crash, a silence and then Maeven laughing. His father began humming under his breath.

It was the descent song, but only the turn between verses. Drevik had heard it while working at the shop, his father repeating the same short phrase until somebody complained. He took up the next line without thinking.

His father stopped.

“Where did you get that ending?”

“From the singer.”

“She always liked that one.”

“Did you know it?”

“I knew better than to sing it in front of your mother.”

The neighbor’s daughter asked what it meant. Neither of them answered.

After she left, Drevik checked the oil in the household’s other lamp. His father watched him from the chair.

“You’ll come back before the next wash day, most likely.”

“It may be longer.”

“Not much.”

“We don’t know.”

The old man’s expression hardened. “You needn’t keep telling me that.”

“I’m not trying to—”

“You are. You’ve been telling me all day what you won’t promise.”

Drevik put down the oil vessel too hard. A little spilled on the shelf.

“I can’t promise the road is open because you want the lamp.”

“I asked you to look.”

“And I’m going.”

His voice had risen. Through the wall he could hear Aevra and Maeven moving their furniture, more carefully now. He took a cloth and wiped the shelf. His father turned his head toward the window.

They said little for the remainder of the evening. Drevik lay awake listening to the old man’s breathing and thought of getting up to apologize. The longer he waited, the more difficult it became to decide what the apology should be for.

In the morning there was no time for the conversation he had prepared in the dark.

Maeven came with food. The neighbor came to see whether anything was needed. Aevra called from the gallery that the others were already at the lift. His father wanted his other slippers and would not accept that they were the same slippers viewed from the wrong side of the bed.

Drevik knelt and put them on him.

“Keep that dry,” the old man said, touching the lamp case.

“I will.”

“The paper too.”

“I’ve wrapped it.”

His father’s fingers rested on the case for another moment. Then he took Drevik’s face between his hands and kissed his forehead, as he had done when Drevik was a child leaving for some small errand that would carry him out of sight.

Drevik held him carefully. He wanted to hold harder.

Outside, Aevra was fastening her coat. Maeven stood beside her with the good pot empty in her hands. They had eaten from it before the others woke. Aevra said something too quietly for Drevik to hear, and Maeven touched the back of her neck before stepping away.

They went up first, to the public entrance where Thaereth was collecting the final permits. Drevik had expected to go down at once. The brief sight of the sky surprised him: a strip of pale morning beyond the stone arch, low cloud caught against the outer roofs. A cart stood on the wet paving. Its driver was eating an apple, spitting the skin into his hand.

Drevik breathed the cold air. The wind carried a smell of horse and leaf mold. He had been complaining about that smell on the road up only three weeks earlier.

Thaereth returned with the permits. They turned toward the inner stair.

Drevik glanced back once. The arch had narrowed to a bright shape behind other people’s shoulders. Then the stair curved, and he could no longer see it.

Chapter 6 — Between the cisterns

Aevra had forgotten how much noise eight people could make before they had gone anywhere.

Baerun’s frame knocked against a stair rail. A loose pan rang on Drelset’s pack. Zerla’s boots squeaked whenever she stepped on the damp center of a tread, and Kouven had a bottle wrapped in something insufficiently thick inside his bag. Drevik was the quietest until he tried to avoid a puddle and struck his case against the wall.

They stopped at the first broad landing and corrected what they could. Drelset tied the pan inside his bed cover. Kouven unwrapped the bottle and padded it with a spare stocking. Drevik looked at the mark on his case as though the wall had chosen him personally.

“It’ll get worse than that,” Baerun said.

“I know.”

“I meant the case.”

“So did I.”

Aevra let them finish. There was no reason to hurry through a staffed road. She had left enough hurry at home, where Maeven would be looking at the north window she had finally repaired the previous afternoon. The new fastening was a little stiff. It would ease with use. She had said so twice, and Maeven had told her she could go without first persuading the entire room to last forever.

The road descended beside the great cisterns.

From the upper gallery the water appeared black, divided by the broad feet of the pillars. As they went down, reflections gathered on its surface: yellow lamp streaks, a red hanging cloth, the slow movement of a white shirt on a bridge far below. The far wall curved out of sight behind a screen of galleries and pipework. Water fell somewhere within it, a continuous deep rushing that made the nearby voices seem unusually clear.

Aevra had crossed this chamber hundreds of times, usually by the direct bridge with her head full of the job awaiting her. From the long stair she could see the undersides of bridges she had never thought to look beneath. One carried a thin white seam across the dark, a repaired joint catching the light from the water. Another had an old carving on its inner support, half hidden by a later pipe. The carved face looked down toward a market stall where a woman was laying out onions.

Drevik stopped at the rail.

Aevra almost told him to keep moving. Then she put her pack against the wall and let the others pass them to the next landing.

“It’s bigger from here,” he said.

“Yes.”

“Do people live all the way round?”

“Where you see windows. The far side’s stores above the water.”

A little boat emerged between two pillars, a worker standing in its middle with a pole. Its wake broke the reflected shirt into scraps. Drevik watched until the boat had disappeared again.

“My father would like this.”

“We can bring him to the upper rail.”

“He’ll say he can’t see from there.”

“He’ll say that from anywhere.”

Drevik smiled, but did not move at once. Aevra waited beside him. The stone rail was cool beneath her palm, worn shallow where generations of hands had rested at the turn.

Below, the market was opening.

They threaded through stalls placed between the cistern pillars. There was bread, lamp oil, worked leather, a table of bright metal scraps sorted by size, a woman selling greens grown near an upper shaft. The smell changed with each few steps. Aevra bought dried plums from a man who tried to charge her for the weight of the cloth. He did this every time and expressed the same surprise when caught.

Zerla was already eating a hot cake. Thaereth told her to finish it before they reached the narrow section. Ildret stopped at a lamp stall to compare a new wick with the ones she carried. Drevik began talking to the seller about the weave and had to be fetched when the rest moved on.

“They’ll still be selling it when we come back,” Aevra said.

“He says it burns more evenly.”

“They always do.”

“I’d like to try a length.”

“Then buy a length.”

He did, pleased by the permission she had not meant to give.

At the lower arch, a warden checked Thaereth’s paper and counted the lamps. He knew Ildret and asked whether her sister had settled the quarrel over the shared room. Ildret said her sister had settled it by taking the door off. The warden looked impressed.

“What are you carrying?” he asked Baerun.

“Everything they don’t want.”

“The frame?”

“Stores. Or somebody who falls over.”

“Keep it folded past the bend. There’s a cart stuck.”

The inspection took less time than the conversation. Thaereth’s permit covered the next two vaultmouths and the stores station beyond. The warden returned it, unfastened a chain and sent a boy ahead to warn the cart crew.

Aevra recognized the lock by the old maker’s cut on its plate. The key turned reluctantly, then cleanly. They passed through into the sound of people arguing.

The cart had lost a wheel pin beside a drain. Its load of tile had shifted just enough to press one corner against the wall. The driver stood with both hands on his hips while three people offered incompatible advice. Water was running across the floor from a blocked branch channel, reaching the cart’s lower wheel.

Drelset set down his pack.

“We’re not here to repair every cart,” Thaereth said.

“We’re not getting past it either.”

Baerun had already gone to look at the axle. Aevra moved to the channel and found a clump of old sacking caught behind the grate. She asked the driver whose it was. He said he did not know. The wet edge matched the cover hanging from the back of his cart.

She pulled it free with an iron hook. The water sank, taking a string of black leaves with it.

Drelset and Baerun lifted the load just enough for the driver to replace the pin with a spare from his tool box. He had owned the spare all along. What he had lacked was somebody willing to stand in dirty water while he fitted it.

Drevik held the lamp. When a splash reached his cuff, he flinched but did not lower the light.

They were past within a quarter of an hour. Aevra’s right boot had filled with water. She emptied it at the next dry landing while Zerla shared the remainder of her cake with Ildret. The air was warm enough that the wet stocking was merely unpleasant.

“Will the waterworkers come?” Drevik asked.

“For what?”

“The flood.”

“It was a sack,” Aevra said.

“The channel might need clearing farther up.”

“The driver will tell them.”

Baerun made a noise.

“All right,” she said. “We’ll tell them at the station.”

They continued down the road. The houses thinned. Public lamps remained at regular turns, their flames protected behind small panes of horn. Beyond one arch, the wall had been cut with household marks where people had once lived. The doorways were stores now, filled with rope and spare supports. An old lintel had a child’s scratched drawing beneath it. Aevra remembered adding to something similar with a nail when she was small.

At a steep turn she handed her short hammer to Drelset.

“Tap those before we bring the frame down.”

He did not take it.

“Drelset?”

“I’ve got a hammer.”

“Then use yours.”

“I’m looking at the side wall. Baerun can tap a stair.”

She turned toward the wall. A thin gap showed beneath a facing block. The mark above it was older than the public lamp bracket, but a new flake had fallen onto the stair.

“Do you think it’s moved?”

“I’m trying to see.”

She put her hammer away. Baerun tested the treads while Drelset examined the facing. It proved a shallow piece, loosened by a rusting fixture behind it. He marked it for the road crew and they passed one at a time.

At the bottom Aevra caught his sleeve.

“I shouldn’t have handed you mine.”

He looked tired rather than angry. “You always do it.”

“I know.”

“You don’t, usually.”

“No.”

He shifted his pack. “The mark wants another line. They’ll think I mean the whole face.”

She went back and added it while he waited. When she returned, he had taken the frame from Baerun for the next stretch.

They ate at a counter beside the final public cistern. The woman serving them had known Aevra’s mother and asked for news of everyone except Aevra. She ladled beans into bowls and gave Drevik more when he admitted he was hungry. Drelset loosened his belt beneath the table. Thaereth ate quickly, then sat with her hands around her cup while the others finished.

A man at the neighboring table asked where they were going. Baerun told him. The man laughed until nobody joined in.

“Outhren?”

“The approach,” Thaereth said.

“There’s nothing there.”

“Then we’ll have an easy report.”

“My grandfather used to threaten to send us there if we wouldn’t sleep.”

“Did it work?” Zerla asked.

“No.”

“Perhaps he should have sent you.”

The man laughed again and went back to his meal. Aevra found herself relieved that the name could still be laughed at in a room smelling of cooked beans.

The service road beyond the cistern was broad enough for two people abreast. Workers passed them going the other way, carrying empty baskets and wrapped tools. Aevra knew several by sight. One asked her to look at a cracked threshold when she returned. She said she would, and realized she had already begun putting things after the journey.

They reached the staffed station before the evening bell.

Its keeper had eight sleeping places ready in a room with a low stove and a rack for wet clothing. He complained that Thaereth’s message had given no arrival hour, then brought hot water without being asked. Each bed had a folded blanket and a wooden peg above it. Drevik inspected the pegs before choosing one, as if their relative positions might affect the night.

Aevra hung her wet stocking near the stove. The keeper took their names and asked when they intended to return.

“By the tenth day from tomorrow,” Thaereth said. “Earlier if the road’s closed.”

“And if you send word?”

“We’ll name what we need.”

He nodded and made the note.

The station keeper’s daughter came into the sleeping room with a basket of stockings. She placed it beside the stove and began pairing them while the company ate. Some belonged to the staff; some had been left by workers who would claim them on another journey. She recognized Baerun and gave him a gray pair without waiting for him to ask.

“I wondered where those had gone,” he said.

“You left them wet in the wrong room.”

“That sounds like me.”

She sat with Ildret and complained about a man who kept paying for washing with promises of a better drying rack. Aevra had met him. He had once tried to sell her a lifting attachment with a joint she could bend in her hands. They compared their experiences with increasing satisfaction until Thaereth pointed out that they were describing two different men.

This made the keeper’s daughter laugh so hard that she dropped a stocking into her lap and had to begin pairing the pile again.

Drevik watched them for a while, then asked about a darn along the toe of the gray pair. He had seen the stitch used on fine lining and wondered how it would hold under a foot. The daughter turned it inside out for him. Soon they were both leaning over the work, Ildret supplying a needle so he could show a variation.

Aevra let the conversation go past her. Her shoulders had begun to ache where the pack sat, and she was enjoying the ache now that she could put it down. She had expected to spend the evening planning the next stage. Thaereth and Zerla were doing that at the other end of the table, arguing gently over a turn Zerla knew by a stain rather than a name.

Drelset had taken off his boots. One stocking had worn through at the heel. The keeper’s daughter offered to mend it for a little money. He said he could mend it himself.

“Yes,” she said. “But will you?”

He considered the hole, then handed it over.

The room grew warmer. Steam rose from the clothes near the stove. For a time the only sounds were the needle through wool, a spoon against a bowl, and Baerun telling Kouven about someone neither of them liked. Aevra closed her eyes. When she opened them again, Drevik was holding the yarn while the young woman wound it, and Drelset had gone to fetch more hot water.

Later, after supper, Aevra went to the little exterior landing above the station’s water channel. No houses were visible from there. The road behind them glowed at its turns, a chain of small maintained lights receding toward Kharveth. A man came out to scrape a cooking pot, greeted her and went back inside. She could hear Drevik laughing at something through the open door.

She stayed until the warmth left her face, then went in and claimed the bed nearest her drying stocking.

Chapter 7 — Orrvek’s porch

On the second day the road became a place where things had been left.

A pair of broken carriers leaned against a wall. Beyond them stood a wooden box without a lid, full of bolts too rusted to use. Someone had put a boot on top of it. Drevik looked for the other boot and was annoyed with himself when Aevra asked him to keep up.

The public lamps ended at a narrow iron gate. Its lower half had been repaired in a different pattern from the upper. Thaereth opened it with a key from the station and gave the key to Zerla to carry. The girl put it inside her coat on a separate cord.

“Why separate?” Drevik asked.

“So I can find it.”

The explanation was so simple that he regretted asking.

For several hours the route followed a dry watercourse. Their lamps lit the walls to shoulder height, leaving the roof in darkness except where it bent low enough to catch a moving shine. Pale mineral deposits had spread over old chisel marks. Here and there the stone looked soft, like cloth laid over something beneath it, but where Drevik touched it the folds were hard and cold.

He found that he preferred walking immediately behind Ildret. She held her lamp steadily without seeming to attend to it. His own light shifted whenever he looked at anything, making the surface he wanted to see escape into shadow. After a while she told him to use hers and save his oil.

“Am I carrying it badly?”

“You’re carrying it where your eyes go. That’s all.”

He lowered the cap over the wick. It caught once before closing. She waited until he had put the lamp away.

They ate at a wide spot where the watercourse divided. Baerun unfolded a corner of the carrying frame to make a dry place for the food. The meal was hard bread, cheese and sliced root pickled in something sharp enough to make Drevik’s eyes water. He took another piece. The sound of their chewing seemed impolite in the quiet.

Zerla had been this far before. She pointed out a high mark on the wall where water had stood during the last change at the catch. It was well above their heads.

“Was the road closed then?” Drevik asked.

“I should hope so,” Baerun said.

Zerla wiped her fingers on a cloth. “We went round by the upper cut.”

“Is that open now?”

“Not for loads. There’s a narrow squeeze.”

Drevik looked at the frame and his own pack, then at the water mark again. Nobody else seemed interested in it. He finished his bread.

They slept in an old work shelter, the door gone but the wall hooks still usable. Aevra and Drelset examined the roof before they settled. Thaereth put one small lamp near the entrance and told them there was no need for the others. Darkness gathered immediately outside its circle. Drevik could see the soles of Baerun’s boots and, beyond them, nothing.

He woke several times. Once to Kouven coughing. Once to a scrape that proved to be someone turning over. The last time there was no sound he could identify. He lay listening until his ears seemed to supply their own faint rushing.

In the morning he felt foolish for having slept badly when nothing had happened.

The third day’s road climbed out of the dry course. They passed beneath an arch whose carving had been covered with lime wash so long ago that the wash itself had become a pale crust. Drevik could make out a hand and the corner of a bowl. Aevra stopped to examine a crack above it. He was grateful for the excuse to set down his pack.

Beyond the arch the ground rose steeply. The porch ought to have been near, according to the copied distances. Drevik took out the paper, trying to keep it clear of the damp wall. The last instruction before the crypt referred to a turn beneath a standing figure.

There was no figure.

Aevra held her lamp higher. Rough stone filled the place where the drawing showed a side passage.

“It could have fallen,” Drelset said.

Drevik studied the line again. “Or been filled.”

“Same result for us.”

“No. If it was filled, the opening may still be there.”
