Aevra passed him the lamp and went to the wall. She scraped a narrow patch with the point of her tool. The surface beneath was shaped stone, set between older shoulders.

Drevik felt a small, fierce pleasure.

They could not remove the filling without work and permission they did not have. Zerla found a worn track climbing behind a fallen slab to the left. It led to a low gap where a person without a pack could squeeze through. Drelset went first, lying on his side and pushing his lamp ahead. His voice came back muffled but ordinary.

“There’s room.”

“Roof?” Aevra asked.

“A lintel. Good one.”

They passed the packs through one by one. Baerun and Aevra stayed outside until the frame had been folded as small as it would go. Drevik followed Ildret, holding his case against his chest. The stone pressed his shoulder and the side of his head. For a moment his coat caught, and he had to stop himself pulling harder.

“Back a little,” Ildret said from the other side.

He did. The caught cloth loosened. He emerged with dust in his mouth and found himself looking at a carved face no higher than his own knee.

It had been cut into the side of the low lintel. The head was bent, just as the singer had said, but the reason was visible now: the figure held up a small basket and was peering into it. The old drawing had turned the basket into a bowl and made the figure solemn. Here the corners of its mouth were lifted. One cheek had a shallow chip in it.

Drevik laughed once, unexpectedly.

“What?” Ildret asked.

“Nothing. He’s looking for something.”

She looked down. “So he is.”

The porch opened beyond the lintel, a rectangular space supported by two short pillars. Their lamps spread across a dry floor. The right-hand wall contained the basin from the verse, now almost filled with mineral crust. A narrow channel ran from its lip to a drain beneath the outer step. Above the basin, the same divided square appeared in the stone.

Drevik touched it.

He had expected an emotion he could name in advance. Instead he was occupied by the depth of the cut, the roughness on one edge, the way the extended stroke ended in a tool slip too small to have survived in the drawing. He could put his fingertip into the place where somebody’s hand had moved farther than intended.

The others came through behind him. Baerun swore at the squeeze. Thaereth asked everyone to stand clear while Aevra checked the porch. Drevik moved obediently, though he disliked hearing his ancestor’s burial described in the same tone as a store chamber.

A door stood in the inner wall. It was stone-faced, hung on iron pivots, its lower edge clear of the floor by the width of a thumb. No royal seal remained. There were old fixing holes where a plate might once have been attached.

“Don’t force it,” Drevik said.

Aevra glanced at him. “I wasn’t going to.”

He apologized. She tested the pivot sockets, then the lintel above. Drelset held the lamp while she examined the side joints. The examination took a long time. Drevik waited with both hands on his case, looking at the narrow line around the door.

When she finally pushed it, it moved.

Only a little at first. Grit scraped beneath the lower edge. Baerun put his shoulder to the outer face and helped. The opening widened enough for light to enter.

A burial room lay beyond, smaller than Drevik had imagined. The sarcophagus occupied most of it. Its lid carried no life-sized king or warrior, only a shallow relief of two hands folded over a tool. The tool’s head had been worn almost smooth. Along the lower side ran the name he knew, with several words of work and kinship in a form he had to read slowly.

Orrvek.

Drevik went down on one knee. Nobody had told him to. The floor was cold through his trousers.

He read the name again and found a tiny correction in the final letter. The mason had cut one stroke too long and disguised it in a border. Drevik had made the same sort of correction on memorial plates. He felt, abruptly, an unreasonable affection for the unknown worker.

The lamp niche was empty.

It stood at the head of the burial, exactly where the drawing placed it. A hook remained above it, and a pale oval marked the stone shelf. Drevik rose and held his light nearer. The mark was clear. The shape of the reservoir had protected it from whatever had darkened the surrounding surface.

“Gone?” his father would ask.

He could already hear the disappointment becoming accusation, though his father had accused him of no such thing yet.

“Is that where it was?” Thaereth asked.

“Yes.”

She did not offer consolation. He was grateful and angry about that too.

Aevra was examining the back wall. A thin water track descended from an upper joint, stopped at a fitted strip and ran sideways into a small channel. The strip had been installed after the rest of the room. Its fixing pegs were unlike the old iron sockets at the door.

“Come and look,” she said.

Drevik went reluctantly.

The channel led into the porch drain. Somebody had cut it through the old basin’s crust and kept the cut open. Near the floor, a small accumulation of swept grit lay in the corner behind the door.

“How long ago?” Drevik asked.

“I can’t say. These edges haven’t had long to gather anything.”

“Years?”

“Perhaps. Less, perhaps.”

He went back to the niche. Beneath it, the floor showed no fragments of bronze, no sign that the lamp had broken where it stood.

“They’ve taken it.”

Kouven, standing in the doorway, looked at him. “Who?”

“I don’t know.”

“Then we’ll have to ask.”

“If we find them.”

“Yes.”

Drevik ran his hand over the empty shelf. “It belonged here.”

Kouven shifted the medical bag on his shoulder. “If people have been living down here, they’ll have wanted lamps.”

“It was beside a burial.”

“I can see.”

The man’s tone was gentle enough to be intolerable. Drevik left the room before he said something worse.

Outside, Baerun had begun laying out the bed covers in the dry half of the porch. Ildret arranged the lamps so that one would illuminate the low entrance without dazzling anyone looking toward it. Zerla found a clean trickle feeding the basin from a side channel, and Thaereth told her to test it before filling the bottles.

Drevik sat on the outer step. Beyond the porch the passage descended through fallen stone into a dark he could not judge. Faint moving air touched his damp collar. There was another approach somewhere below; the crypt had not been reached only by the squeeze through which they had entered.

He wanted to go and look immediately.

Instead he opened his case and took out the replica. Its shape fitted the pale mark in the niche when he held it in front of it. Not exactly. The copied lamp was a little taller, the wick mouths set at a different angle. He put it back in the case without placing it on the shelf.

They ate in the porch that evening. Nobody sang. Drelset told a long and rather bad account of a load he had once lowered into the wrong courtyard. Baerun interrupted with details proving he had been present, which improved the account considerably. Drevik laughed despite himself when they reached the part involving a woman in a bath.

Afterwards he copied the burial inscription. Aevra held the light. She did not hurry him, though her arm must have tired.

When he had finished, she asked, “Are you glad you came?”

He could not answer at once.

“Yes,” he said finally. “I am.”

She lowered the lamp and flexed her fingers. They closed the burial door gently, leaving its pivots as they had found them, and went out to the sleeping company.

A man sat on the lowest porch step, fastening his boot.

Drevik stopped. The man wore a short coat with a broad pale patch at one shoulder. His head was bent, his hair caught back with a pin. He pulled at the fastening, tested it with his thumb, and gave the toe a small knock against the step.

“Who’s there?” Drevik said.

The man rose. He was shorter than Drevik, broad through the chest, and the lamplight showed the joints of the wall through his coat. He stepped down into the fallen stone and was gone.

Aevra held the lamp still. Nothing moved below them.

“Was that—”

“I don’t know.”

They waited. Water ticked into the basin. Presently the man sat on the step again, pulling at the same fastening. His thumb tested it; the toe struck stone without a sound.

Drevik watched him leave a second time. He could not make out the pin’s shape, though he knew he would try to draw it in the morning.

Part II — The road that ended

Chapter 8 — The gate that opened

The lower approach to the porch had been cleared by hands.

Aevra could see where loose stones had been laid along its outer edge. Smaller fragments had been packed behind larger ones to keep them from moving underfoot. The work was rough and competent. It continued down to a landing where a narrow stair met the old road.

Drevik crouched beside one of the packed gaps.

“Would they have come this way?”

“Somebody did.”

“With the lamp?”

“Perhaps.”

He stood. She wished she had a different answer, and was glad he had stopped asking whether the work could be dated precisely. There were things a mason could tell from a cut edge. The name and intention of the person holding the tool were not among them.

They left the crypt after a morning spent measuring both approaches. Thaereth made a sketch of the porch and noted the later drainage work. Drevik added the exact burial name beneath it, correcting one letter that the officer had copied in its modern form. She accepted the correction without fuss.

The road below was wide enough for the frame. Its central paving remained level, though rubble had accumulated against the walls. At two turns the clearing continued. At a third it stopped abruptly, as if those using it had gone elsewhere. Aevra found no sign of a side opening, but the old stonework had been covered by a fall she did not intend to dismantle.

They followed the route given in the drawing.

The gate stood beneath an arch carved with the three bars of Outhren. Its metal face was dark, wet in places, with an old mechanical housing projecting from the left wall. Aevra recognized the general arrangement: a counterweight gate with a service opening that could be released separately. The service opening was narrow, intended for a person carrying a lamp and tools, not a loaded cart.

Thaereth examined the lock plate. “Same family as the upper catch.”

“Older.”

“The issued key may fit.”

It did not. The stem entered, but the wards stopped it turning.

Drevik looked at the plate. “May I?”

He knelt with his tool roll and measured the visible opening with a thin strip. After a moment he shook his head.

“It’s the lower form. The key needs the outer tooth removed.”

“We’re not altering the key,” Thaereth said.

“No. I can make a strip for the service tongue if Aevra says that cover can come off.”

Aevra checked the housing. Four fastenings held the cover; two were too corroded to move, but the lower pair could be loosened enough to turn it aside. Inside, a release bar lay within reach. Drevik could operate it without damaging the main lock.

They worked together. Ildret shaded the lamp so the reflection would not blind them inside the housing. Drelset watched the lintel and the counterweight channel. When the tongue finally released, the small door moved inward with a deep rubbing sound.

Air touched Aevra’s face.

“Wait,” she said.

Ildret held a test flame low in the opening, then higher. It burned steadily. There was air moving, faintly, from the chamber beyond.

Aevra put her head through.

A receiving room opened on the other side. She knew the type from old public works: a bench along one wall, a water recess opposite, two further doors with a screened opening between them. At the far end stood a broad stone plinth where a warden’s table would usually have been placed. The roof rose in three shallow arches, each separated by a rib.

The chamber was empty.

That was no surprise. The road was closed. Yet the familiar arrangement made her expect a keeper somewhere beyond the screen, a person delayed by their arrival or gone to fetch something before dealing with them.

They passed the stores through the small door. Baerun complained of the awkward turn, and Aevra told him he could complain from the other side once the frame was clear. The opening had to remain propped because the service leaf tended to swing shut under its own weight. Drelset placed a short timber where it could not be displaced by a passing boot.

Inside, their voices sounded comfortably contained after the open road. Drevik put his case on the bench. Zerla went to look at the screen but stopped when Thaereth told her to stay within the light.

Aevra examined the plinth. There were fixing holes where furniture had once stood and a black stain beneath one corner. A bell cord hung from a small hole in the ceiling. Its lower end had rotted away.

“Someone used to sit here,” Drevik said.

“Several someones, over time.”

“Do you think the bell we found—”

“Too small for this chamber,” Ildret said.

She had set a lamp on the floor while checking the wall brackets. Aevra heard her scrape one bracket with a tool, then stop.

The further doors were closed. Aevra tried the nearest. It moved slightly against something on the other side, but would not open. The second had no visible handle from this face. Both were older than the service door through which they had entered.

Thaereth consulted the plan. “The upper and lower gates. This should be the room between.”

“Then the lower one’s that side.”

“Should be.”

Drelset was tracing a narrow channel along the base of the wall. Baerun sat on the bench and stretched one leg. They had been walking for several hours. The chamber was dry, the bench convenient, and there seemed no reason not to rest while the way ahead was examined.

Aevra took out her water bottle.

Drevik was rubbing a hand over the back of his neck. “It’s warm.”

“Old rooms hold it,” Baerun said.

There was warmth, and a flat smell beneath the old damp. Aevra had encountered worse in crowded storerooms. She drank, then went to the screened opening.

Beyond the screen lay a narrow passage with a shaft rising above it. The shaft should have drawn air through the room. She could feel almost nothing at its foot.

“Ildret.”

“I’m looking.”

The lamp worker had lifted the floor lamp. Its flame had shortened. She lowered it again and watched.

“Out,” she said.

Baerun looked up.

“Take the lamps. Out now.”

Aevra did not ask her to explain. “Leave the frame until we’re clear.”

They moved toward the service door. Drevik stood too quickly, caught the corner of his case against the bench and almost dropped it. Kouven steadied him. Thaereth went through first, then turned to take a pack.

The little opening that had seemed adequate on entry became miserably narrow. Everyone had something in a hand. Aevra made them pass things separately. Ildret extinguished two lamps and carried the third high.

By the time Aevra reached the outer side, her head hurt.

She had not noticed it beginning. Now it was a dull pressure behind her eyes, worsened by the bend through the door. She sat on the road beside Drevik and waited while Drelset and Baerun brought the frame through without stopping inside.

No one had been overcome. No one had had to be dragged. That did not make her less angry.

“How long?” she asked Ildret.

“Before what?”

“Before we’d have known without the lamps.”

“You were rubbing your head.”

“I thought—”

“I know.”

The lamp worker knelt and checked the remaining flame in the moving air outside. It recovered to its ordinary shape.

Drevik was pale. “The first test was good.”

“The door had just opened,” Ildret said. “And we were outside it.”

“But air was coming out.”

“Some air.”

Aevra got up too soon and had to put a hand on the wall. She waited until the light steadied.

The service door stood propped. Beyond it lay the bench, the plinth, the waiting-room arrangement she had recognized so readily. She could almost furnish it from memory with people from another place: the warden who would complain about their arrival, the boy sent for water, somebody arguing over the next gate.

Drelset touched her elbow. “Sit down.”

“I want to see the shaft.”

“After.”

She sat.

They rested farther up the road, where a bend carried a perceptible draught. Kouven made them drink and asked questions that seemed foolish until Aevra found herself slow to answer one. He asked Drevik to count the people present. Drevik looked offended, then did it.

Eight.

Baerun wanted food. Thaereth told him to wait until his breathing settled, and he ate a small piece when she was looking elsewhere. Nobody rebuked him.

After a while Aevra and Drelset returned to examine the outside of the gate housing. They stayed out of the chamber. Above the arch a service shaft could be reached by a short ladder of iron pegs, several missing. Aevra climbed only high enough to see the old air shutter.

It was closed.

A linkage ran from it into the masonry. Where that linkage emerged lower down, a short length had been removed. Opening the gate no longer opened the air path. The mechanism had been separated deliberately at some point, or repaired without restoring all of it. She could not tell from here.

She came down and explained.

“So we open that,” Baerun said.

“We don’t know where it draws from.”

“Up.”

“Into what?”

He looked at the shaft and did not answer.

Thaereth marked the chamber on her plan. Ildret hung a warning cloth across the service opening and added a clear mark beside the latch where the next person would see it before entering. Drelset removed the prop and let the little door close.

Before they resumed work, Thaereth asked Aevra to walk back with her to the last bend. There was enough moving air there to make the wick lean.

“Do you want to return?” the officer asked.

Aevra looked at her sharply.

“I’m asking.”

“The chamber can be passed. There’s a service route somewhere.”

“I know what you think about the road. I’m asking whether you want to turn back.”

Aevra rubbed the bridge of her nose. The headache had almost gone. She could see Drevik sitting beside his case, looking determined not to look ill, and Drelset inspecting his lamp as though the fault might be found in its metalwork.

“No,” she said.

“Then we make the next examination without everyone coming in after us.”

“Yes.”

“And if we don’t find a way by the morning, we go back to the porch and report what we found.”

Aevra nodded. She wanted to say that she had never intended to march them all into bad air. Thaereth already knew it. Saying it would do nothing except oblige the woman to reassure her.

They returned to the others. Ildret had the lamps open for cleaning and made Aevra sit beside her with a cloth.

“You don’t need me for this.”

“I’ve got eight of them.”

So Aevra cleaned. The oily soot came off in soft black streaks. She could smell the wick smoke in her own sleeve now that she had time to notice it. Drevik asked Ildret how a flame could burn at the door and fail in the same room a little later. Ildret drew the room in the soot beside her and showed him where the air had entered. Aevra listened while wiping the same corner twice.

Kouven told Baerun to stop asking if everyone felt better. “They’ll tell you yes to make you stop.”

“I’m being considerate.”

“Try doing it quietly.”

Baerun subsided, offended for perhaps half a minute, and then offered Aevra a piece of cheese. She accepted. It tasted of the cloth in which it had been wrapped.

They spent the rest of the afternoon examining the outer wall. A narrow maintenance passage left the road behind a slab fallen from the facing. It did not appear on the family drawing. Aevra could feel cool air moving through it.

Zerla lay flat and shone a lamp along the floor. “It widens.”

“How far?”

“Farther than I can see.”

They opened enough space to pass without packs. Aevra and Ildret examined the first stretch together, returning before the others had finished eating. The passage rose along the outside of the receiving room and emerged above the blocked lower door. From there, a steep stair led down to the road beyond.

“The side stair,” Drevik said. “It might be the one in the margin.”

“Show me.”

He brought out the copy. A thin line she had taken for a damaged edge of the old drawing might indeed have been a stair. Drevik had marked it with a question.

They camped outside the bad chamber rather than move the stores at the end of the day. Aevra slept lightly. Once she woke convinced she had left the gate open, although she had watched Drelset close it. She got up and checked.

The warning cloth hung motionless beside the latch. She touched the wood of the door with her palm, then returned to the others and lay down without waking anyone.

Chapter 9 — The allotted hour

They had to take the frame apart to carry it through the side passage.

Baerun laid its pins on a cloth and made Drevik repeat where each belonged. Drevik knew perfectly well how a folding joint worked, but the hauler looked so worried about the pins that he repeated it without objection. The long pieces passed first. Then the stores, pushed ahead or handed back where the passage bent. The lamp case came last among Drevik’s things, and he crawled after it with his elbows close to his ribs.

His boots scraped behind him. He could hear another person breathing somewhere ahead, the sound traveling through the stone close to his ear. It was uncomfortable but manageable. He kept his attention on the worn corner of the case, moving it forward one handspan at a time.

At the widening above the stair, he was able to kneel.

Aevra stood farther down, holding a light away from the wall. Behind her, a closed door showed where the receiving chamber would have opened onto the lower road. Its upper hinge had been buried by a fall. The steps they were using passed outside it and descended into a broad paved corridor.

“Don’t stand yet,” she said. “Low bit above you.”

Drevik remained on his knees until he could see the edge of the stone. He was grateful she had spoken before he hit it, and still wished she had spoken less loudly.

They reassembled the frame in the corridor. It took longer than taking it apart because one joint had swelled in the damp. Drelset eased it with the back of his knife rather than force it. Baerun checked all the pins again.

The road sloped toward the sound of water.

Drevik had become used to sounds he could locate. A lamp shutter clicked beside him. A boot struck the paving behind. This sound had no clear direction beyond down. It filled the passage in a low, continuous movement that he felt in the soles of his feet before he could decide how loud it was.

Zerla stopped at a branch.

“The old control approach should be left.”

“Should?” Thaereth asked.

“The upper plan puts the feeder this side.”

Aevra examined the floor. The left branch had carried heavier traffic, its center worn into a shallow hollow. The right rose by three broad steps to a smaller opening. They marked the junction and went left.

For a time the corridor remained easy. Here were lamp niches, empty but familiar. Here a bench had been cut into the wall beside a drain. Drevik found a broken handle in the corner, green with corrosion, and bent to look at it. Aevra waited for him, impatiently enough that he left it where it lay.

Then the road narrowed beside an opening in the floor.

It was an old service shaft, covered once by slabs. Two slabs had fallen, leaving a dark space against the inner wall. The outer side remained broad enough for a person, and Baerun began passing the frame along it while Drelset watched his footing.

A corner of a small stores bundle caught on a projecting edge. The fastening tore. The bundle slid, struck the paving and dropped through the missing slabs before anyone could put a hand on it.

There was a short fall, then a dull impact.

Baerun stood very still.

“Which one?” Thaereth asked.

“Small fittings. Some spare cloth.”

Kouven looked at Drevik. Drevik touched the medical wrapping in his own pack and nodded. The clean cloth was safe. The dropped bundle held iron pegs, wire and spare leather lashings.

They could see it when Ildret lowered a lamp on a cord. It had caught on a narrow shelf below, alongside several objects half buried in pale grit. Beyond the shelf the shaft continued into darkness. Water moved somewhere much farther down.

Drevik saw a bronze glimmer beside the bundle.

“Hold the lamp there.”

Ildret steadied the cord.

A little plate lay on the shelf, one corner clear. It bore a hooked line within a divided square.

Drevik’s chest tightened.

“That’s the same mark.”

Aevra crouched beside him. “It may be.”

“It is.”

The plate might have belonged to a door fitting or a household box. He could not see enough to tell. It lay so close to their dropped bundle that recovering one should recover the other. He had the absurd sense that something had been placed within reach after the empty niche.

Baerun tested a long hook against the bundle’s strap. It was just too short. They could lengthen it with a timber and a lashing, but the angle was awkward and there was a risk of pushing the load off the shelf.

“We can lower someone,” Drevik said.

“No,” Aevra said.

“It’s not far.”

“The edge won’t take a line across it until we know what’s holding that slab.”

She pointed to the remaining cover. A crack ran behind it, hidden by dust until her light touched it obliquely.

Drevik moved back.

They took the stores off the frame and laid the long pieces across sound paving to distribute weight. Drelset and Baerun lashed together a reaching pole. Aevra examined the slab and knocked loose a small fragment rather than leave it for someone’s boot to find. The fragment fell past the shelf. A long time seemed to pass before Drevik heard it strike.

Thaereth set a sandglass on a level stone.

“One turn. Then we leave it.”

Drevik looked at her. “Why one?”

“Because we have a road to examine and this is an exposed shaft. We aren’t spending the day above it.”

He almost said that the plate might tell them where to go. He knew how that would sound. Instead he took the spare lamp and held it where Drelset asked.

The first attempt caught the strap but turned the bundle the wrong way. The hook slipped out. The second drew it an inch toward the edge of the shelf. Baerun swore softly and told Drelset to stop pulling until he had the angle.

Dust sifted onto the reaching pole. They paused until it stopped. The light swayed whenever Ildret’s hand tired. Drevik watched the sand thinning in the upper bulb and felt it was falling faster than it ought to.

The hook caught again. This time Baerun raised the bundle until it hung clear of the shelf. Drelset guided the pole’s outer end. The bundle struck the underside of the broken slab, twisted, and came up into Aevra’s reaching hand.

She pulled it onto the paving.

Drevik leaned forward. “Now the plate.”

Thaereth looked at the glass. “No.”

“There’s still some left.”

“Enough to get this apart and move.”

“You said one turn.”

“To finish. We have.”

The bronze mark gleamed below them.

“It could be from the household.”

“It could,” Thaereth said.

“And we’re leaving it?”

“Yes.”

He hated her calm. It seemed practiced in a way his objection could not be. He looked to Aevra, but she was checking the recovered bundle, making sure the torn cover had not spilled anything important.

“We could at least—”

“No,” she said, without looking up.

Baerun began untying the pole. “If it was a man beyond what we could reach, we’d still have to stop.”

Drevik stared at him.

The hauler’s hands kept working. He had dirt beneath every nail and a tremor in the left wrist after holding the pole so long. He seemed unaware that he had said anything requiring an answer.

“That’s an easy thing to say when it’s a piece of metal.”

Baerun looked up then. “It isn’t.”

Nobody spoke for a few breaths. Drelset gathered the loosened cord. Ildret raised the lamp from the shaft. With the light gone, the plate disappeared.

Drevik helped reload the frame. He did it carefully because anything less would have looked like sulking, and he was angry that he had begun judging his own hands from their point of view.

They moved on.

At the next resting place, Kouven unwrapped the dropped bundle and found a puncture through one of the leather pieces. He cut the damaged edge away. Baerun gave Zerla his dry outer cloth because she had knelt in seepage while helping at the shaft. She protested that he would need it later. He folded it around her shoulders and went to check the frame’s damaged fastening.

Drevik sat apart, eating without appetite.

Ildret came to him with the family copy. He had left it beside the shaft.

“Thank you.”

She waited until he had put it away. “You were useful with the light.”

He could not tell whether she meant to comfort him. “It was just holding it.”

“Yes. Where we needed it.”

She went back to the others before he could decide what to say.

At a shallow drain farther on, they stopped to refill two bottles from a tested seep. The water came slowly enough that each bottle had to stand beneath it for several minutes. Drevik volunteered to wait with them while the others ate. He wanted a job he could finish.

Zerla sat nearby, repairing the tie on her sleeve. She had lost the bright thread from her braid somewhere at the shaft. He had noticed its absence but did not know whether she had.

“You could draw the plate from here,” she said.

“I will.”

“I mean, you saw enough of it.”

“I know.”

She pulled the tie with her teeth, made a face at the taste and spat delicately into the drain. Drevik watched a drop form above the bottle mouth, enlarge and fall.

“Do you think I was making a fuss?” he asked.

“Yes.”

He looked at her.

“You asked.”

“I did.”

“My mother would have made a worse one. She sent my brother back up three flights once for a spoon.”

“It wasn’t a spoon.”

“No.”

The bottle filled. He replaced it with the second.

Zerla held out her sleeve. “Can you get that through? It’s frayed.”

Drevik trimmed the end with his little scissors, twisted it firmly and threaded it through the worked hole. Her sleeve had been repaired many times with different cloth. The stitching was serviceable and ugly. He resisted the temptation to straighten a row she had not asked him to touch.

“Thanks.”

“You’ve lost the thread from your hair.”

“I know.”

“I’ve got some blue.”

“That’s all right. It’ll come out again.”

He returned the sleeve and watched the bottle fill. By the time they rejoined the others, he was hungry enough to eat the food he had put aside.

The afternoon passage rose beside a wall full of narrow mineral seams. When their lights moved, the seams flashed in separate places, like distant windows appearing and going dark. Drevik found himself turning his head to follow them. Once he stopped altogether and Aevra waited without calling.

Then he heard a voice from the right-hand wall.

It was no more than a cadence, two short sounds and a longer one. He thought of the way his father called from the back room when he needed help lifting something and did not want to admit he needed help.

Drevik turned.

A shallow opening lay behind a screen of hanging stone. His light reached only the nearest folds. He drew breath to answer before remembering that his father was in Kharveth.

“Did you hear that?” he asked.

His words came back from above, thinned and oddly divided.

That. Hear. A sound that might have been you.

Aevra moved beside him. She held her own lamp low and listened.

From somewhere ahead, Baerun called to Drelset. The sound entered the folds of stone and returned in the same questioning cadence.

Drevik’s face grew hot.

“The shape catches it,” Aevra said.

“Yes.”

She did not ask whom he had thought he heard. He was grateful enough that he nearly told her.

They camped on a raised section above the next watercourse. Thaereth said they were making adequate progress. Aevra examined the ceiling. The word adequate irritated Drevik until he realized he had wanted some reassurance and would have resented any she gave.

After they ate, he took out the copy and drew the small bronze plate in the margin, recording only what he had actually seen. Its size he could not know exactly. He left the far edge unfinished.

Baerun settled beside him with his left arm folded across his chest.

“Did I say something wrong?” the hauler asked.

Drevik kept looking at the paper. “At the shaft?”

“Yes.”

“I don’t know.”

Baerun rubbed the edge of his thumbnail against his beard. “I wasn’t talking about you.”

“I didn’t think you were.”

“I’ve stopped for people.”

Drevik looked at him then.

“Sometimes we got them,” Baerun said. He seemed about to add something, but changed his mind. “You want that cloth under your case. The ground sweats here.”

He pushed a folded scrap toward Drevik and lay down with his back to the wall.

Drevik put the cloth beneath the case. He sat for a long time with the unfinished drawing on his knee, until Ildret asked him either to use the light or let her put it out.

Chapter 10 — Drelset’s support

The old control room could be heard before it could be seen.

Water struck something hollow at irregular intervals. Between the impacts came a grinding sound, followed by a brief release and a rush. Aevra stopped at the top of the stair and held up her hand.

The others halted behind her. Their equipment settled with small clicks and creaks. In the quiet between two impacts she could hear the water continuing, fast and close.

“Gate moving?” Drelset asked.

“Something is.”

Zerla came forward. “The old feeder had a flap below the wheel chamber. If it’s still there—”

Another impact cut her off.

Aevra took Ildret with her down the first stretch. The stair followed the outer wall of a chamber much larger than the receiving room they had left two days before. Their lamps revealed a rail, then a landing, then the upper part of an iron wheel mounted horizontally over a shaft. Beyond it, water moved through a channel whose far wall was lost in spray.

The wheel had once operated the lower gates. Its spokes remained, thick with rust, but the handle sockets were empty. A length of chain descended from its center. The chain tightened, slackened and tightened again as something below shifted under the water’s pressure.

Aevra watched several movements before going farther.

The landing beneath the wheel was sound. The road continued along the near wall, crossing a short service opening by three long slabs laid side by side. The inner slab lay close to the wall; two others made up the outer width of the road. Beyond the crossing, a second stair rose toward what Zerla thought would be the upper bank route.

There were marks of water above the road, but the present stream lay below it.

“Better look at the crossing,” Aevra said.

Ildret held the light while she knelt.

The slabs were old, their upper surfaces worn shallow. The first rested on a visible shoulder of masonry. The second and third met over a support she could not see from above. Aevra tested the edges with a small hammer. The sound was not as clear as she wanted, drowned by the intermittent grinding below.

She went back to the others.

“We pass the loads separately. No one on the crossing while a load’s there.”

Baerun nodded. Drelset took the short timbers from the frame. Thaereth asked where they could stand clear if the wheel moved farther. Aevra pointed to the stair recess behind the landing.

They brought the stores down in stages. Drevik set the lamp case on a dry shelf and asked where he could help. Aevra put him beside Ildret to take passed loads. Kouven waited with the medical bag. Zerla crossed lightly to inspect the second stair, then returned by the same route.

“It goes up,” she said. “There’s a turn I can’t see past.”

“Later.”

The wheel moved again. Aevra felt the vibration through the soles of her boots. She disliked working beside a mechanism she could not isolate, but nothing on the road had shifted.

They passed the first stores bundle across. Then a second. Baerun stood on the near edge guiding the folded frame while Drelset received it from the far side. The frame was awkward in the narrow space. One iron corner caught against the rail, and Baerun stepped sideways to free it.

The slab beneath his left foot dropped.

It did not fall clear. One end remained caught while the other tilted sharply into the service opening. Baerun went down with it, his weight striking the broken edge at the knee. The frame twisted out of his hands. Drelset caught one end and nearly followed him.

“Let it go!” Aevra shouted.

Drelset released the frame. It clattered against the wall and lodged across the opening below Baerun.

The hauler made a sound Aevra had never heard from him. His right arm hooked over the remaining rail; his left leg was trapped between the tilted slab and the side of the opening. Water showed through the gap below his boot.

Aevra was on her knees before she knew she had moved.

“Don’t pull,” she told Drelset.

He had caught Baerun’s coat across the shoulders. He stopped, holding the man where he was without lifting.

“Baerun. Look at me.”

The hauler looked up. His mouth was open. He was trying to breathe around pain.

“Your leg’s caught. We’re going to take the weight off it. Keep that arm there.”

He nodded once.

Aevra examined what she could see. The slab had lost its inner bearing. Its outer end still sat on the near masonry, but the edge was breaking under the changed load. Baerun’s boot was wedged where the slab pressed against an old iron bracket. If they pulled him upwards, the trapped leg would take it. If the slab moved farther, it might take him with it.

Drelset lay flat on the far side, one arm through a gap in the rail. His face had gone pale beneath the dust.

“Give me a line,” he said.

Ildret passed it. He got a loop under Baerun’s arms with the hauler’s help, working it behind him while Aevra kept her hands clear of the moving edge. Thaereth and Zerla took the line from above, securing it around a sound post on the landing. It would keep him from falling farther. It would not free the leg.

The wheel ground behind them.

“Where’s the long bar?” Aevra asked.

Drevik brought it without a word. She slid it into the gap near the slab’s upper end, tested the purchase and stopped. Lifting there would push the lower end harder against Baerun’s leg.

Drelset saw it too.

Aevra tested the outermost slab and crossed it on hands and knees, keeping her weight toward its sound outer bearing. Zerla followed at her direction. They were now on the far side with Drelset, while Thaereth held the safety line from the near landing.

“Under from the side,” Drelset said.

“There isn’t room.”

“From the frame.”

“The frame isn’t fixed.”

“It will be if we pin it.”

He pointed with his free hand. The fallen frame lay diagonally below the slab, one end caught against the wall bracket, the other resting on the opposite shoulder. It could not take the full slab’s weight safely. But it might hold a short timber in position long enough to create a new bearing.

Aevra shook her head. “That shoulder’s too narrow.”

“Then double it.”

“With what?”

“The cut pieces. The ones from stores.”

She looked. Two short lengths could be set side by side on the surviving masonry below the frame. A crosspiece would distribute the load. They would have to reach in from the far side, where Drelset lay, and set them without dropping anything onto Baerun’s trapped foot.

The plan was possible. She had not thought of it.

“Show me where,” she said.

He told her. His voice was quick, nearly angry, but the positions were exact. Aevra repeated them back and he corrected one. They moved.

Ildret shifted the lamp so he could see beneath the slab. Drevik passed the short pieces one at a time. He kept his body back from the edge, extending only his arms. Kouven spoke to Baerun while the others worked, asking about the feeling in his toes, keeping him answering.

The hauler swore at the question.

“That’s an answer,” Kouven said. “Can you move them?”

“Yes.”

“Both feet?”

“The one I can feel.”

“Which is that?”

Baerun told him in language that would have made Aevra laugh anywhere else.

Drelset got the first piece into position. The second slipped, struck the bracket and hung. He reached for it and stopped short of putting his hand beneath the slab.

“A little lower with the light.”

Ildret lowered it.

He used the end of the short hammer to push the timber clear. It dropped into place beside the first. He laid the crosspiece over them, then asked Aevra to test the upper bearing.

She could see enough now. It would take the movement they needed if the slab did not break across its middle.

They put the long bar in from Drelset’s side. Aevra and Zerla took its outer end. Thaereth kept the safety line tight without lifting Baerun. Drevik braced the new support with a second short piece while keeping his fingers outside the joint.

“Ready,” Drelset said.

Aevra pushed.

The slab moved a fraction. Baerun cried out. She stopped.

“Again,” he said, before she could ask.

Kouven was watching his leg. “A little. We need it clear of the boot.”

They pushed again. The timber creaked. Dust fell into the water below.

Baerun dragged his foot sideways. The boot did not come with it at first; then its upper tore where the iron had bitten through. His leg came free in a rush that pulled him against the safety line.

Thaereth paid out the secured line while Drelset and Aevra drew him onto the far paving. Kouven caught his injured leg before it struck the ground. Aevra eased the bar back until the slab rested on the temporary support.

For a moment nobody spoke.

The wheel ground again, exactly as it had before.

“Move him back,” Aevra said.

They carried Baerun to the recess beyond the crossing. Kouven came over the outer slab with his medical bag; Ildret and Drevik followed separately, bringing the lamps. Once Baerun was clear and the safety line could be released, Thaereth crossed last. He was shaking violently. Kouven cut away the damaged trouser leg. The wound beneath the knee was deep and dirty, but the angle of the leg was not the awful angle Aevra had feared. The boot came off in pieces. Blood had soaked the stocking along one side.

“Can you bend it?” Kouven asked.

Baerun tried and stopped with his teeth clenched.

“Don’t do it again.”

“You asked.”

“I did.”

Aevra left them enough light and returned to the crossing. Drelset was still lying beside it, looking down at his support.

“Come away.”

“In a moment.”

“Now.”

He sat up slowly. His hands were trembling. He pressed them against his thighs, then rubbed them down his trousers, leaving dark streaks.

Aevra knelt beside him.

“It held.”

“Yes.”

“You saw it before I did.”

He looked at her with a flash of anger. “I’d rather you’d seen the slab.”

She absorbed that without answering. He turned away, breathing hard.

After a little while he said, “I didn’t see it either.”

“No.”

They sat together until Ildret called for another water bottle.

Cleaning Baerun’s wound took most of the next hour. Kouven used water they could afford only because the station bottles were still full. He removed grit with patient hands while the hauler gripped a rolled strap. The sound of his breath filled the recess. Drevik stood holding the lamp, face turned toward the work even when he plainly wanted to look elsewhere.

Thaereth examined the route behind them. Getting Baerun back up the side stair would require help and a carrying arrangement. The damaged crossing could not be used casually again. They were all on the far side now except for part of the stores, which remained on the landing near the wheel.

Drelset and Aevra devised a way to retrieve the remaining packs without putting weight on the broken section. A long line, a guide from the rail, one bundle at a time. It was tedious work and, after the rescue, frightening in a way it would not have been that morning. Aevra tested each fastening twice. She knew she was doing it. She did it anyway.

They recovered the food, medical stores and lamp oil. The folded frame could not be removed without disturbing the support beneath the slab. Baerun asked about it while Kouven bound his leg.

“Leave it,” he said when told.

“We may need it for you.”

“I’m not going on that thing again.”

His laugh broke halfway through. Kouven told him to keep still.

They settled in the recess for the remainder of the day. Thaereth sent Zerla a short way up the far stair to check whether there was a safer resting place above. The runner returned with news of a broad landing and an old service route toward the upper bank. It might connect with maintained workings. She had not gone beyond the marked turn.

“Tomorrow,” Thaereth said. “We don’t move him farther today.”

Aevra agreed. She wanted Baerun resting, the group fed, and an hour in which nothing required her to decide under moving stone.

They ate with the sound of the gates below them. Ildret warmed a little water for a drink. Drevik produced the dried plums Aevra had bought in the market; she had forgotten passing them to him when rearranging the loads. They shared them out unevenly because Baerun claimed the largest and nobody felt like arguing.

Drelset called his support the cooper’s stair. Aevra objected that this was unfair to a sound support. He smiled for the first time since the fall.

Baerun wanted to know who would carry his share of the food now. Zerla said they could solve that by eating it. He told her she had the mind of a hauler and the shoulders of a broom. She threw a plum stone at him, missed and had to retrieve it because Ildret would not have debris left where someone might sleep.

As the lamps were shaded, Aevra went back to look at the water.

The channel below the wheel had risen slightly. She marked the level with chalk on a dry edge and watched several gate movements. The grinding interval was longer than it had been in the morning. A wad of branches struck the lower obstruction, hung, and tore away. In the brief gap she could see the far wall, slick and steep, with a new pale scrape where something large had passed.

Thaereth joined her.

“Rising?”

“A little.”

“Enough to reach us?”

“Not at this rate.”

The officer waited.

“I don’t know what’s holding it below,” Aevra said.

“Then we take the upper stair in the morning.”

“Yes.”

They returned to the recess. Baerun was asleep, one hand still holding the rolled strap. Kouven eased it from his fingers and set it beside the medical bag. Drelset had lain down with his boots on. His face, without its usual expression of prepared amusement, looked younger.

Aevra sat beside the wall and checked her hands. A splinter had entered below the nail of her left thumb. She had not felt it until now. Drevik lent her his fine pick. She worked the splinter free, cleaned the point and gave it back.

He put it carefully into its little roll, beside the tool he used to lower his father’s lamp wick.

Chapter 11 — The bank between

The chalk mark was under water.

Aevra held her lamp over the edge. Last night she had drawn the line where she could reach it without kneeling. Now the current covered it, and the wheel above the channel had stopped moving. Its chain stood taut. The lower gate was being held against something she could not see.

She went back to the recess.

“Up,” she said. “We’re going.”

Thaereth was already awake. She looked at Aevra’s face and began rolling her cover. Nobody asked for hot water.

Baerun had stiffened during the night. When Kouven tried to help him stand, his injured leg folded and he caught the wall with a flat hand, striking it hard enough to make Aevra flinch. They lowered him again. The dressing had spotted through, but the bleeding had not begun afresh.

“I can hop,” he said.

“Not up that stair,” Kouven told him.

They made a seat of two joined carrying straps. Drelset and Aevra took the weight, with Baerun’s good arm around Drelset’s shoulders and his bad shoulder supported rather than pulled. Thaereth carried Aevra’s pack as well as her own. Drevik divided the remaining stores with Ildret. Zerla went ahead to check the stair and returned before they had lifted Baerun.

“It’s clear to the turn. There’s water across the landing.”

“How deep?”

“Ankle.”

“Moving?”

“Toward the old crossing.”

Aevra nodded. She had hoped for a dry upper route. Ankle-deep was still a route.

The first dozen steps were the worst. Baerun could not keep his injured foot from touching the risers. He apologized every time he jerked against them, which made Drelset angry.

“Stop saying it.”

“I’m trying not to kick you.”

“You’re kicking her.”

“Then I’m doing well.”

His breath caught on the last word. They stopped at the turn and let him sit on the dry upper edge of a step while Kouven adjusted the support beneath his knee.

Below, the wheel gave a single long metallic groan.

Aevra looked down. She could no longer see the broken crossing. Water had spread over the near landing, and the place where she had left the chalk was a smooth dark sheet.

“Leave the long bar,” she said.

Thaereth had been carrying it through the loops of her pack. “We may need it.”

“We need to get him up.”

The officer laid it along the step, where it would not roll. She took Aevra’s end of the sling for the next flight. Aevra followed with the packs, grateful enough to be ashamed of it.

At the landing, water ran over their boots and drained through a break in the outer rail. The old service road crossed a feeder on a short bridge before reaching the upper bank. Beyond the bridge, a rising passage carried a line of newer lamp brackets. Zerla recognized them.

“That’s the branch from the upper works. It must be.”

Thaereth put Baerun down against the wall.

“Go to the first turn. Don’t go out of hearing.”

Zerla tightened the cord of the gate key inside her coat, took her lamp and crossed. The bridge was narrow but had both parapets. Aevra watched her feet. The paving was uneven and wet, yet it did not move.

Zerla reached the far side and raised her hand. She went up several steps, examined the first bracket, then came back.

“It’s theirs. There’s an issue mark.”

Kharveth’s waterworkers. An open route, or at least a route that somebody had maintained more recently than the road behind them.

Aevra took off her pack. “We’ll put a line across before we move him.”

Drelset uncoiled the longest rope. Zerla caught the thrown end at the second attempt and looped it around a substantial wall ring beside the upper passage. She pulled to show that the ring held.

The bridge shuddered.

Not much. Aevra felt it through the soles of her boots at the approach, a short vibration followed by the sound of stones rolling beneath water. She looked down into the feeder. The flow had changed direction along its near edge, curling back on itself around a submerged obstruction.

“Zerla. Stay there.”

The girl had begun to come back. She stopped with one foot on the bridge.

A branch rose out of the feeder, turned, and struck the underside of the span. More branches followed. For an instant the water lifted like a moving floor. Then the bridge’s middle joint opened.

Aevra stepped backwards. She could not have said afterwards whether she had ordered the others back or merely shouted.

The central paving dropped first. A section of parapet fell inward, breaking as it struck the debris beneath. The rest remained for a moment, a crooked shelf extending from either bank, and then the near section slid away with the noise of a cartload of stone poured down a shaft.

Spray struck Aevra’s face. She raised an arm. When she could see again, Zerla was crouched above the far approach, lamp held against her chest.

“Are you hurt?” Thaereth called.

Zerla shook her head. Perhaps she answered. The water was too loud.

The rope still crossed the gap. Its middle lay in the current, straining from the ring to the coil Drelset had caught under his boot. A fallen stone struck it. He looked at Aevra.

“Let it go.”

He lifted his foot. The loose end whipped across the landing and vanished into the feeder. The line remained attached to Zerla’s ring, trailing downstream. She reached for it.

“Leave it!” Thaereth shouted.

Zerla looked up.

“Go back. Tell them where we are.”

The girl stared across the gap. Aevra saw the refusal begin in her face.

“We can’t cross,” Thaereth called. “The control approach is gone. Seven here. Baerun hurt. We’re taking the side road away from the water.”

Zerla cupped a hand behind her ear. Thaereth repeated it in pieces, making her call each piece back.

“Seven. Baerun. Side road.”

“Tell them we need a lower connection.”

Aevra pointed to the mark on the wall beside her. Three bars, half hidden by mineral crust. Zerla nodded fiercely. Her mouth twisted; she pressed it shut.

Then the wheel below let go.

The sound came up through the stair and over the feeder, a tearing crash followed by a rush so great that the air moved against Aevra’s coat. Water burst from the landing behind them. It struck the outer parapet and spilled through, carrying a piece of their abandoned bar briefly into sight.

Thaereth grabbed Aevra’s sleeve.

They could not remain at the broken bridge. The landing was becoming a channel. Baerun had pulled himself higher against the wall, his face gray, while Kouven and Drevik tried to lift him without slipping.

Aevra took the sling. Drelset took the other end. They moved sideways along the wall to the only road left, a narrow cut rising away from the feeder before turning beneath a low arch. Ildret went ahead with her lamp. Thaereth brought the last loose pack.

Aevra looked back once. Zerla stood on the far bank, high enough for the moment, one hand raised. Then Ildret called about the low lintel and Aevra had to turn her attention to Baerun’s head.

They passed beneath the arch.

The cut rose for twenty paces and opened onto a ledge above another reach of the river. The water here was lower than the road they had left, but rising fast between steep walls. Aevra could see the underside of a natural shelf across from them, streaked with white deposits like long fingers. Things were moving along its edge. Small animals, wet-backed, pressing together as they sought higher ground.

Their own ledge narrowed ahead. There was nowhere to set Baerun down until a recess farther along.

“Keep going,” Aevra said.

Her shoulder burned. She wanted the injured man to weigh less. The thought came with such plain force that she nearly laughed. Drelset’s mouth was open as he breathed, and she could hear each step he took as an effort separate from the one before.

They reached the recess and lowered Baerun. Drevik put down his load, then went back several steps to help Thaereth. Aevra sat on the stone without meaning to.

Ildret counted the lamps. Kouven checked the dressing. Thaereth put her hand inside her coat and took out the plan. Its lower edge had become wet. She tried to open it carefully, but a folded corner tore.

“Where are we?” Drevik asked.

The officer did not answer immediately.

Aevra looked at the water. A thick plank floated past with a piece of ironwork still attached. She recognized the shape of the mounting. It had come from the control room.

“Past the feeder,” she said. “Below the upper bank.”

“Which way back?”

“We have to find another way.”

Drevik’s gaze went toward the arch they had passed. A brown surge filled its lower half, subsided and left the stone shining.

Baerun said, “Don’t look at it. Give me the bottle.”

Drevik knelt beside him and held the bottle while he drank. Baerun’s hands had begun shaking again.

They rested only long enough to shift the loads. Aevra could feel the river’s cold in the air. The recess would be safe for a little while, but she could not tell how little. There were old pegs farther along the ledge, possibly belonging to a path above. Ildret took her lamp to examine them.

A light moved beyond hers.

Aevra got up too quickly, swayed, and steadied herself against the wall.

The second light was low and pale. It disappeared behind a projection, then returned. A small figure stood on a higher shelf beyond the pegs, holding one hand out with its palm toward them.

“Wait there,” a woman called.

Her Aeler was accented but clear. Aevra almost answered that they had nowhere else to go.

The woman knelt, felt along the edge of the shelf, then came down by steps Aevra had not seen. She was a Mazereth, shorter than any of them, broad through the body beneath a close-fitting coat, with a soft cap pulled over her ears. Her light hung from a clasp at her chest. A coil of fine line crossed one shoulder. There was no open flame.

She looked at Baerun, then at the river, then up at the wet arch.

“How many?”

“Seven,” Thaereth said. “One went up.”

“Up where?”

Thaereth pointed. The woman made a small movement with her mouth that Aevra could not interpret.

“Good if she can.”

“You know the upper road?”

“I know that water.”

She crouched beside Baerun without touching him. “Can he put a foot down?”

“The right,” Kouven said. “A little on the left. Not safely.”

“Then you’ll need three at the turn.”

“What turn?” Aevra asked.

The woman pointed back toward the pegs. “Mine. This shelf goes under when the lower throat closes. It’s closing.”

She spoke as someone reporting a door being shut. Aevra looked at the current, trying to see what she saw, and could not.

“Who are you?” Thaereth asked.

“Uivren. Put your questions in a pocket. You need your hands.”

She went ahead to the pegs. Drelset rose and took the sling again. Aevra wanted to ask where the path went, whether there was a place to stop, whether anyone could get back from it to the bank where Zerla had stood. Uivren was already pointing out a foothold to Ildret.

Aevra lifted Baerun with Drelset. Kouven supported the injured leg at the turn. For several moments all three of them were pressed against cold stone, unable to see where the others had their feet. Then Baerun was on the higher shelf and Aevra could kneel without water touching her boot.

Uivren returned for the smallest pack. She picked it up, adjusted its straps and walked away with it as if they had settled the matter.

Aevra followed.

Chapter 12 — Something feeding

Uivren’s path had no rail.

It had places where a hand could be put, and occasionally a worn hollow for a foot, but the river lay below it with nothing intervening except the width of stone on which they stood. Aevra understood how to carry weight on a stair. This asked for the same movements without allowing the same mistakes.

Uivren went first, then Ildret with a shaded lamp. Kouven walked behind them carrying two packs. Baerun came between Aevra and Drelset, using a stick in his right hand when the ledge was wide enough. Drevik and Thaereth followed with the remaining stores.

The little surveyor stopped frequently. At first Aevra thought she was waiting for them. But Uivren touched the wall at each stop and sometimes put her face close to a crack without looking into it. Once she changed their order, moving Ildret’s flame behind Baerun before they passed beneath a low overhang.

Aevra did not like obeying instructions whose purpose she could not establish. She obeyed them.

They reached a dry pocket large enough to rest. Uivren put down the pack she had taken and unfastened the top of her coat. A narrow strip of woven material beneath it was wet with sweat. She wrung the strip out carefully away from her instruments and laid it across a warm stone.

“Is there a road up?” Thaereth asked.

“Several. Not here.”

“Which is nearest?”

“Nearest to what?”

“To the upper works. Kharveth.”

Uivren shook her head. “I don’t go there.”

“Someone must.”

“Then ask someone.”

The officer closed her mouth.

They shared water. Uivren had her own bottle, flattened to sit against the curve of her hip. She declined their bread but accepted a piece of cheese after smelling it. Baerun asked her how far to somewhere he could lie down without his head touching a wall.

“If you can walk, one sleeping,” she said. “With carrying, more.”

“One day?” Drevik asked.

Uivren glanced up at the roof of rock. “If you call it that.”

“We do,” Thaereth said.

“Then more than one.”

Aevra ate slowly, trying to make her mouth accept food. The river filled the pocket with noise. Somewhere beyond it came little cries, thin and repetitive. She had heard the same sound before they climbed from the lower ledge.

“What are those?”

Uivren turned her head. “Water-runners. Their banks are under.”

The surveyor said the local name too, a short sound Aevra could not reproduce. She did not try.

When they set out again, they saw the animals gathered on a broad sloping shelf across the narrow reach. They were longer than rats, with smooth dark backs and pale feet. Some carried smaller animals in their mouths. Others climbed over one another to reach crevices already occupied. A few slipped, struck the water and vanished without a splash Aevra could hear over the current.

Drevik stopped watching first.

Uivren led them beneath another overhang. Ahead, the path rose by a steep natural step into a cleft. The step was high enough that Baerun would need lifting. Beyond it lay dry ground. Aevra could feel a cooler draught coming from the opening.

They began moving packs through.

Drelset climbed first, using an iron dog Aevra set in a crack. The dog held a short line by which they could steady Baerun. Uivren inspected the upper footing and told them where not to place the weight. The whole arrangement took time. Aevra became impatient with Drevik for opening the wrong pack and then realized she had named the wrong one.

“Leave it. That one’s all right.”

He tightened it again without answering.

A sound from the animals changed.

Aevra looked across the reach. Their bodies were pressing higher into the slope. A dark shape moved at the water’s edge below them, too large for any of the animals she had been watching. It advanced in short, low movements, the heavy tail following in a curve through the shallows.

A drake.

She had seen one dead in a market yard, brought in on a sledge for its hide. That animal had seemed big enough. This one’s shoulders rose nearly to the shelf on which the water-runners huddled. Its head was broad and low, with a pale scar crossing one eye. When it turned, she saw the wet line of its mouth.

It took an animal from the edge without apparent haste.

“Keep moving,” Uivren said.

Her voice had changed. No riddle, no dryness. Aevra passed the next pack up.

They were on the other side of a fast channel. For a moment she allowed that fact to reassure her. Then the drake moved downstream to a place where fallen rock broke the current. It put one forefoot on the first stone, tested it with its weight and stepped across.

“Lights in,” Ildret said.

She closed the side shutters of the lamps, leaving only narrow downward openings. Aevra could still see the rock at her feet. Much of the river disappeared. The drake remained visible as movement against water, then was lost behind the lower curve of their own ledge.

They had not got Baerun up.

Drelset was crouched above, one end of the steadying line around his waist. Kouven stood behind the hauler, supporting the injured leg. Baerun had both hands on the rock, face pressed close to it, trying to do more of the work than he could.

“Wait for us,” Aevra said.

“I am waiting.”

The drake’s head appeared below the overhang they had just passed.

Its eyes caught the faint light. One shone more brightly than the scarred other. It opened its mouth enough for Aevra to see the uneven line of teeth and the dark moving tongue. Then it lowered its head toward the stone.

Something scraped along the rock. Claws, or a loose plate on its belly.

Drevik had gone still beside the food bundles.

“The fish,” he said.

“What?”

“The oil on that wrapping. It’s been leaking.”

One of the preserved-food packets had been damaged in the flight from the landing. A strong smell had followed them which Aevra had attributed to the river. Drevik touched the greasy edge and held up his fingers.

Uivren looked at it. “Away. Far.”

Thaereth reached for the bundle. Drevik caught her wrist.

“Not all of it. The inner cloth.”

His hands moved quickly now. He unfastened the wrapping, took out the worst-soaked packet and folded the greasy cloth around it. The remainder went into the open pack. He looked for somewhere to throw the bait that would not merely bring the animal along the path they needed.

A low rock tongue extended into the water below and behind them.

“There,” Aevra said.

Drevik drew back his arm. The drake lifted its head. He stopped.

It was closer than she had realized. Beneath the low roof its size was difficult to comprehend: the head entirely distinct, the body a pressure filling the darkness behind it. Drevik’s hand remained raised, the packet dangling from his fingers. He could not release it.

Aevra took it from him.

She did not throw immediately. She had to judge the water, the angle and the weight. The cloth was warm from his hand. The drake shifted forward, flattening itself beneath the lowest stone.

She threw.

The packet struck the rock tongue, slid and caught in a crack. The drake’s head followed it. For a breath nothing else happened. Then it moved down toward the smell, the tail scraping their path.

“Now,” Uivren said.

Aevra put her shoulder beneath Baerun’s right thigh. Kouven steadied the left leg without pulling it. Drelset took up the line, and Baerun pushed with his good foot. He rose awkwardly, crying out once when the dressing caught the rock. Then his upper body was across the step and Drelset could pull him clear.

Ildret went next. Drevik was still looking toward the drake.

“Give me your hand,” Aevra said.

He held out the wrong one, the fingers greasy.

“The other.”

He obeyed. She put his hand on the iron dog and guided his foot to the hollow below it. Once he began climbing, he moved well enough. Thaereth shoved his pack after him. Uivren went up without using the line.

The drake tore the cloth apart.

Aevra could hear the little snapping sounds despite the river. There was very little food in the packet. She wished Drevik had let Thaereth throw the whole bundle.

The officer climbed. Aevra waited below until her boot cleared the foothold, then went after her. As she reached for the upper edge, something struck the lower rock. The drake had turned back toward them, its head raised, smelling where the food had come from.

Aevra’s wet boot slipped.

Drelset caught her forearm. She struck the wall with her knee, found the hollow again and pushed. Her pack snagged under the overhang. She could feel it holding her back, absurdly stubborn cloth while the animal moved below.

“Buckle,” Thaereth said.

Aevra released the chest strap. Drelset pulled her clear. The pack remained wedged for a moment, then fell to the ledge beneath.

The drake struck it.

The sound was soft, a bursting of cloth. Aevra lay on the upper ground while Thaereth pulled the remaining strap off her arm. Below, the animal shook the pack once and dragged it away from the rock.

Her bed cover. Two days of shared food. A short hammer, a wedge roll, a dry shirt Maeven had folded.

“Leave it,” Drelset said.

“I know.”

She had not moved to go back. She resented being told and was glad he had told her.

They crawled farther into the cleft before standing. The drake could not fit its shoulders through the narrow approach, but nobody proposed testing its patience. Uivren led them around two bends into a space where the roof rose and a thin stream crossed the floor.

Baerun was swearing continuously now. Kouven made him sit and began unfastening the bloodied dressing. Aevra went to help.

“Hands first,” he said.

She looked at her fingers. Oil, black rock dust and a smear of blood from her knee filled the creases.

He pointed to the water. “Wash. Properly.”

She knelt at the stream. It was cold enough to hurt. Dirt came from beneath her nails slowly. Drevik knelt beside her and scrubbed the grease from his hand with a piece of cloth. Neither spoke.

When they returned, Kouven had the dressing off. The wound had opened at one edge during the lift, but the bleeding was manageable. Baerun stared at the roof while the orderly worked. Ildret sat where she could watch the cleft behind them, one lamp shaded beside her knee.

Drevik said, “I should have thrown it.”

Aevra wrung out a clean cloth.

“You found it.”

“I couldn’t—”

“Hold this.”

She gave him the bowl. He held it level for Kouven. His hands shook a little, then steadied as the bowl grew heavier.

They stayed until Baerun could bear being moved again. Aevra borrowed a dry cloth from Thaereth and wrapped it around her scraped knee. There were no teeth in her leg, no one missing. Her shirt was still somewhere in the drake’s mouth. She found herself thinking about the way Maeven had turned its sleeves inward when packing it.

When Uivren rose, Aevra rose with her.

Chapter 13 — Uivren waits

Uivren slept with one hand beneath her cheek and the other inside her coat.

Aevra watched her for a while before lying down. The surveyor had found them a dry chamber above the stream, reached by a slope they could manage without lifting Baerun. There was room to stretch out. Aevra had almost forgotten the pleasure of extending both legs without putting a heel against somebody else’s stores.

She had no bed cover. Thaereth divided hers, laying it across them both without discussion. Between their shoulders there remained a narrow cold gap. Aevra tried to sleep with her arms folded into it.

From the wall came a sound like a distant person drawing breath.

She opened her eyes. Uivren had not moved. Ildret sat near the entrance with the only burning lamp, its flame almost covered. Baerun snored once, woke himself and subsided. The wall breathed again.

Aevra put a palm against it. The stone was dry and cool. There was no vibration she could feel.

Uivren said, without opening her eyes, “It does that.”

“What is it?”

“A space.”

Aevra waited for more. The surveyor turned onto her other side.

At some point Aevra slept. She woke with Thaereth’s elbow in her ribs and the full conviction that she had missed a service bell. For a moment she could place herself in a work shelter anywhere beneath Kharveth. Then she saw the pale light at Uivren’s chest and remembered her pack falling.

Kouven was changing Baerun’s dressing. The hauler had a little fever. He could drink, answer questions and put some weight on the right leg, but the left remained swollen. Kouven wanted another day without travel. Uivren wanted them out of this chamber before the next pressure change.

They argued through the practical details. The surveyor had words for the changes in the passages that neither Aevra nor Kouven understood. She demonstrated with her hands, bringing one palm toward the other until the air between them would have been squeezed out.

“Then we open a path,” Aevra said.

“To where?”

“A shaft. Any—”

Uivren’s face made her stop.

“Drink first,” the surveyor said. “You’re talking before drinking.”

Aevra took the bottle because she needed it, and hated the woman briefly with an intensity disproportionate to the offense.

They went on after eating. Uivren carried less than anyone, but much of what she carried was metal or glass in fitted pockets. She checked several pieces before setting out. One was a short tube with a thin membrane stretched across its end. Another looked like a pair of unequal calipers. Aevra wanted to ask about them. The earlier exchange kept her silent longer than it should have.

The passage rose through old worked stone and then narrowed into rock untouched by tools. Roots hung in one fissure, hair-fine and colorless. Aevra could not imagine a surface tree above so much mountain. Uivren said they belonged to something growing elsewhere and did not seem to find that an explanation requiring further detail.

At a smooth shaft, she stopped.

The crossing was short. Its floor was sound, the far opening visible, and a clear strip of dry stone ran between damp patches. Aevra could have carried Baerun across it in less than a minute with Drelset’s help. Uivren put down her small pack and sat against the wall.

“How long?” Thaereth asked.

The surveyor held up a hand.

They waited. Baerun leaned against Aevra, his weight gradually increasing as his good leg tired. She lowered him onto a folded cloth. Kouven loosened the binding above the wound and examined the foot.

Aevra approached the shaft. Its walls were smooth from water, with no loose pieces overhead. She looked up and could not see the top. The air felt warmer near its center.

Uivren caught her sleeve.

“Here.”

“I’m only looking.”

“Look here.”

She pointed to a shallow hollow near the floor on their side of the opening. A few loose fibers lay in it. Aevra watched. The fibers stirred, settled, and stirred again, this time moving outward.

Uivren drew her back.

The shaft gave a low thump. A breath of warm air passed across the opening, followed by a stronger rush that flattened the flame in Ildret’s lamp against its shutter. Ildret capped it at once. Something sour and mineral entered Aevra’s mouth. She pulled her sleeve over her nose before Uivren pushed her farther along the wall.

The noise grew until she could feel it in her teeth.

It lasted perhaps a minute. Afterwards the shaft stood as before, empty and apparently harmless. A wet line had appeared along one wall. Aevra looked at the crossing she had wanted to use and could think of nothing useful to say.

Uivren waited longer.

When she finally let them cross, she went first, carrying a little thread tied to the end of a rod. She did not hurry. The others followed at the pace she set, Baerun between Drelset and Aevra, his injured leg held clear by Kouven.

On the far side, Aevra asked about the tube.

The surveyor took it from her coat and put it in Aevra’s hand. The membrane was translucent, thin enough to show the dark ring beneath its edge. A little bead rested inside a bent channel on the tube’s side.

“For difference,” Uivren said. She pointed back, then ahead. “Not for everything.”

Aevra turned it carefully. “And the fibers?”

“Also difference.”

She took the instrument back before Aevra could touch the membrane.

They reached Uivren’s stored equipment later that day.

At least, they reached the opening where it should have been. A fresh fall had narrowed the entrance to a triangular gap. White dust covered the ledge. Uivren set down her pack very slowly.

Aevra watched her press two fingers against the side of the opening, then reach through to feel the inner edge. For the first time since they met, the surveyor’s movements had lost their quick assurance.

“What’s in there?” Thaereth asked.

“My long measure. Lenses. Dry things.”

“Can you leave them?”

Uivren turned her head. “Can you leave yours?”

Nobody answered. Aevra took Drelset’s lamp and knelt beside the opening.

The fall had come from a facing above the doorway, disturbed by something moving behind it. The inner lintel remained in place. A bag lay visible beyond, its strap caught under a piece of stone. The rest of the chamber was obscured.

“Was the facing loose?” Aevra asked.

“No.”

“When you left?”

“No.”

Aevra let the answer stand.

They could stabilise the small opening with the two short timbers Drelset still carried and blocks from the fall. Aevra did not propose removing the upper stone. She wanted only to prevent the sides from moving while Uivren reached the bag.

Drelset examined the arrangement and altered the angle of one timber. She accepted it without feeling obliged to remark on the acceptance.

Thaereth set a limit. Uivren glanced at the officer’s sandglass and then away. Aevra wondered whether the little woman had understood what it meant. She did not ask until later, when the surveyor told her, with some irritation, that she knew what falling sand was.

They worked.

Drevik helped hold the light. Ildret lay on her side to watch the gap above Uivren’s head. The surveyor went in only as far as her shoulders. She unfastened the trapped bag instead of pulling it free, passing its contents out one by one: a wrapped pair of lenses, a jointed instrument with several arms, a small packet of pale strips, and a stained cloth she insisted they take just as carefully as the rest.

The long measure was farther in.

Uivren could touch one end with a hook but could not turn it through the gap. Aevra could see her considering whether to move deeper. She put a hand on the woman’s ankle.

“No.”

Uivren remained still. Then she withdrew the hook, backed out and sat with dust on her face and a cut along one knuckle.

The measure stayed where it was.

They did not get a roof over themselves until the following rest. The shelter was another natural hollow, dry enough at one side for Baerun. Uivren arranged the recovered instruments on her cloth. One lens had cracked beneath its wrapping. She examined it, put it down, picked it up again and held it close to the pale light at her chest.

Aevra sat beside her.

“Can it be used?”

“For some things.”

“Can it be replaced?”

“Not here.”

They sat without speaking. The crack flashed when the light caught it. Uivren wrapped it again, more gently than before.

By their next waking, it was the ninth day since leaving Kharveth. Thaereth kept the count on the margin of the plan. Aevra knew because Drevik asked. He asked first how far they had come and then, when nobody could give him an answer he could put beside the old measures, how many days they had been away.

“Nine,” the officer said.

The number came reluctantly. Her fingers remained on the edge of the paper.

Drevik looked at his case.

“Zerla will be there.”

“By now, if the upper route held.”

“And they’ll tell my father.”

Thaereth folded the plan. “She knows where you were staying.”

They ate smaller portions that morning. Aevra’s lost pack had held enough food to make its absence visible. Uivren had stores of her own, including dense gray cakes she shared sparingly. They tasted of something roasted and something sour. Baerun said he liked them, then asked what they were made of. Uivren told him three names he did not know, which ended the inquiry.

Kouven washed Baerun’s leg with warmed water and let it dry before redressing it. The swelling had not worsened. They made the day’s first carrying shift with a little more patience.

At the next bend Uivren paused, and this time Aevra put down her load before asking why.

Chapter 14 — The lights ahead

Drevik dreamed of a door that would not stay on its hinges.

Every time he fitted the upper pin, the lower one came free. His father stood behind him holding the lamp too close to his shoulder. Drevik asked him to move it. He said he could not see if he moved it, and Drevik said it was Drevik who needed to see, and then someone shook him awake.

“Your turn,” Kouven said.

The orderly’s face appeared above him, lit from below by a closed lamp. Drevik sat up, trying to remember what he had agreed to do.

“Baerun wants to move. I’ve had him for a while.”

“Of course.”

They helped the hauler change position. His leg lay on a folded coat. He apologized once, sleepily, and then began snoring before Drevik had got the coat straight. Kouven lay down at once. Drevik remained sitting.
