They were in a chamber with a low stone shelf along one side. Water dripped somewhere behind the wall, with long intervals between drops. He had slept enough to wake cold. The corner of his case pressed against his hip through his bed cover. He moved it and felt the outline of the lamp inside.

He had almost thrown it away the previous day.

Not thrown it. He had considered leaving it on a ledge when they rearranged the packs. The thought had come so easily that he had picked the case up at once, as though somebody might have noticed.

He was tired of carrying it. He was tired of the care necessary to keep it dry. He wanted his father to understand, without having to be told, how much easier the journey would be without it.

Across the chamber Uivren’s pale light shifted. She was awake, checking a small piece of equipment against the wall. Drevik watched her hold still, read something he could not see, and move on. She did not appear afraid. He no longer took that as evidence that there was nothing to fear.

The next day’s walking began badly.

Baerun could use his good leg for longer stretches, but carrying him over rough ground tired everyone quickly. Aevra’s scraped knee had become stiff. Drelset snapped at Drevik for moving a pack before he had finished with it. Drevik snapped back. They apologized without making either apology pleasant.

Later, in a passage with pale bands across the roof, Drevik heard his father call.

“Mind the lid.”

He turned so quickly that his case struck the wall.

Behind him, Ildret was speaking to Kouven. “Mind the light,” she said again, pointing to the lamp hooked at his waist. Its shutter had opened against a projecting stone.

Drevik looked away before either of them could see his face.

He had known where his father was. He knew it now.

They rested near a fissure through which cool air moved steadily. Uivren gave the place a name. It sounded to Drevik like a word for a kind of cloth, but when he asked, she repeated it with a different stress and pointed at the fissure. He could not discover whether he had misunderstood the word or the question had made no sense.

He ate a small piece of cheese. The ration was small enough to be gone before he had finished wanting it.

Aevra sat with her head against the stone, eyes shut. Her hands were swollen at the knuckles. He thought of asking whether she regretted coming and was glad he did not. She opened her eyes before they set out and smiled at him without apparent reason.

They spent another night below a shelf of warm rock. Uivren said the warmth was ordinary there. Drevik slept better than he had in the cold chamber, and woke guilty about having found comfort in something he did not understand.

The lights appeared late in the following walk.

At first he thought they were reflections. Small pale marks lay ahead at different heights, separated by distances impossible to judge. Their own path had opened out, and the far wall was gone. He could feel the space in front of him before he could see its shape.

Aevra stopped.

“Are those yours?” she asked Uivren.

The surveyor made a sound of recognition and walked on.

“What did she say?” Thaereth asked.

“I don’t know,” Drevik said.

“Wait.”

Uivren looked back. “Beds.”

“People?”

“Beds.” She gestured impatiently for them to follow.

Baerun leaned on his stick. “I’ll take a bed without people.”

They went after her more slowly than she wanted. The pale marks multiplied. Some were still, others moved. As Drevik watched, one moving light paused beside three fixed ones and then continued along a line he had not seen before.

Traffic.

He could distinguish a narrow way now, rising diagonally across the darkness. Something had been placed along its outer edge at regular intervals. The moving light belonged to a figure carrying a load. Farther down, another figure stopped and waited for it to pass.

“They’re working,” he said.

Nobody answered. Aevra was looking at the same thing.

The air changed. A smell of wet wool and earth reached them, followed by a warmer, almost bread-like smell. Drevik’s mouth filled with saliva.

Their path descended into a low arch grown over with pale, fibrous material. Uivren called ahead. The answer came as a low hum, then words in a language Drevik did not know. The surveyor replied at length, more fluently than she spoke Aeler, and turned to wave them forward.

A person stood at the arch.

Drevik had seen drawings of Mycotheres in a trader’s book, but the flat image had not prepared him for the layered folds over the shoulders or the fine pale dust that moved when the figure lifted an arm. The face was set beneath a broad growth like a shelf, its edges darker than the center. The hands were recognizably hands. One held a small shallow vessel full of softly shining fibers.

Drevik had his lamp open.

The host reached for it. He drew it back instinctively.

“No,” he said. “It’s hot.”

The figure spoke sharply. Uivren came between them, not pushing either but bringing her own hand down over the lamp’s cap. The flame died.

“No burning here,” she said.

“I was warning—”

“No burning.”

The host pointed to the pale growth on the arch, then to the lamp and away down the path. He had arrived carrying fire into something that could burn.

“I’m sorry.”

He did not know whether the words were understood. He lowered the lamp, holding it clear of his clothes while the metal cooled.

The host handed him the shining vessel.

It was warm, with a rough underside that sat securely against his palm. The fibers inside gave off a low greenish light. When he moved his fingers over them, they dimmed where his shadow fell and slowly brightened again.

He stared too long. The host touched the edge of the vessel, then pointed toward the passage beyond.

“Come,” Uivren said.

They entered.

The passage opened onto terraces edged with low walls. Above and below, pale growth spread in ordered beds, interrupted by channels and narrow paths. Figures moved among them. A stream crossed beneath a bridge no wider than a table; beyond it stood a group of enclosed rooms with rounded openings. Warm moisture touched Drevik’s face. The sound he had taken for distant water resolved into many soft movements: brushing, pouring, something being turned against a rough surface.

Baerun could go no farther without sitting. He lowered himself against a wall and shut his eyes.

The host knelt in front of him. Kouven began explaining the injury in Aeler, slowly. Uivren translated some of it, then the host asked a question directly in a few careful Aeler words.

“Hot leg?”

“A little,” Kouven said. “Less than yesterday.”

“Water. Clean.” The host pointed to a room.

A second Mycothere appeared carrying folded material. The two spoke rapidly. The newcomer looked at the eight of them, then at the room, and set the folds down with unmistakable displeasure.

Drevik found this reassuring. He understood someone being annoyed by unexpected guests.

They moved Baerun inside. The room smelled of warm earth and washed cloth. A low platform occupied one side. The second host stripped its covering, shook it outside and replaced it with something dry. Baerun was allowed to lie down only after his outer clothes had been loosened and his boots removed.

The first host touched his own chest and said, “Hesh.”

Drevik gave his name. Hesh repeated it with difficulty, then tried Aevra’s. By the time they reached Thaereth, the second host had gone away and returned with water.

“Vorrin,” Uivren said, indicating her.

Vorrin gave Drevik a bowl. Something thick and steaming filled it. He hesitated, then saw Uivren eating from another and took a mouthful.

It tasted of toasted grain and a savory thing he could not name. There was a little sharpness at the end. He swallowed too quickly and burned the roof of his mouth.

Hesh made a soft, rattling sound. Laughter, Drevik thought, and this time he did not mind being its cause.

He sat on the floor with his back against the low platform. Across the room Aevra had stopped unfastening her coat halfway through and was eating with both hands around her bowl. Drelset’s eyes were shut. Ildret placed each extinguished lamp where the cooling metal could touch nothing delicate.

Drevik put the shining vessel beside his case and ate until the bowl was empty. Hesh took it from him and filled it again.

Part III — Houses under another sky

Chapter 15 — Clean sleeping

Aevra woke because something cracked beside her head.

She was upright before she knew where she was. Her hand went to the place where her hammer should have lain. There was no hammer. There was a greenish light, a wall of pale material and the smell of damp cloth.

The crack came again.

She put a hand against the wall. Its outer surface yielded slightly, with firm support behind. A split ran from a seam near the floor toward the low ceiling. As she watched, it widened. Air moved through it, cool against her fingers.

“Up,” she said.

Drelset opened his eyes. “What?”

“The wall.”

He sat up, blinking. Across the room Thaereth had already pulled her boots toward her. Drevik struggled out of his cover and knocked over the light vessel. Its shining fibers spilled onto the floor.

Vorrin came through the doorway.

She looked at them, then at the wall, and made an exasperated sound. With one hand she pulled a cord beside the opening. A section of the pale wall folded back on a frame, revealing a narrow ventilation space. The crack had been the first edge of the panel opening under its own tension.

“Morning,” she said.

Aevra remained standing with one boot on.

Vorrin pointed through the opened panel toward the terraces, where the lights had changed. Some were dimmer, others being uncovered. Several people were already carrying baskets along the paths.

“Morning,” she said again, with emphasis.

Then she knelt to gather the spilled fibers. Drevik joined her at once. Aevra took off her boot and sat on the platform’s edge. Her heart was still hammering.

Drelset lay back down and put an arm over his face.

“Anyone else?” he asked.

Nobody answered.

Hesh brought water for washing. He had acquired enough of their names to use them with enthusiasm, usually at the wrong moment. He said Aevra’s name while she was trying to take off a stocking adhered to a scrape, and Baerun’s while Kouven was persuading the hauler to let him see the wound. Vorrin said something in their own language. Hesh became quieter for several minutes.

They had been given the drying room.

Uivren explained this over breakfast, which resembled the previous meal but had something sour spooned over it. The household used the room to prepare cloth and light material for storage. Their arrival meant the work had been moved elsewhere, some of it to less suitable space. The platform was not ordinarily a bed. The layers beneath them were drying mats.

“How long can we stay?” Thaereth asked.

Uivren spoke to Hesh. The answer was longer than Aevra expected. Vorrin interrupted twice.

“Until the leg can move,” Uivren said at last. “Not until all of you decide you like sleeping.”

Baerun looked at his bowl. “I could learn to like it.”

Vorrin understood something of his tone and gave him more food.

Kouven wanted clean water and light. They had water in abundance, but obtaining a light he could use for detailed work proved more difficult. The vessel in the room cast everything in a dim color that concealed the distinction between blood, bruising and dirt. Ildret asked for her oil lamp. Vorrin refused.

“Outside, then,” Ildret said.

The hosts conferred. Hesh took them to a paved washing recess beside the stream, where bare stone surrounded a shallow basin. There, with a vessel of water ready and nothing fibrous nearby, Ildret was allowed one small flame under supervision.

Kouven worked on Baerun’s leg while Aevra held the light.

The swelling had begun to ease. The wound remained ugly, its edges red, but the heat was less than it had been in the passages. Kouven removed the old cloth gently, soaking the places that stuck. Baerun stared past him at water falling from a shaped spout.

“Can you stitch it?” he asked.

“Not now.”

“Why?”

“Because I want it clean before I close anything.”

“It looks clean.”

“It looks better.”

Aevra had heard enough to understand the distinction without welcoming it. She held the lamp steady. Hesh watched from the entrance, arms folded close to his body. When Kouven asked for another cloth, Hesh supplied one cut to the same size as the first.

Afterwards they washed the traveling clothes.

Vorrin had sorted the garments into separate piles according to a judgment none of the Aeler understood. Wool could be rinsed in one basin. Leather had to remain elsewhere. The cloth that had wrapped Drevik’s lamp case was taken away and returned in a small sealed pot because the residual oil was unwelcome near the drying beds.

Drevik looked stricken when he saw the empty case. Vorrin took him to the pot and made him open it himself.

Aevra had lost her spare shirt. Hesh brought her a sleeveless garment with a broad overlap down the front. Its fabric felt strange, faintly springy under the fingers, and it hung on her shoulders differently from anything she had worn. She could not find the fastening until Vorrin showed her the two flat catches concealed beneath a fold.

“Thank you,” Aevra said.

Vorrin nodded and pointed at Aevra’s dirty coat.

The washing took hours. By then Aevra had begun to enjoy it. Warm water entered the bowl without having to be carried far. Dirt moved out of a seam under her thumb. A thing could be made clean and then left clean while she did the next thing.

She put Drevik to rinsing and told Drelset to take the finished pieces to the hanging frame.

Vorrin stopped them both.

The frame was carrying material that could not have wet wool laid above it. Droplets had already fallen on one folded length. Vorrin lifted it, spoke sharply and took it away.

Aevra followed with the offending shirt in her hands.

“I’m sorry. I thought—”

Vorrin pointed to a line lower down, above a stone gutter. The difference was visible once someone showed it to her. Aevra hung the shirt there and went back to the bowl.

She did not assign anyone another task until Vorrin had shown them where things belonged.

Uivren spent much of the day away. When she returned she had a repaired strap and news of routes, most of which meant nothing to the company. The high dry passage toward the upper works had lost a stretch of its floor. A longer connection might exist through a market below. She would go there herself to replace parts of her instrument and could take them as far as that.

“Afterwards?” Thaereth asked.

“Afterwards I will know what they say there.”

“Is there anyone here who goes to Kharveth?”

“Here, no.”

Thaereth pressed her lips together. Aevra knew the expression now.

“How do they know you?” Drevik asked.

“I work the ways.”

“From here?”

“From farther down.”

She told him the name of her own settlement. He asked her to repeat it and wrote an approximation on a scrap. Uivren watched him, curious rather than offended. When he read it back, she laughed.

Aevra looked at her for a moment. It was the first time she had heard the woman laugh without irritation in it. Uivren took the scrap and drew the stresses in places Drevik had not expected.

The hosts ate separately that evening, at first. Hesh brought food to the drying room, then returned to the household’s inner space. Voices rose there, the low humming quality becoming sharper as Vorrin spoke. Aevra could not understand the words. She could hear that one person wanted something done now and the other believed something else had already been promised.

Drevik, sitting near the doorway, quietly moved his bowl farther inside.

Later Hesh came back with a flat tray of small objects. He set it down between Drelset and Baerun and demonstrated a game using pale pegs and pieces of dark stone. The board had shallow cups joined by scratches. Drelset learned quickly and began losing with great confidence. Baerun watched two rounds before offering advice, which made Drelset lose faster.

Vorrin appeared, listened to their noise and took a place on the floor. She played once. Drelset was defeated so completely that even Thaereth laughed.

Aevra sat with her back against the wall panel she had thought was failing. She could feel its occasional movement as the air shifted. It still made her uneasy, but less so when the others were talking.

Hesh asked for a song.

They understood the request only after he hummed a phrase and gestured toward Drevik. Drevik looked at the company, then down at his bowl.

“Not the long one,” Aevra said.

He gave her an offended glance and began a short surface drinking song she did not know. Its rhythm was easy to follow. Baerun joined the last line of each verse, increasingly wrong. Hesh tapped two fingers against his bowl. Vorrin listened with her head tilted, the gill-like folds beneath her collar moving faintly.

Aevra thought of Maeven hearing about the river failure. Zerla would have reached someone by now, unless the upper branch had failed too. Maeven would ask what tools they had, who was hurt, how much food remained. She would be furious that Aevra’s last message had concerned the cooper’s stair.

The song ended. Someone asked for another.

Aevra remained where she was, feeling the panel move against her back. Her coat lay within reach. She left it there.

When Hesh brought more water, she accepted a cup and stayed for the next song.

Chapter 16 — A verse below

Hesh’s fastening had worn thin where its pin turned.

Drevik examined it on the low work surface outside the drying room. It secured a folded cover over a tray of fibers. Whoever had repaired it before had wrapped the weak section in fine wire, neatly enough, but the wire now caught the cloth each time the cover was opened.

He showed Hesh the snagged edge.

“Yes,” Hesh said. “Bad.”

“Old,” Drevik said. “It did its work.”

Hesh considered that distinction, then repeated old with evident pleasure.

Drevik could replace the pin with a small piece from his tool roll. The outer fitting would have to last. He sat where the green light was strongest, laid his tools on a clean cloth and began unwinding the earlier repair. Hesh watched for a while, then went to work on a tray beside him.

The settlement had a different measure of quiet from Kharveth. There were no great service bells. People called across terraces in brief phrases, sometimes answered by a struck hollow vessel or a low note held for several breaths. Water moved everywhere but rarely loudly. A soft chime came from growth along one channel whenever droplets touched it. Drevik had spent part of the morning listening for the hand responsible before realizing there was none.

Hesh hummed as he worked.

At first Drevik noticed only the recurring rise and fall. Then the middle phrase caught him. It belonged to the descent song, though the ending was stretched and the rhythm changed to fit the work of turning fibers in a shallow bowl.

He looked up.

Hesh continued, adding words Drevik did not understand. The last line returned to Aeler, accented but recognizable.

Take off your boots at the lower door.

Drevik laughed in surprise. Hesh stopped.

“I know that,” Drevik said.

He sang the first line of the upper version. Hesh listened politely, then resumed his own, beginning farther on. This time another person answered from the next terrace, a woman carrying a basket. She supplied a line in the local language and laughed when Hesh shook his head at her.

“That’s not—” Drevik began.

He stopped, remembering the old singer’s face when he had said something similar.

Hesh waited. Drevik asked him to sing it again.

They managed only pieces between them. In the version Hesh knew, the person behind the lower door told the singer that the floor had been washed, that supper was unfinished, and that there was no sense bringing the whole road in on one’s boots. Drevik recognized the comic verse. But Hesh also sang a quieter part in which the person inside named things that needed doing before anyone could leave.

“Who wants to go?” Drevik asked.

Hesh shook his head, not in refusal but because he did not understand.

“The person below. Do they want to go with the singer?”

“Go?”

Drevik pointed upward, then toward himself, trying to act the movement without making it ridiculous. He failed. Hesh laughed and repeated a phrase that meant neither yes nor no to Drevik.

Uivren passed them carrying a coil of line. He asked her to help.

She listened to the question, spoke to Hesh and shrugged.

“He says the singer hasn’t asked yet.”

“But that’s why the singer comes.”

“Then perhaps he should say.”

She went on before Drevik could pursue it. Hesh had returned to turning fibers.

Drevik bent over the fastening again.

He wanted to write the words down. He also wanted, more urgently, to finish something useful and have it work. The new pin needed to be filed smooth where it would pass beneath the cover. He did that, tested it with the pad of his thumb and fitted it through the eye.

Hesh tried it several times.

“Good.”

Drevik showed him how to lift the cover without bending the weakened side. Hesh copied the motion. Then he brought another fastening.

Drevik laughed. “How many?”

Hesh spread his hands.

There were four.

By the time the last was done, Drevik’s shoulders ached and his appetite had returned with an insistence he had not felt since leaving home. Hesh took the repaired pieces away and came back with food wrapped in a soft, warm leaf-like sheet. Drevik ate a little before asking what it was. Hesh named it. The name did not help, but the taste did: rich, savory, with a crisp edge and a soft center.

Vorrin came to inspect the fastenings. She turned one over, tested the pin and spoke to Hesh. He answered. Their exchange grew sharper. Drevik wondered whether he had damaged something.

Uivren, returning again, listened briefly.

“He told her there were two to repair,” she said.

“There were four.”

“Yes.”

Vorrin took three away and left Hesh holding the fourth.

Drevik found himself grinning. It was exactly the sort of dispute Maeven and Aevra would have over a piece of work that expanded after someone agreed to it. He did not say so. Hesh’s expression suggested that the comparison would not improve matters.

That evening the household ate on an open terrace above one of the channels. The company had been invited to join them rather than having food brought to the drying room. Baerun came slowly, using a stick Uivren had obtained for him. Kouven made him sit where he could stretch the injured leg.

There were other guests: two Mycotheres from a neighboring bed, a small dark-haired trader who spoke Aeler readily, and a young person helping Vorrin carry the bowls. Drevik could not tell the newcomer’s age. He watched how Hesh made space and treated them as young without being able to name how he knew.

Conversation moved among three languages. Uivren translated what she thought necessary and ignored the rest. Thaereth initially tried to follow every exchange. After a while she gave up and attended to the food.

Drevik asked the trader where he had learned Aeler.

“From people wanting things.”

“That’s most people.”

“Yes. Good business.”

His grin made the answer friendly. He traded lenses and light materials, sometimes with upper communities. Drevik asked whether he knew Kharveth. He knew the name but had never been there. He had once seen the sky at a foothill entrance and stayed under the overhang because the open space made him ill.

“What does it smell like?” Hesh asked through him.

“The sky?”

“The outside.”

Drevik considered. He could have said grass and rain. Those were what people said when they wanted the surface to sound pleasant. The first smell that came to him was the warm drain beside their shop on a summer morning.

“Different things,” he said. “Wet horses. Hot stone after rain. Fruit going bad behind a seller’s stall. Smoke when everyone lights fires at once.”

The trader translated. Hesh looked pleased.

“And flowers,” Drevik added.

“Food?”

“Some. Mostly smell.”

“Then why grow them?”

He laughed. “For the smell.”

The young helper touched the edge of a pale growth in a bowl beside them and asked something. Hesh answered. The trader said, “We have that too.”

Drevik leaned nearer. The growth smelled faintly sweet, with a sharper note underneath. He had passed it all afternoon without knowing it had been placed there for pleasure.

Later, when the bowls were nearly empty, the trader asked about Drevik’s work. He explained the memorial objects, the small repairs, the names fitted onto metal. The explanation became easier when he took a thin plate from his tool roll and showed how he marked a line without cutting it too deeply.

Hesh brought a piece of scrap and asked for his own name.

Drevik hesitated. “I don’t know how you write it.”

“In yours,” the trader said.

So he made the approximation, saying it aloud until Hesh accepted the sound. Hesh traced the marks with one broad finger. The young helper wanted theirs next. Drevik could not make out the second part and had to ask repeatedly. Nobody hurried him.

Across the terrace Drelset was trying to teach the stone game to Kouven, despite not having understood it himself. Vorrin watched with increasing impatience until she moved one piece and ended the round. Baerun laughed so hard that he had to put down his bowl.

Drevik stayed after the others began going back to bed.

The terrace lights were being lowered. A figure crossed the bridge below carrying two covered vessels. Hesh began humming again, the same song, and Drevik joined where he knew the tune. Their words did not match. For a few lines the difference did not matter to the singing.

Then Vorrin called from inside, and Hesh broke off to carry the bowls.

Drevik helped him. They stacked the clean ones upside down on a slatted shelf, leaving space between them as Vorrin showed. When he returned to the drying room, Aevra was asleep with her hands loose beside her face. He took off his boots quietly and set them beneath the platform.

Chapter 17 — Water for the beds

The basket was meant to be carried on the head.

Aevra had understood this. Hesh had demonstrated it twice, settling the woven ring above the shelves of his brow and lifting the basket onto it with no apparent effort. What she had not understood was how to keep it there while looking down at her feet. Each time she looked, the weight shifted. Each time the weight shifted, she caught it with both hands and stopped.

Behind her, someone laughed. Hesh turned and waited.

“I’m coming.”

He pointed ahead, then touched the side of his head. She kept her chin up. The walkway descended between low walls padded with growth, the edges difficult to distinguish in the green light. She had walked narrower things carrying heavier loads. None had required her to look away from where she intended to put her foot.

At the next landing she lowered the basket and put one arm through each of its carrying loops. They were too short to be comfortable. They worked.

Hesh considered her, lifted his own basket onto his head, and went on.

It was their third morning in the settlement. The scrape on Aevra’s knee had formed a hard edge which caught whenever she bent. Baerun had walked the length of the drying room before breakfast and announced that the floor needed leveling. Kouven had made him walk back before answering. They would leave after two more nights’ rest if the leg continued to improve.

Vorrin had already begun putting trays back into the room.

They passed under a low arch where water shone on the underside of the stone. Beyond it, the cultivated shelves opened out. Aevra had seen their lights from above, but the beds themselves had been hidden by the retaining walls. There were pale fans as wide as open hands, cushions of rust-colored filaments, narrow stalks with dark heads. A group of workers moved between them carrying shallow baskets. One knelt with a small hook and lifted out growth which, to Aevra, looked exactly like the growth being left.

Hesh called to the worker. The answer was brief and apparently unfavorable. He bent to look, said something more quietly, and continued.

Their baskets held crumbly material with a warm, sour smell. Aevra had been warned not to put her hands in it. It had come from a covered pit below the washing place, where Hesh mixed refuse from the household with the chopped remains of something brought in boats. Nothing in the task required her to know more. She was grateful for that until the basket brushed the sore place on her knee.

At a junction Hesh set his load down. A channel beside the path had been stopped with a flat board. Above it water spilled over a side lip and ran across the walking surface, darkening the feet of the growth. Below it the bed was dry.

Hesh put a hand to the wall and made a noise she had begun to recognize as irritation.

Someone answered from the far side. A slender Mycothere rose from behind the retaining stones, wiping both hands on a hanging cloth. The two spoke across the channel. Hesh pointed toward the water, and the other pointed toward a gap under the path. Their voices rose, then settled into a rhythm Aevra knew from people discussing work neither wanted to do again.

She put down her basket.

The channel lining had lifted at one corner. A root or cord lay beneath it, swollen and pale. Water entered the crack, washed out fine material, and escaped below the path. Someone had packed the underside with loose chips. They had fallen away.

“Don’t stand there,” she said as Hesh stepped toward the edge.

He stopped at her tone. She moved him back with a hand held low, then knelt where the paving remained dry.

The other worker watched closely.

“I could lift this,” Aevra said, touching the sound end of the lining. “But I don’t know what that is.” She pointed at the pale cord.

Hesh shook his head before she had finished. He touched the cord with two fingers, then pointed toward the beds farther down. The other worker said a word and repeated it more slowly.

Aevra sat back on her heels.

She could have cut it without noticing what she had cut. It looked like something which had grown where it was not wanted. Hesh went to fetch another board while the other worker crouched beside her and showed, by pointing along its course, where the cord entered the beds. It lay in a separate shallow groove. The displaced stone had pinched it against the channel.

They waited for the upstream water to stop. It took longer than she expected. Aevra watched the flow diminish to a thread, then felt a sudden sting in the scrape on her knee and stood to move away from the wet edge.

By the time Hesh returned, Uivren had appeared with her repaired instrument bag.

“She is asking whether you need the whole bed empty,” Uivren said.

“No. That length. And someone who knows where to put this.”

Uivren translated. The worker nodded and began moving the loose chips.

“Her name?” Aevra asked.

“Seth.”

Aevra tried it. Seth corrected the last sound, then handed her a broad wooden wedge.

There was a flat iron tool at the household above the beds. It was blunt enough that Aevra would have put it aside at home. Here she cleaned its edge on a piece of gritstone and used it to ease the lining up. Seth drew the swollen cord out of the pinch without pulling it taut. Hesh supported the far end on a block. The whole piece rocked in their hands, heavier than its thin edge had suggested.

“Down,” Aevra said. “Here. Slowly.”

Uivren did not need to translate that.

The trouble was beneath it: a pocket where the bedding had washed out, deeper toward the retaining wall. Aevra lay on her side and reached in with the tool. The back was firm. She could patch the pocket and give the lining a bearing without disturbing the wall.

She could not mend the entire channel. Three more lengths had sunk above them, and one was cracked right through. Seth looked where she pointed and nodded as though she had been pointing there for weeks.

They worked until Aevra’s hands were coated to the wrists. The material Seth brought was fibrous and resisted the trowel. Aevra began to add water, then stopped at Seth’s sharp protest. Seth took a little from the bucket, pressed it flat on a board, and waited. It softened by itself.

“All right,” Aevra said. “Show me.”

By the time the lining was back, Hesh had fetched food. They ate seated on the dry end of the walkway. Seth’s household was two terraces below; a small figure there kept appearing in a doorway and calling up. Seth answered once, twice, then called something which made Hesh laugh. The figure withdrew.

“What?” Aevra asked.

Uivren chewed before answering. “The pan is in the same place as yesterday.”

Aevra laughed too. Her younger sister had once come to the workshop to ask where Maeven kept the spare lamp oil. She had passed the oil shelf on her way out of the house.

At the meal’s end Hesh and Seth began another discussion, quieter than before. Uivren listened with her eyes on the channel.

“Are we waiting for the patch?” Aevra asked.

“Among other things.”

Aevra waited.

“The boats are late. Material for the beds. Three should have come. One came. It had passengers instead.”

“From above?”

“Along the river. Vouthra.”

The name meant nothing to Aevra. “Is that where we go?”

“After the market, perhaps. There are stairs from those reaches.”

“To Kharveth?”

Uivren turned her head. “I have not walked them.”

Aevra looked away first. Seth had begun cleaning the iron tool. Aevra held out her hand for it and finished the job while they waited.

When the board was raised, the first water carried grit. It darkened the patch and found the channel’s slope. A thread escaped at the edge, stopped, then began again. Aevra asked for the board to go down. Hesh groaned. She ignored him and lifted the corner once more.

The second attempt held.

Seth watched it longer than anyone. Then she touched Aevra’s wrist with the back of one dry finger and went down to her household. Hesh lifted the abandoned baskets. Aevra followed with hers hanging from her arms.

By the time they had emptied them where Hesh wanted, the strange light had dimmed along the far terraces. Her shoulders ached. She had not asked whether the basket work still needed doing after the repair, and Hesh had not offered to excuse her.

At the drying room, Baerun sat on the platform with his foot on a folded cloth. Beside him lay a new sole cut from layered fiber, with long ties threaded through its sides. Vorrin was showing Kouven how to fasten it without drawing the upper edge over the wound.

“Look at that,” Baerun said. “I’ll be fashionable.”

“You’ll be able to put your foot down,” said Kouven.

“A second advantage.”

The sole bent in a way leather did not. It gave under Aevra’s thumb, then slowly rose. Vorrin had made it broad enough for the swelling. Baerun tried to thank her in words he had practiced with Hesh. She answered at length. He looked at Uivren.

“She says don’t soak it.”

“I said more than that.”

“You did.”

Vorrin took his foot and demonstrated the fastening again.

That evening they spread their remaining food on a clean cloth. It looked worse arranged in portions. Kouven wanted enough that no one would begin skimping on Baerun’s meals. Ildret wanted weight left for lamp supplies. Thaereth had money which might or might not be useful at the market. Hesh, summoned to look, prodded one of their remaining hard cakes, broke off a corner, tasted it, and offered it to Vorrin.

She declined.

Their provisions arrived in several parcels the following day. Hesh brought dense flat cakes and narrow strips of something dried until it snapped. Seth sent a wrapped slab which had to be kept apart from the dry food. Two other households contributed packets in exchange for Drevik’s repairs. It took most of the morning to establish which things could be eaten without preparation and which emphatically could not.

Ildret made marks on the wrappers. She asked to see each one prepared. Hesh grew impatient halfway through, then brought a pot and did it. Nobody complained while they ate the result.

Aevra’s replacement cover was smaller than the one she had lost and smelled of the drying room. There was also a woven carrying bag, patched at one corner. She did not know who had owned it. She thanked Vorrin, who pulled it open to show her that the side seam would not bear a hard edge unless the edge was wrapped.

Drelset traded his spare buckle for two short lengths of good cord and a hammer with a narrow head. Their iron dog and the short line had been left at the drake’s step; the last paired timbers stood under Uivren’s cache. Aevra handled the new hammer, feeling how the head sat. It would do for dressing small stone. It would not shift a slab.

On the last night Baerun walked to the washing place with only his stick. Kouven followed at a distance close enough to be insulting. Aevra saw them returning, arguing, and let herself imagine the road to the staffed station: a lamp above a door, a place to sit while someone else made the meal, news sent ahead in a hand Maeven would recognize.

Then she rolled the borrowed cover and tied it onto the patched bag.

They left in the next light cycle. Vorrin had trays waiting outside the room before the last pack was lifted. Hesh came to the lower path carrying a bowl of warm food which he seemed to have remembered too late. They passed it between them, standing, because he insisted.

Drevik tried the household’s farewell and got a word wrong. Hesh corrected him without letting go of his hand.

Aevra was last through the little arch. Looking back, she saw Vorrin pulling one end of the sleeping platform away from the wall. Hesh was still waving.

“Go and help her,” Aevra called.

He looked over his shoulder, lifted both hands, and disappeared inside.

Chapter 18 — The market in the height

Drevik saw the underside first.

Lights hung from a web of black beams overhead. Between them moved other lights, crossing and recrossing without any surface beneath that he could distinguish. A drop of water struck his cheek. He looked up and saw, far above him, the soles of someone’s feet through an open floor.

“Keep walking,” Uivren said.

The path had narrowed. Behind him Baerun was breathing heavily, and he moved aside to let the others pass. Aevra waited without appearing to wait. He wished she would go on.

“How high?” he asked.

“Which bit?”

He pointed toward the nearest cluster of lamps.

“High enough.” She put a hand against a support and looked along it. “This is sound.”

That did not help him look down.

The river filled the bottom of the cavern, black where no light fell. A boat passed through a strip of brightness beneath the walks. Drevik could see three figures in it and a long pale bundle lying across the middle. Beyond that brightness the boat vanished, then emerged again beneath another lamp. He had the sensation that it ought to fall between the lit places.

Their road reached a stair hung against the wall. Each tread was a thick slab held in a cradle of metal straps. The straps disappeared into sockets higher up; water had left pale runs beneath them. Aevra looked at the sockets. Drelset pressed one strap with his palm. Thaereth was already helping Baerun onto the first step.

Drevik followed with his eyes on the back of Ildret’s coat.

They had slept once since leaving Hesh’s household, in a recess Uivren shared with two people traveling the other way. One had snored in a whistling register Drevik had never heard from a person. The other had left before they woke, taking the little cooking pot which all of them had used. Breakfast had been cold. Baerun had asked, quite seriously, whether they could go back and marry into Hesh’s family.

Now he stopped twice on the stair. No one hurried him. A figure descending with two baskets waited on a wider tread and began talking to Uivren. Their conversation continued around Baerun’s breathing. Drevik heard a place name repeated, then a short emphatic refusal.

At the top the market opened sideways into the rock.

There was solid floor beneath the first stalls. He stood on it a little longer than necessary, looking at folded cloth, jars in padded racks, hooks, handles, an enormous bowl filled with small gray shells. Beyond the stalls the walks crossed open space. They were wider than they had seemed below, with rails on both sides and things hung from the rails: drying nets, strips of hide, cages containing a dim flutter. Someone was frying food. Someone else was hammering, three strokes followed by a pause, three more. A child sat on a counter solemnly pulling the fibers out of a brush while an adult argued with a customer.

“First a place for the leg,” Kouven said.

They found one through Uivren: a room cut into the wall behind a stall selling dried food. Its doorway had a curtain rather than a door, and the floor sloped toward a drain. Baerun lowered himself onto a bench and shut his eyes. The keeper spoke Aeler well enough to quote a price without help.

“For all?” Thaereth asked.

“For the room.”

“How many nights?”

“One.”

She looked toward Uivren, who had taken out a small purse and was counting her own payment.

Thaereth produced her commission. The keeper glanced at the folded document, waited while she opened it, and looked at it again.

“Who pays me?” he asked.

“We do. I am establishing—”

“The room is that price.”

Drevik busied himself with a pack strap. Thaereth put the document away and paid from the small reserve she had kept in a stitched inner pocket.

Outside, she asked Uivren where messages for the upper roads were taken.

“Ask the boat people. Ask each one.”

“There’s no office?”

“There is a tally place for freight.”

“Then I’ll begin there.”

She went with Aevra, leaving Drevik to buy what Kouven needed. It was a short list, repeated twice: clean cloth, something to protect the spare dressings from damp, salt if it was food salt, and no salve merely because the seller said it cured everything.

“I wasn’t going to.”

“You look hopeful when people speak to you.”

“I’ll look miserable.”

“Try listening.”

Ildret went with him. She wanted light supplies and did not intend to buy them untested.

The first cloth seller offered a fabric which shone when stretched. Drevik rubbed it between finger and thumb and asked for water. The seller hesitated, then passed him a cup. A drop sat on the weave without sinking in.

“For wrapping,” he said. “Not for the leg.”

The seller nodded and brought out another roll.

This one had been washed. It smelled faintly of smoke and had an uneven edge. Drevik asked how it was finished, found that the seller did not know the Aeler word he used, and showed by unpicking a thread. They spent several minutes establishing that the cloth would not shed fibers into the wound. Ildret waited patiently until the price discussion began, then wandered away.

He bought the second cloth, a little of the first for an outer cover, and two narrow ties. He refused a roll which had been stored against the wet wall. The seller shrugged and cut from another.

When he found Ildret, she had taken a lamp apart on somebody’s counter.

Its light came from a stiff, flat growth held between plates. A sliding cover exposed more or less of the surface. The seller demonstrated the catch; Ildret operated it with one hand, then with her gloves on. Drevik watched her press the edges, checking for places which would catch on cloth.

“How long?” she asked.

The answer involved a gesture toward the river and a phrase neither understood. Uivren, returning with a lens wrapped in soft leather, translated it as three market intervals if kept moist and shut.

“How long is a market interval?” Ildret asked.

Uivren smiled. “Ask when the next boat comes.”

“I need a time.”

“Then keep your flame as well.”

They bought two small lights and a sealed packet for feeding them. Ildret marked the covers with her own knife. She had the seller show her the right amount of moisture twice, then closed them and put them in different packs.

Drevik bought a paper twist of the fried food. The pieces were hot enough to hurt his fingers, crisp outside and soft in the middle. Ildret tried one, frowned, and took another.

They were halfway across a walk when the crowd moved against the rail.

Drevik stopped with a piece still in his mouth.

The drake came round a stall in a low deliberate gait, its belly swinging close to the boards. A pale ridge ran from its head to its shoulders. Its handler walked beside it holding a short stiff pole attached to a collar. Two panniers hung clear of its legs. The animal paused at a smell, lifting the front of its body, and the handler tapped the boards twice.

It lowered itself and continued.

Drevik had backed against Ildret. She put a hand on his elbow.

The handler said something sharply. Drevik thought it was meant for the drake until she pointed at the food in his hand.

He folded the paper closed.

She waited. He put it inside his coat.

The drake passed near enough that he could see scratches on the collar and a glossy rubbed patch beneath it. One pannier held covered pots. A pot shifted; the animal twitched; the handler stopped immediately and reset the load before allowing it to move on. At the far end of the walk she put herself between its head and the child sitting on the counter.

Drevik did not eat any more until they had reached solid floor.

“I thought—” he began.

“So did I,” said Ildret.

She took the paper twist from him and opened it. The remaining pieces had gone soft.

When they returned, Baerun was asleep. Kouven inspected the cloth and accepted it with a nod which gave Drevik an unreasonable amount of pleasure. Thaereth was still out. Aevra sat beside the curtain rubbing grit from her hands.

“Any luck?” he asked.

“People know upper roads. Different upper roads.”

“Where is she?”

“Arguing with a freight tally.”

He put his purchases down. The room smelled of damp packs and the meal being cooked in front. From outside came the repeated hammering. He found himself fitting it to the song from Hesh’s terrace, then stopped before anyone heard.

A person stood at the curtain.

Drevik had not heard an approach. The newcomer was taller than Uivren, lean through the shoulders, with long fingers resting on the curtain edge. Their skin held a gray undertone; the dark hair was bound behind the head with a narrow band. Drevik recognized a Trow from the face and the eyes, though he had met only one before, years ago, at a surface fair. That person had sold carved pieces for board games and disliked being asked where they slept.

This one’s coat was good and badly mended.

“Who wants the upper stair?” the newcomer asked.

Aevra looked up. “Which stair?”

“The one you are asking about.”

“We’re asking about several.”

“Then perhaps one of them.”

Drevik felt Aevra’s patience shorten. He stepped nearer the curtain.

“Do you know a road to Kharveth?”

“I know part of a road. I know a stair with a mark divided in the middle. I know a turning where the wall is warm, and a place beyond it where the sound comes down before the people.”

Aevra stood.

“Have you walked it?”

“No.”

The answer came easily. Drevik distrusted it less for that, then distrusted himself.

“How do you know it?” he asked.

“I carry it.”

The Trow tapped two fingers against the side of their head. It looked like pointing to a pocket.

“Ausk,” they said. “Ask for Ausk when you have decided whether you want to listen.”

“What would it cost?” Aevra asked.

“A place with you when you go.”

“We don’t know where we’re going.”

“You are going away.”

Ausk glanced toward Baerun, asleep with his leg supported, then at the packs. Their eyes paused on Drevik’s separate case. He put his hand over its fastening without intending to.

“I have work until the lower bell,” Ausk said. “At the rope tables.”

They let the curtain fall.

Drevik and Aevra listened to the footsteps diminish. Then Kouven said, from beside Baerun, “If either of you knows what that meant, you might tell the rest of us.”

Outside, the hammering stopped. In its absence Drevik could hear the river moving under the floor.

Thaereth came back carrying a scrap of wood covered in names. She had found two people who might carry a message, neither leaving toward a place she knew. She had paid nothing yet.

When they told her about Ausk, she sat down and took off one boot.

“A recollection?” she asked.

“So they said,” Aevra answered.

“Whose?”

“We haven’t asked.”

Thaereth rubbed her heel. The skin had begun to blister. Drevik had not seen her limp.

“Then we should.”

Drevik went out to find the rope tables before the bell sounded. He took the cloth wrapping Kouven had rejected as too small for a dressing; he intended to buy more of the same. On the walk he stopped at a gap between stalls and looked down deliberately.

A boat lay against the landing. Its pale bundle was being lifted by two people, carefully, while a third held the bow clear of the wall. A rolled sleeping mat.

Above him somebody emptied a bucket through a pipe. Water splashed on the river and spread a bright broken circle around the boat.

He crossed the next walk without touching the rail until someone coming the other way needed room.

Chapter 19 — A memory’s owner

Ausk was winding cord onto a board when Aevra found them. Their left hand held the board still; the right drew the cord from a loose coil on the floor and laid it in close, even turns. They watched a customer arguing at the next table without once crossing a turn.

Aevra waited. Drevik stood beside her with a parcel of cloth under his arm. Thaereth had remained at the lodging to write the messages she hoped to send.

“The end,” Ausk said, nodding toward the coil.

Aevra picked it up and felt for the little knot securing it. Once it was free, the last turns ran through her fingers. Ausk tucked the end beneath a loop, turned the board over, and put it on a stack.

“Now I can go.”

They spoke to the table’s owner, received a small packet and a flat metal token, and led Aevra and Drevik down a narrow stair. Halfway down, Ausk stopped to let a laden worker pass. The worker touched their shoulder and said something. Ausk answered without smiling. After the worker had gone, they continued.

Below the rope tables a landing held two benches and a water jar. The landing’s outer edge looked down on the ferry stages. Voices rose clearly from below. Aevra could hear a person counting, losing count, and beginning again.

Ausk poured water into a cup, drank, and offered it. Drevik accepted. Aevra did not want a drink but took it because she disliked the appearance of refusing.

“Whose memory?” she asked.

“An Aeler called Oskel.”

“Is he alive?”

“I don’t know.”

“When did you last see him?”

“I never saw him.”

Drevik leaned forward. Ausk set the cup down between them.

“It came to me from Sere. Oskel gave it to Sere. I was present when she gave it to me. I was not present when he gave it to her.”

“Why did he?” Aevra asked.

“He needed a debt paid.”

“For a memory of a stair?”

“For more than the stair. I don’t carry all of it.”

Aevra looked at the water jar. Its rim had been mended with small metal staples. She followed the line of them to the handle and back.

“What happened to him when he gave it?”

“I was not there.”

“What usually happens?”

Ausk waited until she looked at them.

“You will have to be more particular.”

“Did he forget it?”

“Some people do. Some retain the account and lose the occasion. Some find what they gave up in another recollection later. I have only Sere’s word for what he surrendered.”

Drevik shifted beside her. She knew he wanted to ask about the road.

“And Sere?”

“Dead.”

“I’m sorry.”

“So am I.”

Ausk said it quietly enough that Aevra regretted having made it a courtesy.

Below them the counter reached a number which apparently satisfied everyone. A load scraped over boards. Ausk rubbed the pad of one finger against their thumb.

“She used to mend nets there,” they said, looking down. “The table is gone now.”

No one spoke for a little while.

Then Drevik asked, “Could we see the stair?”

“You can hear what I know of it.”

“That isn’t what I meant.”

“I know.”

Ausk took the empty cup and turned it until the handle faced away. Their gaze settled a little short of the jar. Aevra had seen people remembering: the search, the pause, the movement of the eyes. This did not resemble it. Ausk’s shoulders shifted as if they bore a weight which had just been put there.

“The right boot rubs beneath the little toe,” they said.

Drevik went still.

“There is a smell of heated iron. Someone has put a hand on the wall. Not mine. A child is asking to be lifted, but we cannot see the child. There are six steps to the turning.”

Ausk’s fingers tightened around the cup.

“The mark is above the head. Two pieces. One on either side of the stone’s seam. I am thinking that the man who cut it should have found a better stone. I am tired and I want to be done.”

The last words came differently, lower, with an impatience Aevra had not heard from Ausk. They closed their eyes.

“A bundle strikes my knee. The sound is ahead. Metal against metal. I know who is making it.”

“Who?” Drevik asked.

Ausk opened their eyes. “I don’t have that.”

They released the cup. It rocked once against the stone bench and Drevik caught it.

Aevra’s hands had gone cold. She had understood the offered memory as something like an extraordinarily exact account. The rubbed toe made her want to remove her own boot.

“Was that the end?” she asked.

“Of what I can follow together.”

“You said warm stone.”

“The left wall, above the turning. Warm enough to feel through the sleeve. The stair continues.”

“Up?” Drevik asked.

“Yes.”

“Does it reach the upper roads?”

“Someone called it that. Whether it meant your roads, I don’t know.”

Aevra took out a piece of chalk. Ausk drew the mark on a flat section of wall where other people had left tallies.

She had trusted the rubbed toe before the mark. Now she made herself separate the two. A person could remember pain exactly and put the turn on the wrong side; she had done it herself. Her body had nevertheless accepted the stair as something already walked, and would not give up that ease because she had caught it happening. The mark was made in two unequal halves around a central line, with a shallow hook below. Aevra knew the form. Not its particular place or reign, but the old arrangement: two authorities shown together, their signs left distinct.

Drevik had taken out his folded copy of the family drawing.

“Not the same,” he said after looking.

“No,” Aevra agreed.

“But the division.”

“Yes.”

Ausk watched them compare the marks. They did not supply encouragement.

“How old?” Aevra asked.

“Sere had carried it before I knew her. Oskel was not young when she met him, she said. He had walked the stair when his child could still be carried.”

“Decades.”

“Possibly.”

“Possibly more.”

“Yes.”

Drevik refolded the drawing with care. Aevra could see his disappointment. It made him look younger, and she resented Ausk briefly for having put hope in his face. Then she remembered that Drevik had arrived here carrying a lamp made to replace one none of them had ever seen.

“Where is it?” she asked.

“Near Vouthra. I know the approach from here as far as the river forks. I have been to the lower stages. There are people there who may know which ascent he meant.”

“Why haven’t you asked them?”

“I have work here. I had work here.”

Ausk turned their token over in their palm.

“The west walk is shut. Two traders have moved their tables. The boat which takes my work downriver has not come in three intervals. I can still eat here, for a while. I would rather leave while I have something to offer.”

“Can you walk?”

“Yes.”

“Carry your own things?”

“Yes.”

“We have someone hurt.”

“I saw.”

“We may be slow.”

“So will everyone leaving by the same boats.”

Aevra looked toward the stages. The loading crew had sat down. One person lay flat with an arm over their face. The river under them seemed almost still.

“I can’t promise you a place above,” she said.

“I haven’t asked you for one yet.”

That was fair. She did not like it.

They agreed to meet at the lodging after Ausk had collected their things. No payment for a road which might not exist. Food shared while they traveled together, work shared where possible. If they reached the stair, they would look at it before anyone went ahead. Ausk repeated that they had never walked it. Drevik said he had heard.

On the way up, he stopped her.

“You kept asking about the man.”

“I wanted to know.”

“So do I. But if the road is there—”

“I didn’t say we couldn’t use it.”

“I know.” He pressed the parcel under his arm more firmly. “I keep thinking of my father waiting for a letter which says we’re at the station. He’ll count from the wrong day.”

Aevra had no useful answer. Maeven would count from the right one.

They climbed the rest of the stair together.

Thaereth read the messages aloud before folding them. One named everyone still together and described Zerla’s separation without announcing either her death or her safe arrival. The other was for the staffed station, asking that no lightly equipped party be sent down the failed control approach.

“Put that the floor above the wheel is gone,” Aevra said. “Not merely the bridge.”

Thaereth added it.

Drevik wrote to his father on a narrow scrap bought at the tally place. He began twice, cut the first scrap down, and used the back for the address. Aevra wrote Maeven’s name and sat for a long time before adding anything else.

She wanted to say that the people here had fed them. That Baerun was improving. That she had lost the cover Maeven disliked and would need another, which meant she would come home to complain about the price. She wrote the first two things. Then she wrote, I would like to be in the kitchen with you.

There was still room below it. She folded the paper.

Kouven was changing Baerun’s dressing when Ausk arrived. They carried a long narrow bag and a rolled cover. The bag had been tied shut with several mismatched lengths of cord. Baerun opened one eye and asked whether they played the stone game. Ausk said they knew three games with stones and disliked two of them.

“That’s promising,” he said.

Uivren returned late from the stages. She had found Ivrassa, a ferry keeper she knew, and secured space to speak to her in the morning. She had not secured passage.

“Keep everything packed,” she said. “She may leave. She may not.”

“Toward the stair?” Drevik asked.

“Toward Vouthra.”

Ausk looked up at that. “Is the cool reach open?”

Uivren shook her head.

Neither explained immediately. Aevra waited until Uivren had taken off her bag.

“What closes it?”

“Wyrms in the shallows. Moving together. Ivrassa turned back from them yesterday.”

Drelset set down the cup he had been holding.

“How many?”

“She did not stay to count.”

They slept with their packs beside them. Somewhere beyond the curtain an argument continued, the same two voices returning to the same point until one of them began laughing. Aevra lay awake through the laughter and the quieter conversation which followed. Across the room Ausk turned in their cover, muttered a word she did not know, and settled again.

She could feel the little toe of her right foot against the inside of her boot, though the boot stood beside her head.

Chapter 20 — The ferry keeper’s kin

Ivrassa had a mouthful of pegs when Uivren introduced them.

She took out two, fitted them through a joint in the boat’s side, and held up a finger. Aevra waited. The ferry keeper leaned her weight against the joint, listened, then spat the remaining pegs into her palm.

“Now,” she said.

She was Lethai-ar, narrow through the face and shoulders, her pointed ears bare above dark hair tied so tightly that the skin beside her eyes drew back. Her coat had been shortened above the knees. Beneath it her trousers were patched in a different cloth at each shin. She spoke Aeler without difficulty, though the vowels fell in places Aevra did not expect.

Uivren made the introductions again. Ivrassa looked over the company, resting longest on Baerun’s tied shoe.

“Can he step down?”

“With help,” Kouven said.

“Can he sit low?”

“Yes.”

“Then he sits forward of the cargo. No one steps over that leg.”

Aevra let out a breath.

“We haven’t agreed a price,” Ivrassa added.

Thaereth came nearer. They discussed the price, the weight of the packs, and the cost of waiting if the river did not permit departure. Ausk stood slightly apart and said they could pay for their own passage. Ivrassa counted again, this time including them.

“Nine.”

“Seven Aeler,” Thaereth said, “Uivren and Ausk.”

“I have eyes.”

Drelset bent to hide a smile. Thaereth did not look at him.

The ferry was broader than Aevra had expected, with a low center and raised ends. Its sides showed repairs in three kinds of timber and one material she could not identify. A long steering blade lay on the landing beside a coil of line. Two other boats were tied beyond it. One had a roof over the middle; from beneath the roof came the dry cough of someone waking.

Aevra crouched to look at the pegged joint. Ivrassa watched her.

“Will it hold?” she asked.

“Yes,” Aevra said. “If the wood behind it is sound.”

“It is.”

“Then yes.”

Ivrassa returned to her work.

While they waited, Drevik brought out the drawing. He had protected it between two flat pieces of board. Aevra recognized the care with which he approached Ivrassa: not interrupting until she had finished a task, holding the paper where she could see without taking it from her work.

“We’re looking for a stair near Vouthra,” he said. “But we first came down to find something from Outhren. Do you know that name?”

Ivrassa glanced at the drawing.

“Outhren?”

“The lost kingdom.”

She looked at him then.

“What has it lost?”

Drevik’s mouth remained open a moment. Aevra felt herself lean forward.

“The old kingdom,” he said. “Above the river. With the divided mark, and the long hall—”

“There are several long halls.”

He pointed to the little outline of the lamp, then to the notation beside it. Ivrassa took the drawing at last. She studied the lettering, turned it toward the light, and asked Uivren a question in another language. Uivren answered with the name they had been hearing for days.

“Vouthra,” Ivrassa said to Drevik. “You have an old way of saying it.”

Aevra heard Baerun’s stick strike the boards behind her. He had moved closer.

“People live there?” Thaereth asked.

Ivrassa looked from one of them to another.

“My sister’s household lives there.”

No one replied.

“They have a leaking roof over their store,” she continued. “They have been asking me to bring suitable wood since before the last high water. If any of you can tell them why that has become difficult, you may save me a conversation.”

Drelset laughed once, then stopped because no one else had.

Drevik took the drawing back. His hands were steady; Aevra was watching for that.

“How long?” he asked.

“My sister? Twenty-seven years.”

“No. I’m sorry. I meant the town.”

“Longer.”

“We thought it was gone,” Aevra said.

Ivrassa’s expression changed only a little.

“You had people there?”

“Family. Long ago.”

“Then ask when we get there.”

She lifted the steering blade. Aevra moved out of her way.

For a while the company stood in a loose knot beside the packs. Thaereth was the first to move. She sat on a bollard and took out her messages. The one for Kharveth had already been sealed.

Drevik watched her turn it in her hands.

“You can’t put it in that,” he said.

“Not yet.”

“She lives there.”

“Her sister does. I heard her.”

He looked angry, then ashamed of looking angry. Aevra wanted to take him away from the others but knew he would resent that too.

“Help me with the case,” she said instead.

He crouched with her beside his pack. They checked the replica lamp’s padding. Its little dent was still there, the dent his father had made at a wedding; the seam had not opened. Drevik pressed a finger against the cloth wrapped around its neck.

“I don’t know who I’d give it to,” he said.

“We’ll find out.”

He nodded without looking up.

They did not leave that morning.

A boat came in against the current with its crew poling hard along the wall. The last length was taken by a line from the landing. Aevra and Drelset helped pull. When the boat touched, an elderly person in the bow began protesting before anyone had offered a hand.

Ivrassa answered sharply. The passenger protested again and was helped ashore, still speaking. Behind them came a woman carrying a sleeping child, a man with a cage covered by his own coat, two young people holding opposite ends of a rolled carpet, and a broad-backed traveler who would not let anyone touch the box under one arm.

The boat smelled of wet bedding.

Ivrassa spoke to its pilot away from the passengers. Uivren joined them. Aevra stayed near enough to hear if Aeler was used, but it was not. The pilot opened both hands, fingers spread, then drew them together. Ivrassa asked a short question. The answer made Uivren turn toward the river.

When they came back, Ivrassa said the cool reach was still closed.

“More of them,” Uivren told Aevra. “Along the bank. In the water.”

“Are they hunting the boats?”

“No,” Ivrassa said. “They are going somewhere. I don’t intend to put a boat where they are going.”

“The other route?” Thaereth asked.

“I am waiting for someone who used it.”

“How long?”

“Until they arrive.”

Aevra could see Thaereth preparing another question and then abandoning it. She helped carry the carpet up to a dry part of the landing.

The young people thanked her in a language she did not know. One unrolled a corner to inspect it. There were insects in the pile, or things which moved like insects. The two began picking them out carefully and dropping them into the river. Aevra went to fetch their second bundle.

By midday, if the market’s bells meant midday, the landing was crowded. The passengers who had arrived were not all traveling onwards, but some were. Others had come down from the walks with baskets and children and the distracted look of people who had packed after an argument. Ivrassa had three conversations at once and appeared to lose none of them.

Drelset offered to help her refit a loose bench.

“If you can hold that end level,” she said.

“I can do better than level.”

“Don’t.”

He held it level. Aevra, passing with a coil of rope, saw him grin.

Later he asked whether she ever came above.

“To the upper landing?”

“Higher.”

“Why?”

“The company is excellent.”

She looked at his torn sleeve and the bruising on his wrist.

“Does it arrive in better condition?”

“Sometimes.”

The pilot beside her laughed. Drelset bowed as far as the bench in his hands allowed. He did not ask again. When the bench was fitted, Ivrassa gave him a cup and sent him for water, and he went.

Aevra sat with Baerun while Kouven bought more clean water. The adapted shoe had left a shallow mark across the sound part of his foot. She loosened the tie at his direction.

“Living there,” he said.

“Yes.”

“All this time.”

“We don’t know all this time.”

He looked at her. She stopped.

“My mother’s grandmother used to keep a place for a brother,” he said. “Not Outhren. Somewhere else. I don’t remember the name. They’d set out a cup on the day they said he’d gone.”

Aevra knew several households which did that. She had put cups away after meals without looking closely at where they had stood.

“Did she ever hear?”

“No.” He moved his foot and winced. “She was dead before I was born. My mother told me.”

She retied the shoe more loosely.

At the next berth the elderly passenger had begun quarrelling with the person carrying the box. The box was apparently in the wrong place. Both sounded as though they had been having the same quarrel for years.

The boat Ivrassa waited for arrived late in the light cycle, its sides marked with a pale crust above the waterline. Aevra noticed that before she saw the people. The crew moved slowly. One had wrapped a wet cloth around the back of the neck.

Their pilot drank a whole cup of water before answering questions.

Ivrassa heard him out. Then she walked down the landing alone, past the last lamp, and stood where she could see the current against the wall. She watched for several minutes.

Drevik came to stand beside Aevra.

“Do we go?” he asked.

“She hasn’t said.”

He nodded, impatient with himself this time.

When Ivrassa returned, she began pointing out loads. The passengers moved. The company moved with them.

“Hot reach,” she said to Uivren. “We cross the open part before the next change. If the lower landing is not usable, we continue to the stone mouth. Nobody asks me to stop at a comfortable-looking bank.”

Uivren translated for Ausk and a passenger who had joined them. Ivrassa looked at Aevra.

“Your people understand?”

“I’ll tell them.”

“Tell them now.”

Aevra did. Ildret checked the new lights and secured the flame lamps. Kouven asked where the water would be stored. Ivrassa pointed beneath a seat. He crouched, tried the reach, and asked for the place beside it; Baerun could not bend that far. Thaereth made certain that all nine had been accounted for, then counted the packs separately.

Loading took another long while. The covered cage had to remain level. The carpet could be wet but the bundle of papers carried by the broad-backed traveler could not. A child objected to the place assigned and then, once moved, wanted the first place back. The parent crouched to rearrange the bundles and almost lost a shoe between the landing and the boat. Drelset caught it.

Baerun descended with Kouven ahead of him and Aevra behind. His injured foot found the boat’s floor, and for a moment all his weight came onto her hands. Then he sat. He was pale around the mouth.

“All right?” she asked.

“Give me a moment.”

She gave it to him.

Drevik handed down the lamp case before climbing in. Ausk took a place near the stern, their narrow bag under their knees. Uivren sat where she could speak to Ivrassa without shouting. Drelset passed the last packs down and took the line from the landing worker.

Aevra turned to look back at the market.

From below, its walks had looked impossibly slight. Now she could pick out the rope tables, the cloth stall, the place where the drake’s handler had stopped to settle the load. Smoke drifted along a beam. A person leaned over a rail to call something to a boat beyond theirs. Higher still, lamps moved in passages she had not noticed before.

She had never asked the market’s name.

The boat shifted beneath her. Ivrassa set the steering blade, spoke to the bowman, and waited while the last line was cast off. Drevik braced a hand on the gunwale. Aevra moved his case away from the water collecting beneath it and wedged it against her own bag.

They passed under the lowest walk. A drop fell onto the back of her neck, cold enough to make her flinch.

Ahead, the river widened. The bowman leaned into his pole, lifted it dripping, and found the bottom again. Behind them the market’s hammer began its three strokes. Aevra heard two sets before the boat carried them beyond the sound.

Chapter 21 — Across the fire

The first red light lay on the underside of a cloud.

Aevra had been watching the riverbank for some time, trying to distinguish rock from its reflection. The banks had drawn apart. Above them, vapour hung in long folds, and the light moved within those folds without reaching the water. It deepened from the color of warm copper to a dense red which made the boat’s little lamps seem white.

Ivrassa touched Uivren’s shoulder. The surveyor unfastened her collar.

“Drink,” she told them.

Kouven had already opened a bottle. He passed it to Baerun, then to Aevra. The water was warm from the boat, but she drank more than she wanted.

The river turned. A black shelf ran along the far bank, its edge sharpened against a brightness beneath it. Beyond the shelf, stone opened into a basin of moving fire.

At first she saw only the nearest part. A skin formed on the surface, darkened, and split into long red seams. Beyond that, where the basin narrowed, the skin broke continuously. Slow bulges rose and sank. Light fell upwards onto the roof and showed columns descending into the glare, their bases lost. Between two columns hung a structure like a bridge, except that its approaches entered no road she could see. Its sides were smooth and pale. Along one edge a row of small projections caught the light.

Something crossed it.

Aevra followed the movement with her head tilted back. The figure was too tall for the proportions she had first assigned it. It carried a load on one shoulder, a black shape with hooked ends, and its legs bent in a way that made her own knees tighten. It stepped off the bridge.

She caught Drelset’s sleeve.

The figure descended through the air, or through something in the air which she could not distinguish. Its foot found a place above the molten surface. The next step took it farther down. Then the glare swallowed it.

Drelset had seen. He did not say anything.

The riverbank between them and the basin was thicker than it had appeared. Channels ran through it, some empty and some glowing far below. Aevra saw repairs: a different surface laid over an older edge, a handrail interrupted and resumed at a new height. Someone used this place often enough to mend it.

The boat veered toward their own bank.

“Hands inside,” Ivrassa said. “Keep the covers on.”

Aevra pulled her cloak over the back of her neck. Sweat ran down her ribs. The parent across from her had wrapped the child’s head in a damp cloth and was keeping the cloth away from its mouth. Drevik stared at the basin until Kouven put a bottle against his hand.

The landing was a stone tongue beneath an overhang. Two other boats waited there, one of them the roofed boat from the market. A figure stood beside a square post at the outer end.

It wore a dark garment fitted closely at the wrists. Its bare feet rested on stone which shimmered with heat, and its head turned before Ivrassa called. Horns followed the line of its skull and curved behind the ears. The face beneath them was narrow and almost ordinary until it smiled.

Aevra heard a voice as clear as a person speaking beside her.

“The landing will be under the second breath.”

Ivrassa answered in another tongue. The figure listened, looked toward the basin, and lifted one finger.

“Not that one. The next.”

Ivrassa put the steering blade across the current. Her bowman caught the post with a loop and held them off while the boat behind made room.

“Why are we stopping?” Thaereth asked.

“To turn together,” Uivren said. “Quiet.”

The figure on the landing looked at Aevra. She had the unpleasant certainty that it could hear her swallow.

“You could stand farther in,” it said.

She realized that she had risen halfway from her seat. She sat down.

It seemed amused. Then a tremor passed through the light and it turned its attention away from them.

The first breath was a long subsidence in the basin. The dark skin drew toward its center, folding upon itself. A sound traveled across the water, low enough to press against Aevra’s chest. The post beside the landing rattled in its socket.

“Off,” Ivrassa said.

The bowman freed the line. The boat swung. Behind them a pole struck stone twice in quick succession. Someone shouted from the roofed boat, and Ivrassa shouted back without turning her head.

Aevra could see the second boat’s stern caught against the landing. Its pilot was trying to push clear. A broad bundle hung over the side where a passenger had left it, trapped between boat and stone.

Drelset stood.

“Sit,” Ivrassa said.

He sat. There was nothing he could have reached.

A knife flashed in the other boat. The bundle fell away. Their pilot got the stern clear just as the second breath came.

The basin rose.

Not all of it, Aevra thought, though she could not tell where the movement ended. A mound lifted through the black crust, shouldered it apart and stood. Fire slid from limbs wider than the boat. A head or crest crossed the line of the hanging bridge. For an instant there was a shape she could understand, a bent body pulling itself through an opening too small for it. Then the heat struck.

She bent over her knees. Her eyes shut of themselves. The cloth over her neck felt as if it had been laid against a hot plate.

The boat lurched. A bowl rolled under her foot. Someone cried out, a short angry sound followed by a child’s wail.

Ivrassa was still speaking. Aevra made herself look up.

The thing in the basin had put a limb against the shelf. Stone broke off its edge. A fissure opened through the landing behind them; the square post tilted and vanished into steam. The horned figure was already on higher ground, walking without haste beneath the overhang.

The roofed boat struck something. Its crew shouted. Their own boat had cleared the falling edge, but the water came at them from the side in a heavy uneven wave. Aevra seized the bench with both hands. Baerun slid against Kouven. A cage hit the floor and the covered animal inside battered against its bars.

“Down,” Ivrassa said, and this time she roared it.

Drevik lay across the lamp case. Ausk caught the child as the parent lost a grip on the wet bundle. Water came over the side and ran beneath their packs. The boat rose, fell, and turned until Aevra thought its bow must strike the bank.

The bowman drove his pole down. It bent. Ivrassa pulled against him, and the bank passed close enough for Aevra to see the individual crystals in the stone.

Then they were beyond the overhang.

The heat remained. The sound diminished slowly, not because the thing behind them had stopped moving but because the river carried them into another bend.

Aevra lifted her head. Drelset was counting under his breath. She counted too and lost her place when the child began screaming again.

Ausk handed the child back. The parent held it so tightly that Kouven had to touch an arm and speak. There was a red patch across the parent’s wrist where spray or heated stone had caught it. Kouven poured clean water over it while the child watched, suddenly silent.

They did not stop until Ivrassa found a broad gravelly bank beyond a cold tributary. The roofed boat followed at an angle, two people bailing steadily. One of its side planks had split. Its pilot had a bleeding cheek.

Everyone who could walk got out.

Aevra put her hands in the tributary and kept them there. They were red but unblistered. Beside her Drevik sat in the water up to his thighs. He appeared not to have noticed.

“Your case,” she said.

He looked down. It was on the bank beside him, wet outside. He opened it at once.

Kouven made a place for the burns. Ildret took the lamps apart to dry them. Thaereth counted passengers from both boats with the pilots, asking the names again when the answers came too quickly. The covered cage was opened a little; a small pale snout emerged and bit its owner’s finger. The owner swore with an energy which made Aevra laugh before she could stop herself.

Across the bank Drelset knelt beside the split boat. He pressed its plank back into place while Aevra inspected the seam. The damage was too long for their available patch to hold under a full load. It would float light, with someone bailing. No passengers, almost no cargo.
