Ivrassa accepted the finding. She and the other pilot rearranged the loads while the passengers watched their possessions change boats. The carpet went into theirs. The roofed boat’s covered pots were divided. A little stool was left on the bank because its owner could no longer carry it and there was nowhere to put it without blocking the bailer.

Aevra tried fitting it under the forward bench. Ivrassa shook her head.

The owner touched its seat once before stepping away.

The parent with the burned wrist could not fasten the child’s coat. Aevra offered to do it and was refused so sharply that she withdrew her hands. A moment later the parent held the coat out without looking at her.

The fastening was unfamiliar: two flat pieces sliding across each other, held by the tension of a little cord. Aevra tried it the wrong way. The child, who had watched her with solemn suspicion, put a finger against the upper piece and pushed.

“There,” Aevra said. “You know.”

The child said something to the parent. The parent answered, then said in Aeler, “She says you are too big.”

“For the coat?”

“For the catch.”

Aevra smiled. “I am.”

The burned wrist had begun to swell. Kouven had cooled it and covered it loosely; now he wanted the arm supported. The parent disliked that too, because it left only one hand for the child and the bundle. Aevra took the bundle to the boat and showed where she had put it. This was accepted.

On her way back she found Ildret kneeling over the growth lights. One cover had been forced crooked. The growth beneath it was dark along an edge, and the little feeding packet was soaked.

“Will it recover?”

“I don’t know.”

“Can you use the other packet?”

“I’ve kept it dry. I’d rather not use all of it trying to mend this one.”

They looked at the damaged light. Ildret adjusted the cover until it closed, then marked the case so she would know which one to watch. She put it in a separate pocket.

The ordinary lamps had survived. One horn panel had cracked, but the frame held it. Ildret gave Aevra the empty oil bottle and asked her to find whether any of the other passengers had spare. Aevra took it from person to person along the bank. She had to explain with gestures twice. At the third group someone opened a jar and let her smell the contents. It was not lamp oil. At the fourth, the pilot with the bleeding cheek pointed toward his damaged boat.

A small reserve flask had been lashed beneath a seat. He could spare half, for payment at the next settlement. Aevra brought Thaereth to agree the amount. The pilot did not ask to see her commission.

When they returned to Ildret, she had the cracked lamp ready. She looked at the filled bottle, touched Aevra’s arm, and went back to her work.

Aevra sat down beside a pack. Her knees hurt when she bent them. Across the bank, Drelset was helping the stool’s owner untie a cushion from the seat. They had found room for the cushion. The owner rubbed a hand over the bare wood as though apologizing to it, then followed Drelset toward the boat.

For several breaths Aevra thought she might not be able to stand. There was no injury to account for it. She had carried heavier loads for longer than this. She put both hands on the pack and waited until her legs felt like her own again.

Baerun called from the water’s edge. His stick had sunk in the mud and he did not want to lose the shoe trying to retrieve it.

She got up and went to him.

By the time they pushed off again, the light above the far bend had changed. The great body was no longer visible. Across the molten basin, dim through vapour, the bridge remained in use. A smaller shape crossed from one column to another, carrying something which shone.

Aevra watched until rock hid it. Then she helped Baerun settle his leg between the redistributed bundles. His new shoe was wet. She loosened it and held it where the moving air could reach the fibers.

Chapter 22 — The courteous creature

Drevik woke to the smell of heated resin.

They had stopped in a covered berth. A pale floor rose from the water, broad and smooth, its edge protected by thick braided fenders. Above it hung lamps in worked metal cages. Their light was amber and steady. He could hear someone sweeping.

For a moment he thought they had reached an inn.

Aevra touched his shoulder. “Bring the case.”

He followed her out of the boat. His legs were stiff. The wet cloth around the lamp had been replaced, but the case still smelled of river water, and he carried it away from his body as if that might dry it faster.

A woman sweeping the landing looked up. She had pale hair bound beneath a scarf and an apron with pockets full of small tools. Her broom made a soft regular hiss across the stone.

“Leave the muddy things there,” she said, pointing toward a rack.

Drevik obeyed. It was pleasant to be told something he could do immediately.

Beyond the landing a gallery opened into a chamber so large that its far side disappeared behind hanging cloths. The cloths moved slowly. They were embroidered, or perhaps woven with the patterns in them: pale branches, small angular flowers, long shapes which might have been birds. The floor held enough warmth to feel through his boots. Along one wall stood deep trays of polished stone, each filled with a different growth. One smelled like the resin; another carried tiny white blossoms.

He stopped beside it.

“You can touch that,” the woman said. “Not the dark one.”

He brushed a blossom with one finger. It was real. He had not expected to doubt that.

The woman went back to sweeping.

Ivrassa spoke to a man seated at a low table. He was copying marks from a board onto a roll of cloth and kept one hand on the cloth to prevent it curling. Uivren stood nearby with her instrument bag. Neither looked surprised by the room.

A sound came from behind the hangings.

Drevik thought of a heavy door rolling in its track. The cloth nearest him lifted. A limb emerged, pale-scaled and jointed, ending in long dark claws which rested carefully on the floor.

He stepped back into Aevra.

The Dragon put its head around the hanging.

Its eye was larger than Drevik’s closed fist. A gold line divided it, narrowing as it looked toward the lamps. The scales around the mouth were small, almost smooth, and beneath the jaw they overlapped in flexible rows. It brought its head down until it could look at Ivrassa without bending the long neck sideways.

“You are late,” it said.

Ivrassa answered, “The lower landing broke.”

“The one with the square post?”

“Yes.”

“That was repaired badly.”

Drevik waited for more. The Dragon turned its eye toward the passengers, then toward the damaged boat beyond them.

“Keep it outside the warm channel. The resin work is cooling.”

The man at the table made a note.

Aevra had put a hand on Drevik’s arm. He did not think she knew she was holding it.

Ivrassa explained that they needed another carrier. The Dragon listened without interruption. Once, when the pilot named a reach, it asked which side she meant. Its voice was deep but clear. Drevik found himself leaning forward to hear the answer more clearly.

“Tomorrow,” it said. “There are two freight boats coming to collect the finished panels. They go that way.”

“Can we rest here?”

“The outer room. No fires near the trays.”

The woman with the broom said, “I’ve just cleaned it.”

“I noticed,” the Dragon said.

She looked at the muddy company and sighed. “I’ll get mats.”

Drevik smiled before remembering the eye watching them.

“You have come down,” the Dragon said to him.

“Yes.”

“From which hold?”

“Kharveth.”

“Still the three water levels?”

“Four now,” Aevra said.

The head moved toward her. “Then someone finished the lower cut.”

“Some time ago.”

“Good.”

Drevik wanted to ask when it had last been there. He could not find a way to put the question which did not sound like asking its age.

“We’re going to Outhren,” he said instead.

“Vouthra. Their south gardens are flooding.”

“You know it?”

“I buy from it.”

The Dragon lifted one forefoot. A chain of small objects lay beneath the claws, each threaded onto a cord. It moved them out of the way with a delicacy Drevik could not stop watching.

“What is in your case?”

“A lamp.”

“For sale?”

“No.”

“Then keep it closed. I have enough.”

It looked toward Aevra’s hands.

“You work stone.”

“Yes.”

“A stair on the outer approach requires opening. Your people might do that while you wait.”

Ivrassa said something quickly in the other language. The Dragon replied in Aeler.

“I heard you. I am asking them.”

Aevra withdrew her hand from Drevik’s arm.

“How much stone?” she asked.

“Not much. A fallen facing.”

“Can we see it?”

The man at the table set down his pen.

He led them through a side passage, leaving the Dragon among its cloths. Drevik went with Aevra and Drelset. The passage was fitted with handrails at two heights. At its end, a stair descended toward a red-lit opening. Part of the outer wall had fallen across it. Beyond the fall Drevik could see steps continuing, then a gap with nothing beyond but moving brightness.

Aevra crouched well back from the broken edge. Drelset stayed beside the wall.

“How often does it move?” she asked.

The attendant shrugged. “It moved last week.”

“Since?”

“I don’t watch it.”

The fallen stones were larger than they had looked from the passage. The biggest lay across two steps, its weight resting on a cracked third. Aevra would not let Drelset go down to inspect beneath it. She asked about lifting tackle, anchors, another way to the lower landing. The man answered what he knew. Most of the answers were no.

They returned to the chamber.

“We can’t open that,” Aevra said.

The Dragon had settled lower, its body extending behind the hanging. One long fold of skin rose and fell beneath the ribs.

“Why?”

She explained. It listened to the description of the cracked step, the lack of an anchor above, the uncertain support below. Drevik watched its eye. He could detect no impatience and took comfort from that until it said, “You are small enough to pass beside the stone.”

“To get down,” Aevra said. “Not to lift it. And the steps may move under us.”

“I could clear the stone myself if I could reach it.”

“I understand.”

The Dragon’s jaw shifted. Drevik heard the faint click of teeth.

“Then the carriers will charge you their usual price.”

Aevra nodded.

It turned its head away. The conversation was over so simply that Drevik remained standing, waiting for an argument which did not come.

The woman brought mats to the outer room and told them where to wash. Her name was not offered. Drevik asked it while helping unroll the last mat, and she said he would not be able to say it properly before demonstrating that he could, after three attempts. He did not try to write it down.

She worked here because the heat let her husband grow something which would not thrive at their former home. She showed him the little white flowers again, speaking of them with a fondness which made him think of his father handling a well-made hinge. Her husband was the man at the table. He kept the accounts because she refused.

“Does it pay you?” Drevik asked, then wished he had phrased it differently.

She looked at him. “Would you sweep this floor for nothing?”

“No.”

“Neither would I.”

At supper she brought them food from her own pot, charged for it, and warned them that the Dragon would be leaving before their boats arrived. When it moved, she said, they were to keep clear of the central floor.

That night the warmth kept him awake.

The outer room held the smell of damp cloth and unfamiliar food. Aevra had fallen asleep with one arm over her eyes. Baerun and Kouven had finally stopped disputing the position of the injured leg. Beyond the doorway, something ticked as it cooled.

Drevik went to the washing basin. He did not venture into the central chamber. The attendants had left a small light at the safe turn, and he kept within its reach.

The man who copied the accounts sat there cleaning his pen. A dish beside him held the remains of supper.

“Too hot?” he asked.

“A little.”

“You get used to it. Then other places feel cold.”

Drevik washed his face. The basin water had lost its first freshness and smelled faintly of the pipes. He dried himself on his own cloth.

“How long have you worked here?”

“Long enough to owe people letters.”

Drevik smiled. The man looked at the pen, then set it down.

“Seven years. We meant to stay two.”

“Because of the flowers?”

“Among other things. My wife’s work sells well. The heat is steady in the growing rooms. We have a place for ourselves that isn’t this.” He gestured toward the chamber. “And our children can visit.”

“They don’t live here?”

“No.”

Drevik waited, but the man did not explain further.

From beyond the hanging came a low scraping noise. Drevik turned his head. The attendant did not.

“Should we move?”

“Not unless you hear the floor.”

Drevik looked down.

“The outer room is outside the path,” the man added. “I showed you.”

“Yes.”

Drevik returned to his mat, lay down, sat up again and moved the lamp case from beside his feet to beside his head. Aevra lifted her arm.

“What are you doing?”

“Nothing.”

She looked at the case, then at him.

“Try to sleep.”

“I am.”

“You’re trying to arrange the room so you can.”

He almost answered sharply. Then he moved the case back, because its corner was now against his shoulder, and lay down.

Aevra reached across the space between their mats and touched his sleeve once. She did not leave her hand there. He was grateful for that and, a moment later, wished she had.

The ticking beyond the door slowed. Someone turned a latch in the gallery. Drevik listened until those small noises acquired places in the room and ceased requiring him to sit up at each one.

Drevik had thought the woman with the broom was exaggerating until morning.

The Dragon rose behind the hangings and the whole chamber seemed to draw a breath. Its body unfolded along a route kept clear between the trays. A tail passed above the table where the account cloth had been rolled away. The attendants stood in places they evidently knew. Aevra pushed Drevik back against the wall before he realized he had stepped forward.

The head turned once toward them.

“Safe traveling,” it said.

Then it went out through the opening above the warm channel. The air behind it carried a dry animal smell beneath the resin. A hanging continued to sway long after the claws had ceased sounding on stone.

They spent another day in the outer shelter before the carriers arrived. The damaged boat could not go on with them. Its pilot arranged for it to be patched here and traveled as a passenger, sitting silently beside the steering gear of a boat he did not own.

The journey afterwards was slower than Drevik had hoped. They slept twice at river stations and once in the boats, moored beneath a wall where pale rootlike growth hung to the water. The freight crews knew which channels had become unreliable and spent hours sounding places they said had been clear on the previous journey. No one used that as a reason to stop sounding.

Baerun’s shoe dried. The parent’s burn began to heal. The child learned Drelset’s name and shouted it whenever he tried to sleep.

Drevik took the lamp out at each stop, checked the seam, and put it back. Once he dreamed that his father had come aboard at a station and was sitting behind him, waiting to be noticed. He woke before turning.

On the sixth day after leaving the market, Ivrassa came to the forward bench and pointed.

At the next bend the river caught a hundred lights.

They ran in terraces above the water: windows, doorways, workshops open to the river. A bridge crossed high between two faces of worked stone. Beneath it a boat was being hauled sideways onto rollers. Someone stood on its upturned hull painting a broad dark stripe.

Drevik stood until the pilot told him to sit. Even seated, he could see a line of old carving above the nearest roofs. Part of it was hidden by washing.

Part IV — The kingdom at supper

Chapter 23 — The old crown room

“Not there.”

Aevra lifted the pack again. The woman in the doorway pointed toward a recess already occupied by three baskets and a sleeping animal.

“There.”

The animal opened one eye as Aevra put the pack beside it. It had too many folds of skin around its neck for her to be sure where its head began. It closed the eye again.

They had been ashore for less than an hour. In that time Aevra had apologized for standing on a loading mark, moved twice to let other passengers past, and carried the same pack up two flights of steps because the first person they asked had sent them to the wrong room. She could hear water running behind the wall. Somewhere close, someone was beating metal with a light rapid hammer.

The room into which Ivrassa led them had once been magnificent.

Its pillars were still there: broad octagonal shafts, their upper faces cut into branches supporting a ceiling covered in shallow relief. In places the relief had been washed smooth by damp. Elsewhere Aevra could see little figures carrying bowls or standing with hands raised. Between the pillars, partitions of wood and plaster made smaller rooms. Smoke rose through a hole cut directly through one carved panel. Above a shop selling lamp fittings, an old royal face looked down with its nose worn away.

Drelset stopped beside Aevra.

“That’s a crown beam.”

“Yes.”

“They’ve put a drying rack through it.”

Drelset sounded offended in a way Aevra felt before she could justify it. The beam’s old work was still evident to them; the new hole arrived against it like an interruption in speech. Then she followed the rack’s fastenings. They had avoided the loaded faces. Whoever had fitted it had read the beam too.

The rack was full of shirts. A child beneath it was trying to reach a sleeve with a stick.

Ivrassa had gone ahead to speak to a woman washing something red in a shallow vat. Her sleeves were rolled above the elbows, and the color had settled into the creases of her hands. She looked at the company, listened to Ivrassa, and shook her head.

“No beds in mine.”

“We need someone to look at his leg,” Ivrassa said.

“That I can do.”

Kouven stepped forward. “I’ve kept it clean. It opened during—”

“Sit him down first.”

The woman pointed toward a bench. Baerun lowered himself gratefully. She rinsed her hands for a long time, then sent the child to fetch someone from the next court.

“Eulva,” she said, when Aevra offered her name. “Don’t lean against that cloth.”

Aevra straightened. A patch of red had already transferred to her sleeve.

Eulva saw it and clicked her tongue. “Cold water before it dries.”

A young man entered carrying a tray of small unglazed vessels. He stopped when he saw Baerun occupying the bench.

“I need that clear.”

“He needs to sit,” Eulva said.

“I can see that. I still need it clear.”

He looked around and set the tray on two crates instead. Aevra helped move the baskets away from his work. He thanked her, then asked her not to stack the heavier one on the lighter.

“This is my son, Thaurik,” Eulva said. “He has become particular since learning to make something that cracks.”

“It cracks when people put baskets on it.”

“I’ve never put a basket on your work.”

“Last week.”

“That was a lid.”

Aevra found herself smiling. Eulva caught her at it and smiled back, briefly, before returning to Baerun’s leg.

The person summoned from the next court brought clean cloth and a salve Kouven insisted on smelling. They discussed the wound without involving the rest of the room. Baerun objected only when asked to try standing again. He had stood often enough for one day, he said. Eulva told him that if he could argue he could put his heel down once.

Aevra went to wash her sleeve.

At the basin another woman was rinsing grain in a cloth. She made room without speaking. Aevra worked at the red mark until the water ran nearly clear.

“You’ve come from above,” the woman said.

“Yes.”

“Which above?”

“Kharveth.”

The woman’s hands stopped. “That far?”

“Not by choice.”

She laughed. “Most people who arrive here say that.”

Her name was Nulith. She wore a ring on a cord around her neck and had the broad shoulders of someone who carried loads without making an occasion of it. When Aevra asked whether Ivrassa’s sister lived nearby, Nulith said that depended which sister Ivrassa was claiming today.

“Household sister,” she added at Aevra’s expression. “They were brought up together. You’ll meet her. She’s out with the boat people.”

Nulith wrung the grain cloth with a practiced twist. Aevra held the bowl for her.

“Have you brought mail?”

“Only our own.”

“Pity.”

“For someone above?”

“For someone upriver. Different direction.”

Aevra gave the bowl back. Nulith thanked her and carried it through a low doorway, calling for someone to clear a place by the stove.

Thaereth found Aevra beside the basin. She had located a person who kept the water records and another who allocated work on the landings. Neither had time to see them until evening.

“Did you say where we were from?” Aevra asked.

“Yes.”

“And?”

“They’re moving stores out of the lower garden houses. Apparently that comes first.”

“It would.”

“I know.” Thaereth rubbed her forehead. “I know. I would still like someone to listen before I forget which part of the road went first.”

They sat together on a low wall. Aevra took out her little notebook. The pages had swollen in the river damp, and she had to separate two carefully. She began drawing the control approach while Thaereth described the sequence. They disagreed about when the wheel had moved. After a while Aevra drew two arrows and wrote both accounts beside them.

A passer-by stopped to look.

“That the north works?” he asked.

“We think so.”

He pointed at the upper stair. “That came down years ago.”

“Part of it was standing six days into our journey.”

He bent closer, studied the drawing, and shook his head. “Other side, then.”

He went on before they could ask more. Thaereth watched him disappear between the pillars.

“Tonight,” she said.

Before the meal, Nulith took Aevra to the room where the company could sleep. It was above a store, reached by a stair with a handrail worn smooth on one side. Two households already occupied it. A curtain separated their bedding from the space offered to the newcomers.

Aevra stopped at the threshold.

“Are we putting someone out?”

“The barrel racks.”

“Where do they go?”

“Below. If the owner ever clears the place he said he’d clear.”

Nulith went down again to argue with the owner. Aevra looked around. The ceiling was low, but dry. A small vent drew air from the court. There was room for the company if the packs were stacked carefully. Behind the curtain someone was telling a child a story in a voice too quiet for her to make out the words.

Ildret arrived with a light and began checking where it could be hung. The hook above the door turned when she touched it. She unscrewed it, examined the thread and held it out to Aevra.

“Someone’s fitted it into plaster.”

“There’s stone farther over.”

“I’ll ask first.”

Aevra nodded. At home she would have fitted it and expected thanks. Here she did not know whether the wall belonged to the room or the people behind it.

A man emerged from behind the curtain with a bowl in his hand. He looked at the hook and laughed.

“My son. He thought it was a good place.”

“For a lighter lamp,” Ildret said.

“For his coat.”

“Ah.”

He showed them a beam above the window where a light could be secured. Ildret thanked him and used a short loop rather than making a new hole. When Nulith returned, the man asked her whether the lower rooms would be ready soon. She gave him the same answer she had evidently given before, and he retreated behind the curtain.

Aevra helped clear the last racks. One had swollen in the damp and would not pass the door without being turned on its side. She and Nulith struggled with it while Ildret held the curtain clear. From behind it the story stopped. A small face appeared at the edge, watching.

“It’s not going to fit,” the child said.

“It came in,” Nulith answered.

“Before.”

Aevra laughed. “That’s usually the trouble.”

They got it through after removing one rail. The child followed to watch them put the rail back, then carried the little wooden pegs to Nulith with exaggerated care.

By the time the bedding was laid out, Baerun had been helped upstairs. He tested the floor with his stick and chose a place where he could reach the wall when standing. Kouven put the medical bag beside him. For the first time since the river failure, he took everything out of it: washed cloth, bottles, the small tools wrapped in oilskin. He laid them in rows and sat looking at what remained.

Aevra crouched beside him.

“What do you need?”

“Most of it.”

“I’ll ask.”

“Ask what it costs too. We can’t keep arriving with empty hands.”

She thought of the work they had already done and almost said so. Then she looked at the thinning pile of dressings. She wrote it down.

At the doorway, Nulith called that the food was ready. Aevra went to fetch the others, leaving Kouven to repack his bag in an order which satisfied him.

They ate in Nulith’s household, crowded around a table which had been lengthened with a board laid across two stools. The grain had become a thick savory dish with small pieces of something smoked in it. There were pickled stems and a soft flat bread. Aevra ate until she realized she had stopped listening to the conversation.

Drevik sat opposite her, answering questions about surface rain. Eulva wanted to know whether it was true that the water could freeze while still falling. Baerun told her yes, and that he had once been paid to clear it from a roof and considered the whole arrangement badly conceived.

Nulith asked about prices for lamp oil above. Thaereth knew some of them. The comparison led to a vigorous argument between two neighbors, one of whom believed they were being cheated by river carriers and the other of whom had a child who worked on a boat.

Aevra listened. No one asked her to decide.

After the meal Ivrassa came back, tired and damp to the waist. She brought a woman who resembled Nulith in the set of the mouth but had a longer face and a burn scar across one thumb.

“Veirsa,” Nulith said. “These are the upper people.”

Drevik stood so quickly that his knee struck the table.

Veirsa looked at him. “I’ve heard. Which family?”

He told her. She asked another name, then another. On the third she sat down.

“My grandmother used to say that name.”

Drevik remained standing.

“Sit,” she said. “Let me look at you without hurting my neck.”

Aevra left them to it. In the outer court the water-record keeper had arrived at last, carrying a bundle of slates. He was younger than she had expected and looked as though he had not slept. Thaereth introduced her. He examined their sketch and asked them to come with him in the morning.

“Not to the old road,” he said when Aevra reached for her tools. “Not first. You need to see where the water goes now.”

Later, in the room Nulith had found for them, Aevra unfolded her letter to Maeven. There was no carrier for it. She added a second sheet anyway.

We have reached Outhren. People live here. I have eaten in a house under the crown room.

She paused. Through the wall she could hear Drevik and Veirsa still talking, the names becoming less familiar as they went back.

You would like the fittings, she wrote. Some of them are ours.

Chapter 24 — The lamp in use

Veirsa noticed his hands before he noticed the lamp.

“You do that,” she said.

“What?”

“Rub the side of your thumb when you’re waiting.”

Drevik looked down. He had been standing in the workshop doorway while she cleared a stool. His thumb was pressed against the first joint of his finger.

“My father does it.”

“So did my mother.”

Veirsa smiled. He smiled back. It was easier than the names had been. They had traced the families far enough to establish the branch, then lost themselves in marriages and reused names, each of them certain about different parts. She had fetched a box of household papers. He had offered his copied drawing. Nulith had finally told them to stop before they began making relatives out of neighbors.

Now Veirsa put the stool beside her workbench.

“Sit there. If you sit on that one you’ll be in the way.”

The workshop opened onto a narrow court. A bench ran beneath a shuttered window, its surface covered in little trays of metal pieces. Finished handles hung from a rod. On the floor stood two kettles awaiting repairs, a basin with a split rim, and a box full of buckles which had been sorted by size. The smell of oil and warm metal was familiar enough to make Drevik’s throat tighten.

Veirsa lifted a lamp from its hook above the bench.

He knew it before she turned.

The reservoir was squat and oval. The paired wick lips projected a little farther than in his copy. Around the bail, layers of braid had darkened under hands and smoke. One side of the lid had a shallow dent near the thumb depression, exactly where the old drawing marked it. A different dent, much later and clumsier, belonged to the replica he had carried down; he could feel the distinction as if both were beneath his fingers.

Veirsa set it on the bench to trim a wick.

Drevik did not speak.

She glanced at him. “What?”

“The lamp.”

“Yes?”

He took out the drawing. His hands had begun to shake, and he flattened it with both palms.

Veirsa looked from the paper to the lamp.

“Oh,” she said. Then, more softly, “That one.”

She pushed it toward him.

He lifted it by the bail. It was heavier than he expected, partly because of the oil. Warmth rose through the braid. The lid fitted with a small uneven catch; someone had repaired the hinge, using a pin of a different metal. Around one wick lip a line of solder followed an old split. The underside was blackened, though no part of its purpose required a flame beneath it.

“Cooking,” Veirsa said when he turned it over. “My grandmother. Before she had another pot small enough.”

He laughed without meaning to. It came out badly.

“She cooked in it?”

“Once or twice. She said everything tasted of lamp oil.”

He set it down with care.

It had occupied a niche in his imagination for so long that he had never imagined it being washed.

Veirsa asked whether he wanted tea. He said yes. While she went into the adjoining room, he touched the dent in the lid. The metal was smooth there. Many fingers had gone over it since the drawing was made.

Veirsa returned with two cups and the box of papers they had abandoned the night before. She put it beside the lamp and lifted out a thin bundle tied with faded cord.

“My mother made this,” she said. “After the roof leaked on the older ones. Some of it’s wrong.”

“How do you know?”

“She put me down as born before my brother. I wasn’t.”

Drevik smiled. The writing was neat, the lines ruled with care. Beside some names were dates; beside others, trades, places or brief remarks. He found a name he knew and followed the line upwards with a finger held just above the paper.

“That one was my father’s grandfather.”

“Then you’ve skipped a person.”

They worked backwards. Veirsa fetched another sheet. Drevik took out his own notes, which had been folded so often that a crease ran directly through the name they needed. For several minutes both were certain. Then he saw that two people with the same name had been joined in his account.

“My father won’t like that.”

“Mine wouldn’t have either. He liked a straight line.”

“Was he the one who kept the papers?”

“No. He liked the line after someone else had drawn it.”

He laughed. She moved the lamp closer so they could see the small writing.

There were marriages to people whose names followed patterns Drevik did not recognize. He asked about one, and Veirsa named a river settlement. Another had come from the Mycothere terraces, an Aeler whose household had worked there for years. The places made no picture in his mind. He asked her to show him roughly where they lay.

She used three cups and a file on the bench, turning the river twice around the lamp. He moved one cup nearer the others because he thought she had put it there for lack of space. She moved it back.

“That’s a long way.”

“How long?”

“Depends on the river.”

He looked at her. She smiled apologetically.

“I know. But it does.”

The family spread across the bench. There were people who had left and been heard from, people who had left and not been heard from, a woman who had returned with three children and never said where the eldest had been born. Veirsa told the last story with relish until she reached the point where the eldest had died. Then she moved the cup aside and found another name.

Drevik asked whether there were any upper relatives in her own lifetime.

“Not that came here. There was a letter once, copied from another letter. It said someone had reached a hold beyond the east routes. My mother kept it because of the handwriting. She thought she’d seen it before.”

“Had she?”

“She never decided.”

Veirsa searched the box and failed to find it. Drevik helped, lifting the brittle pieces only where she showed him. They found a bill, a pattern for a kettle handle and a little scrap on which a child had practiced a name.

“Mine,” she said, taking that one.

She looked at it longer than he expected, then put it back beneath the pattern.

The missing letter remained missing. They had to move the cups when someone brought in a basin for repair, and the river on the bench disappeared.

A boy came through the doorway carrying a bundle of thin rods.

“Are you him?” he asked.

“Probably.”

“The one from the sky.”

“I’ve been under it.”

“What’s above it?”

Drevik looked at him, then saw the grin beginning.

“You’ve been told to ask me that.”

“My aunt.”

“Nulith?”

The boy nodded and put down the rods.

“Eirvek,” he said.

They shook hands. Eirvek had a raw patch along one knuckle and a habit of glancing toward the court when anyone passed. He asked about surface birds, then immediately asked whether Drevik could help straighten a rod which had been bent in transit. He seemed equally interested in both questions.

Drevik took the rod. “Where does it fit?”

Eirvek showed him a frame on the wall. They worked on it until Veirsa returned with tea and told them they had chosen the wrong rod.

Supper was late. Veirsa’s partner came home carrying an empty basket and the news that one of the lower stores had taken water. His name was Rethven. He listened to the explanation of Drevik’s visit, asked two questions about the family, then spent most of the meal trying to persuade Eirvek to finish a task before beginning another.

Drevik liked him. That made what he had to say more difficult.

The lamp stood on a shelf between the eating room and the workshop. Its light reached both. Nulith had come over with a dish, and two neighbors stopped at the door, accepted food and stayed. The conversation turned to the river, the gardens, someone who had sold a bad pump part and was now claiming that the buyer had fitted it incorrectly.

When the neighbors left, Drevik cleared bowls. Veirsa let him, then took the cloth from him because he was using the clean end to wipe the table.

“My father is ill,” he said.

She stopped wiping.

“Ivrassa said.”

“He asked me to bring the lamp back. For his wake.”

There. He had said it without using the word home.

Veirsa looked toward the shelf. Rethven sat back in his chair. Eirvek, who had been about to leave, remained in the doorway.

Drevik explained the replica, the family drawing, his father’s wish to return to Kharveth, the journey which had been meant to take days. He did not explain how often he had imagined laying the original beside the bed. He could not speak of that without sounding as if it had already happened and Veirsa was preventing it.

She listened to the end.

“I’m sorry he’s ill,” she said. “I would have liked to meet him.”

Drevik waited.

“The lamp stays here.”

Veirsa’s hand moved toward the shelf, then rested on the table. Drevik knew that arrested movement. His father made it when someone proposed replacing a sound piece because the rest of an object had worn out. Yet Veirsa had replaced the hinge, the wick lips, almost all the braid. Taking it out of this room had made her stop moving.

He had expected a question first. Something about when he would leave, or how much the lamp mattered, or whether they could make another.

“I brought a copy,” he said.

“I know.”

“It’s very close.”

“I saw it.”

He looked down at his hands. His thumb had found the side of his finger again.

Veirsa sat opposite him.

“My grandmother took it out when the old stores failed,” she said. “The crypt had oil and the house had none. She took the oil first. Later she brought the lamp because the one they had leaked. She repaired it. My mother used it. I learned to work under it.”

He nodded too quickly.

“She sat there,” Veirsa continued, pointing toward the workbench. “Where you sat today. When she couldn’t see the fine work anymore, she held the light for me. She hated being asked whether she was tired.”

Drevik swallowed.

“I don’t need you to tell me which mother had it first,” she said. “I know why you want it.”

“I wasn’t going to.”

She looked at the drawing still beside his plate. He folded it.

Rethven got up and put water on. Nobody asked for tea. He made it anyway, and after a while they drank it.

Eirvek went back to the workshop. Drevik could hear him sorting the rods. One fell, rolled, and struck another. He wanted to go and help, to have something which could be finished correctly before he slept.

Instead he said, “May I stay tonight?”

Veirsa’s face changed.

“Of course you may.”

She sounded hurt that he had asked.

They made his bed in the room beside the workshop. It had a shelf full of things waiting for homes: a cracked bowl holding nails, a child’s shoe, folded cloth too small to be useful until someone needed exactly that size. Drevik placed his case beneath the bed.

Through the open doorway he could see the lamp.

Veirsa came to turn it down. She paused beside his bed and asked whether he needed another cover. He said no, then asked for one because he was cold and there seemed no point in making that harder too.

She brought it without comment.

The household settled. A door closed. Rethven coughed. Water moved through the court’s channel with a soft intermittent patter. The lamp remained on the shelf, its flame low enough to be almost hidden behind the lip.

Drevik had crossed broken roads to reach it. Now there were six paces between his bed and the shelf, and nobody awake in those six paces.

He turned toward the wall. The extra cover smelled faintly of the same oil.

Chapter 25 — A household’s morning

Rethven burned the first bread.

He was arguing through the doorway with someone who wanted a basin repaired before the next boat left. Veirsa had already said it could not be done. The customer appeared to believe that asking Rethven would change the time available. Rethven, who did not repair basins, was explaining why it would not when the smell reached him.

“Oh, come out,” he said, and Drevik thought he meant the customer until he lifted the blackened bread from the pan.

Eirvek laughed. Veirsa took the next piece from the board and put it in herself.

“Your turn to speak to him,” Rethven said.

“I already did.”

The customer remained in the doorway. Drevik offered him the burned bread. He took it, looked at it, and finally left.

For several minutes the household was cheerful.

Drevik had slept badly but woke hungry. Eirvek had questions prepared: whether trees could grow through roofs, whether people who fell into the sky always died, whether surface lamps had lids to keep rain from putting them out. The first and third were sensible. The second was Nulith again.

“You can tell your aunt,” Drevik said, “that I have very reliable boots.”

“Will you tell her yourself? She’ll want to see your face.”

Veirsa gave Eirvek a piece of bread and told him to stop tormenting the guest until he had eaten.

Work began before the bowls were cleared. The basin customer had left his basin outside despite being refused. Rethven carried it back to his door. Veirsa sorted the day’s repairs and asked Drevik whether he could replace a little latch without altering the holes.

He could. She gave him the good files after watching him choose from the worn ones.

The workshop filled with people coming to collect things which were not ready and people surprised to find things which were. A woman brought three handles in a cloth and insisted there had originally been four. Veirsa counted the marks in her book, then counted the handles. Drevik found the fourth still attached to the woman’s basket. She laughed, embarrassed, and stayed to tell them news which had probably been the main reason for her visit.

A marriage had been postponed. Someone had been caught watering lamp oil, an offense Drevik had never imagined needing to distinguish from ordinary stupidity. A neighbor’s daughter had left with a carrier and returned the same day because the carrier’s mother was aboard.

Veirsa laughed at that so hard she put down her tool.

“My mother would have done the same,” she said.

“Returned?” Drevik asked.

“Gone with us.”

He told her about his father following him to a fair to make certain he was paid properly, then buying more than Drevik had earned. She wanted to know what he had bought. Drevik remembered two objects and could not remember the third. The failure troubled him until she began describing a similar purchase of her mother’s, a pot which did not fit any stove they owned.

They worked well beside each other. The latch took longer than he expected because the old holes had worn oval. He made a thin backing plate and tested it against the original piece before drilling. Veirsa watched him do that and asked where he had learned the trick. He told her. She knew a different way using less metal and more time. They disagreed about which was better for a customer who would never oil the hinge.

At midday Eirvek brought water from the court and poured it into an open jar beside the bench. Drevik put the cover on automatically.

“Leave it,” Veirsa said.

“Dust.”

“It’s settling water. For the wash. Drinking’s inside.”

He removed the cover. The jar stood in exactly the place where his father kept drinking water. He had not looked at the sediment already lining its bottom.

A little later Veirsa said surface households must spend a fortune replacing clothes ruined by rain. Drevik explained drying lines, covered yards, the smell of a coat which had not dried properly for three days. She was particularly interested in the coat.

“Why keep wearing it?”

“Because it’s still raining.”

She considered this, then said she preferred the jar problem.

After the meal Nulith arrived with a basket of cleaning things. Eirvek groaned.

“You said you would come,” she told him.

“I said if the rods were done.”

“They are.”

He looked at Drevik with betrayal. Drevik had straightened the last three while waiting for a kettle to cool.

“Where?” he asked.

“The family room,” Nulith said. “You came through it. You may as well see it from the useful side.”

The crypt.

Drevik looked toward Veirsa. She was wrapping a finished piece.

“We go when the river allows,” she said. “Not always as often as we should.”

“You keep it?”

“Who did you think cleaned it?”

He had no answer he wanted to give her.

They went by a small boat through a side channel, then climbed a service passage which reached the crypt below the porch. The route was damp but maintained. Eirvek carried the cleaning basket and complained only until they left the houses behind. After that he asked Drevik about his father’s work.

The boat took less than an hour. Drevik tried to fit it to the long route they had walked from the crypt to the failed control room, the days with Uivren, the river journey around the hot reach. Nulith pointed out the blocked channel which would once have connected the short way. He could see only its mouth, choked with pale debris.

“Not through there,” she said when he leaned forward.

“I wasn’t proposing it.”

“Good.”

The passage into the crypt emerged behind a low stone screen he had noticed on the first visit without understanding its use. He stepped through and found himself beside the empty niche.

For a moment he could hear the expedition as it had been: Baerun complaining about dust, Zerla moving ahead, Aevra asking him not to touch the loose edge. He put a hand against the niche and felt its familiar coolness.

Nulith unpacked the brushes.

Drevik asked about the man on the porch step.

“The one with the boot? He shows when the basin’s running high.” She passed him a brush. “Did you speak to him?”

“He didn’t hear.”

“He never has. Eirvek used to sit beside him and do his own boots.”

Eirvek bent over the basket. “I was little.”

“Have you a name for him?” Drevik asked.

“No.” Nulith looked toward the porch. “We leave that step clear.”

The work was ordinary and took a long time. Dust had collected on the higher carving where the damp cloth could not reach. Eirvek climbed a little stool and brushed it down while Drevik held the stool. Nulith cleaned the basin and changed the small offering of dried growth beside it. Nothing was said about whether the dead wanted it.

Drevik asked about Orrvek.

Nulith knew the name and the trade. She knew which household had continued visiting after the separation and which had married into theirs. She did not know whether the story about his refusing a royal payment was true. Her grandmother had said he was difficult about money, but her grandmother said that of nearly everyone.

Drevik laughed, then felt disloyal, then laughed again because Eirvek was trying to brush dust out of his own collar with the dirty end of the brush.

At the niche, Drevik hesitated.

“Does anyone object to it being empty?”

Nulith looked at the oval mark. “You?”

“I meant here.”

“Some did. When it was taken. So I was told.”

Nulith pressed the cloth into the empty niche without hesitation. Drevik could not have done it so easily.

“What happened?”

“They kept arguing. Then there were other things to argue about.”

She gave him a cloth and went to clean the door.

He wiped the niche. At the back, where no one would see the difference, he worked the cloth into a corner until it came away clean.

On the return journey Eirvek sat beside him in the boat.

“If I went up,” the boy said, “would I be able to come back?”

“I don’t know.”

“Not now. Later.”

“I still don’t know.”

Eirvek looked at the water. Drevik wished he could have given him something more inviting. He described the surface town instead: the yard behind his father’s workroom, a market where the awnings changed the color of people’s faces, the noise of birds under a roof at dawn. Eirvek asked whether he had a room there. Drevik said yes, though someone else was using it while he was away.

“Someone you like?”

“Yes.”

“Then you could share.”

Drevik smiled. “Perhaps.”

When they returned, Veirsa had finished the latch and fitted it to the customer’s box. She had used his backing plate. He noticed and said nothing. The lamp was above her hand, steady in the draught from the court.

Rethven refused to let them work through the evening.

Veirsa had a row of small pieces laid out beside the lamp. She was counting them when he came in and set a folded cloth over the whole row.

“I’ll lose where I am.”

“You’re at six. I counted.”

“I was at seven.”

He lifted a corner, counted again and put it down. “Six.”

She looked offended, then laughed.

They were going to the washing court, he told Drevik. Not to wash. There would be food and people with no interest in his family papers. Veirsa said that last part was untrue; half the court had already asked whether the upper cousin had come to take away her house. Drevik looked at her. She touched his arm.

“They ask about everything. Get your coat.”

Eirvek had gone ahead. Nulith was not coming; she had arranged to eat elsewhere and would not change her plans merely because Rethven had remembered his. Drevik liked the small indignation in her farewell.

The washing court lay beyond a narrow covered way in which pipes sounded overhead. At the far end, people had covered the large basins with fitted boards. Lamps stood at their corners. Steam still rose through the gaps, warming the damp air and carrying a clean mineral smell beneath the frying food. The central drain remained open, and everyone stepped over it without looking. Drevik stepped into it almost immediately.

Rethven caught his elbow.

“Not the first.”

“My boot.”

“It drains.”

“It certainly does.”

Veirsa was laughing too hard to help. He accepted that, stood on one foot and shook the water out while a woman at the nearest table moved her plate to safety.

They bought hot folded cakes from a pan set on a little stove. Rethven knew the cook, who accused him of owing for the last time. He said he had paid. She said he had paid her sister, who had been visiting and had no authority to take money. They argued until the sister, sitting two places away, said she had spent it and both could stop pretending they had not known.

Drevik paid for the cakes. Veirsa took his purse away before he could pay for everybody’s drink as well.

“You won’t buy yourself quiet here.”

“I was buying a drink.”

“For eight people.”

He put the purse away.

A musician sat on a folded blanket beside the warm pipe. The instrument across her knees had strings stretched over a shallow box and a row of loose metal tongues along one edge. A pale hollow in the wood fitted the heel of her hand. She struck the strings with her fingers, then turned the box so that the tongues shivered against one another. The sound was dry and bright, followed by a little rattling shimmer which seemed to arrive from behind the tune. Drevik had heard nothing quite like it.

He listened through one piece without eating. When it ended, the musician rubbed her wrist and asked someone to bring her food before she began another. Nobody treated the request as an interruption.

Eirvek found them and took half of Drevik’s cake. He had lost a game somewhere else and was indignant about a rule which apparently changed depending on who explained it. Veirsa told him to play something he understood. He said that was how people stayed ignorant, then ducked when Rethven reached for him.

Across the court, Drelset was holding a wooden disc at eye level.

Drevik recognized his voice before seeing him. He was telling two local men that he had understood the rule perfectly and was objecting to it on aesthetic grounds.

“That means he’s losing,” Veirsa said.

Drevik went to look.

The game used a board laid over a basin, marked with shallow grooves and little upright pegs. Each player flicked three discs from one end, trying to pass between the pegs and stop within the grooves. Too much force sent a disc into a catching cloth at the far end. Drelset had done that twice. His last disc rested beautifully in a groove which, he now discovered, scored for the other player.

“It’s pointing at me,” he protested.

“That’s how we know it’s yours.”

The local man moved the disc to his own side. Drelset paid him a small coin and asked for another round.

“You needn’t,” Drevik said.

“I know.”

“I mean, if you don’t like the rule—”

“I like the game. I dislike him.”

His opponent grinned and offered him the discs again.

Drelset had washed and combed his beard. The two points were neatly bound, each with a short dark thread. His torn sleeve was folded so the damage barely showed. Drevik had only seen him preparing to work or recovering from it. Here he stood with one hip against the table, talking too loudly because the woman beside the musician kept looking over when he did.

He lost the next round by a smaller margin and took this as encouragement.

When Aevra arrived with Ildret, she watched a single throw and began to tell him how to compensate for the slope.

Drelset put down the disc.

“Are you playing?”

“No.”

“Then get something to eat.”

She stared at him. He stared back, smiling just enough to make it difficult to be angry.

Ildret took Aevra by the elbow and steered her toward the stove. Drevik remained beside the board. Drelset waited until Aevra was out of earshot, then asked whether the slope really was toward the far corner.

“A little.”

“I thought so.”

His next throw was much worse.

He laughed first, loudly enough to make the musician look round. The woman beside her said something Drevik did not catch. Drelset answered in the same language, badly. She corrected him. He tried again, and she came over to show him how to hold the disc.

Drevik returned to Veirsa before he could become the audience Drelset did not need.

Later Rethven danced.

Drevik had not expected it of him, though he could not have said why. He danced with an older woman who insulted his timing and a younger man who nearly trod on his foot. When he came back to the table he was flushed and pleased, carrying a cup which belonged to neither him nor anyone there.

Veirsa refused his hand twice. On the third request she stood, warning him that her knee hurt. Drevik watched them move into the little space between tables. Rethven shortened his step. She caught the change and made him put it back. They argued while dancing and appeared to enjoy both.

Aevra sat opposite Drevik, eating the last of a cake. She watched them, then looked away quickly when she caught him watching her.

“Maeven dances,” she said.

“Do you?”

“Not like that.”

“Neither do they.”

Rethven had just missed a turn. Veirsa struck his shoulder with the flat of her hand and laughed.

Aevra smiled, but did not get up. Ildret did, joining a line in which she was given no time to learn the steps before it moved. She made mistakes and returned breathless, annoyed that she wanted to try again.

The court did not empty all at once. People left because children needed sleep, because a boat would depart early, because someone had stayed out longer than promised. The musician stopped to eat again. Steam diminished beneath the boards. A worker removed a lamp from a table already cleared and began taking up the covering so the basin could be used in the morning.

Veirsa put on her coat. Drevik rose with her. Drelset, still at the game, lifted two fingers to show he had won something. His opponent immediately held up three.

“Don’t tell her,” Drelset called, nodding at Aevra.

“I can count,” she answered.

They left him laughing.

On the way home, Rethven carried the borrowed cup until Veirsa noticed and sent him back with it. She and Drevik waited beneath a pipe which ticked softly as it cooled.

“Does it happen every week?” he asked.

“When someone wants it enough to clear the basins.”

He pictured the covered water, the musician eating, Ildret trying the turn again. At the workshop door he almost asked whether they could go next time. Then he remembered that they meant to leave.

Veirsa took the cloth off the workbench. The six pieces were still there.

“Tomorrow,” she said, before he could sit down to help.

He went to his bed with one boot still damp. Through the wall he could hear Rethven returning, trying to convince her that the cup’s owner had given him another drink for bringing it back.

Chapter 26 — The later door

The water-record keeper’s name was Vaurden. He took Aevra and Thaereth through the lower gardens before he took them to the closure.

Aevra understood why after the first terrace.

The channel which should have fed it carried almost nothing. Water came through the wall instead, a steady sheet down the back of the beds. Workers had dug a temporary drain across the path and laid boards over it. The plants nearest the wall had begun to collapse. Farther out, where the water did not reach, their edges were dry.

Vaurden stopped to help lift a basket onto a woman’s back. She asked him a question which made him look away before answering. Aevra caught only a place name.

“She wants to know when the lower room will be ready,” he said after they moved on. “I told her yesterday. It isn’t.”

“Can we help?”

“Later. First see this.”

They followed him into a maintenance gallery behind the gardens. The stone changed as they climbed: recent patching gave way to older work, then to a rough collapse partly cut through by hand. The passage narrowed between two huge blocks. Beyond it stood a wall fitted across the entire width of the gallery.

Thaereth stopped before touching it.

Aevra knew the method, if not the masons or the period: courses keyed into both sides, a slight curve facing the expected load, drainage openings which could be plugged from the safe side. The safe side was not theirs.

Below the fitted wall lay older broken stone. Its edges had weathered before the later courses were laid over them.

“The first fall,” Vaurden said, pointing down. “The closing, above.”

Aevra ran a finger along the joint between them. The later mortar had filled a chipped edge without any of the crushing which marked the fall. This had been built after someone could get here to build it.

Thaereth examined a narrow groove beside the lowest course.

“Water relief.”

“Yes.”

“Where does it discharge?”

“Above. Or it did. We can’t reach that side.”

Vaurden showed them a side recess holding a stone instruction panel. Part of its lower corner was gone. The surviving lines were in an older formal hand, but Aevra could read enough: upper reservoirs, contamination, unstable connection, closure to be maintained pending further inspection.

Thaereth read the whole surviving passage twice.

“It doesn’t order anyone left below,” she said.

“No,” Vaurden answered.

“It orders the water separated.”

“Yes.”

Neither sounded satisfied.

Aevra put her lamp closer. Beneath the official cutting, much later hands had scratched dates and names. One line had been gone over so many times that the letters were broad shallow wounds in the stone. Vaurden traced it without touching.

“An attempt from here. They heard tools above. They answered. Nothing opened.”

“How do you know that?” Thaereth asked.

“There’s an account.”

“Contemporary?”

“Near enough that the woman who wrote it complained about the men coming home late.”

Before they left the wall, Vaurden asked Aevra to look at the lowest relief opening.

It was narrow enough that she had to lie on one shoulder to bring her eye level with it. Thaereth held the lamp well back. Inside, a metal lining continued beyond sight. Deposits had grown along its lower edge. Aevra could hear a faint intermittent drip.

“Can it be opened from here?” Vaurden asked.

“Not safely by knocking it through.”

“That isn’t what I asked.”

She sat up. He was watching her closely.

“I don’t know what’s on the other side. If the relief still connects to the upper reservoir, taking it out could bring water into this gallery faster than it can leave.”

He nodded. “That’s what the last mason said.”

“Was the last mason wrong?”

“No. I wanted to hear whether you could tell me something else.”

Aevra looked again. She wanted to. The opening was so small, the wall so plainly made by hands like hers. A day with the right tools and someone on the far side might have turned it into an ordinary job.

Thaereth asked where the gallery drained. Vaurden took them back to the junction and showed a shallow channel which dropped toward the gardens. In high water it ran the other way. He had marked its recent level on the wall.

Aevra stood beside the mark. It was above her waist.

They returned to the closure with a longer probe from Vaurden’s tool bag. She fed it into the opening until its end struck something hard, withdrew it a little, changed the angle. The resistance remained. She could not tell whether she had reached an old shutter, fallen stone or a deliberate plug. When she drew the probe out, its tip carried black wet grit.

Thaereth examined it without touching. Vaurden wrapped it separately from the other tools.

“Someone above,” Aevra said. “We’d need someone above to isolate the water first.”

“Yes.”

Vaurden had brought the probe himself. She had nothing to tell him about this wall; above, she could describe the flooded beds and the woman waiting for a room.

“I’ll draw the relief,” she said.

“Please.”

She measured what she could reach. Thaereth wrote the dimensions while Vaurden held the light. The drawing was small and incomplete. Aevra added the observed water level and left space around the unknown connection rather than filling it with a guessed line.

At the bottom of the page she wrote the tools which would be needed on the far side. Then she crossed out one and replaced it with a larger size.

Vaurden watched the change. For the first time since bringing them there, he looked a little less tired.

They returned to a workroom where Vaurden kept the water slates. He brought out a wrapped book, repaired more than once, and found the passage with the help of a woman who had been copying entries onto new sheets. She made him wash his hands first.

The account listed food sent to the workers, a broken tool, the number of shifts. Then it described tapping answered from beyond the wall and a message passed through a relief opening too small for a person. The upper workers had said they must return for authority. There was another entry several weeks later: no answer, more food spent, one worker injured on the approach. The next page concerned a household dispute over a pump.

Aevra read backwards to establish the date. The copying woman corrected one numeral she misread. It was well after the original separation.

Thaereth asked permission to copy the passage. The woman gave her a clean sheet and watched while she did it.

“Do you have the upper reply?”

“No.”

“Only this account?”

“This account, the wall, and the fact that we’re still on this side.”

Vaurden said the woman’s name softly. She shrugged and returned to her copying.

Aevra sat with the book open in front of her. She had written lost often enough in her notes. She turned back to her sketch and crossed it out in the place where it claimed more than she knew.

They ate with the workroom crew. One of the men had come from a hold Aevra had heard named in a winter recitation. She asked him to repeat it.

He did, looking amused.

“That’s been gone for—” She stopped.

“For how long?”

“I don’t remember.”

“My aunt will. She complains about it every year.”

He had been a child when his household left its failing water source. Some had stayed; others had moved in stages along lower routes. The upper account, as Aevra remembered it, had ended with a collapse and a list of names. He recognized two names she could recall. One was his grandfather. The other, he said, had been drunk when the first party set out and missed the departure, but lived for years afterwards.

Thaereth asked where the survivors had settled.

“Several places. Why?”

“To write it correctly.”

He considered that. Then he began naming places slowly enough for her to follow.

On the way back, Aevra and Thaereth walked without Vaurden. He had stayed to deal with the unfinished lower room.

“I’ve signed closures,” Thaereth said.

“I know.”

“I know what comes through when the wrong water meets a reservoir.”

At the meal Thaereth had left her water untouched. Aevra remembered seeing her do that once before, while reading an old failure report at the office. The account had been older than either of them. It had taken the officer the rest of the meal to become thirsty again.

“So do I.”

They passed the garden drain. A child was kneeling beside it trying to float a scrap of bark beneath the boards. The bark caught. An adult called the child away without looking up from a basket.

“The people who signed that are dead,” Thaereth said.

“Yes.”

“The people who failed to come back are probably dead.”

Aevra waited.

“I don’t want to write something that makes it sound simple.”

“Then don’t.”

Thaereth looked at her sharply. Aevra had not meant it as a rebuke, but she could not make it gentle without saying more than she wanted.

At the next stair they stopped to let a load pass. Four people carried a frame full of wet household goods. One slipped and the others steadied it before anything fell. Aevra put her shoulder under the corner until they reached the landing.

When she returned, Thaereth was still waiting.

“We should help with that room,” Aevra said.

“Yes.”

They went to find Vaurden. He gave them work before either had finished offering.

Chapter 27 — Those who would go

Aevra said it while carrying a door.

“We’ll take anyone who wants to come, if we find the way.”

The woman beside her stopped walking. The door tipped. Aevra caught its edge against her thigh and asked her to keep moving until they reached the wall.

Once the door was down, the woman asked whether Aevra meant it.

“If there’s a way,” she repeated.

“Could my father manage it?”

“Can he walk?”

“A little.”

“What about stairs?”

“That was what I was asking you.”

Aevra looked at the door. They had removed it because the room needed wider access for stores, not because anyone had been thinking about carrying an old man through. She had spoken to fill the interval before putting it down.

“I haven’t seen the whole route.”

“But you said—”

“I know.”

The woman waited. Aevra could hear hammering inside the room, Drelset fitting a temporary shelf while the other workers cleared the floor. She wanted to go back to something whose dimensions she could measure.

“Let me find out first,” she said.

By the end of the afternoon three more people had asked her.

One wanted to visit a shrine named in an old household book. One wanted to know whether tools could be carried back down. A parent asked whether there would be a place for children at the upper station and whether those children could return if they disliked it. Aevra could answer none of these questions. She wrote them down because not writing them felt worse.

When she told Thaereth, the officer shut her eyes.

“How many?”

“I don’t know.”

“You have to stop saying it.”

“I have stopped.”

“The first time you said it was enough.”

Aevra put the notebook down too hard. Thaereth opened her eyes again.

“I’ll help you speak to them,” she said.

That was worse, briefly, than being told she was wrong.

The relocation meeting took place in the old crown room, under the drying rack. There were no chairs left for the expedition by the time they arrived. Aevra stood against a pillar while a woman with a deep voice read out the households assigned to the next boats.

The objections were immediate and particular. A loom could not be dismantled before the weaver finished the cloth already on it. An elderly man would not move unless his neighbor moved too. A store room offered for sleeping still held material which irritated people’s throats. Someone had promised it would be cleared. Someone else said it had been cleared, and the argument became a dispute about what clearing meant.

Vaurden waited until they had finished, then named the people who would inspect it again. Aevra’s name was not among them. She felt both relieved and slighted.

Nulith spoke about food for the crossing. Rethven had found another boat but not a pilot. Ivrassa would ask among the freight crews. Veirsa wanted to know whether the workshop could be moved in two loads because several repairs still belonged to neighbors who had not decided where to go.

No one asked whether Outhren should be abandoned. They were discussing which terraces could remain in use, which households needed moving first, and how far downriver those households should go. Some would stay within reach of their gardens. Others intended to live with relatives for a season and see what happened.

When the meeting broke into smaller arguments, Aevra found Eulva beside a pillar rubbing one knee.

“Stairs?” Eulva asked.

Aevra sighed. “I don’t know yet.”

“How many did you use coming?”

“A great many. We can’t return that way.”

“That doesn’t answer it, then.”

“No.”

Eulva nodded. “I can do a few. Not a great many.”

Thaurik came over carrying a cloth bundle. He had found Eulva’s shawl where she had left it near the vat.

“I’m not cold,” she told him.

“You will be.”

“Then I’ll put it on.”

He laid it over her arm rather than her shoulders. Aevra liked him for that.

He wanted to know about upper kilns. She could answer some of those questions. He asked about clay, fuel, drying time. Eulva listened with an expression which suggested she had heard all this before and still preferred it to silence.

“If there’s a road,” Thaurik said, “we might try it.”

Aevra made herself say, “I can’t promise it will be manageable.”

“I heard you.”

Baerun refused the carrying chair.

It was not yet a chair, only a frame borrowed from a household which used it to move an elder between rooms. Kouven wanted to see whether it could be adapted. Baerun took one look and reached for his stick.

“I can walk.”

“Some of the way.”

“Then we’ll start with some of the way.”

Aevra arrived while they were arguing. Thaereth stood beside the frame, looking as though she regretted ever mentioning it.

“It isn’t only for you,” she said.

Baerun looked at her. “Who else have you promised to haul?”

“No one.”

He turned toward Aevra.

She said, “I spoke too broadly.”

“That sounds like something you’ll put in a report.”

“Yes. It does.”

Kouven sat down on the frame.

“Look at me,” he said.

Baerun did not.

“I’m asking how we get you over a section you can’t walk. I’m not deciding where you spend the rest of your life.”

The hauler’s jaw worked. He looked at the chair at last.

“Those crosspieces are wrong.”

“Then show us.”

Baerun lowered himself onto the bench beside it and began explaining where the load would bear. Drelset came in halfway through, heard the explanation, and fetched a piece of charcoal. Aevra left them to it.

Outside, Nulith was waiting with Eirvek.

“He wants to go up,” she said.

“I know.”

“I’ll come with him if there’s a road.”

Eirvek looked at her with an expression halfway between gratitude and annoyance. Aevra remembered being young enough that accompaniment could feel like an accusation.

“Does Veirsa agree?”

“She agrees he can decide after seeing the route plan.”

“And you?”

“I’ve wanted to go for years. I can have a reason of my own.”

Aevra apologized. Nulith accepted it with a brief lift of her chin.

Veirsa would remain with Rethven and the lower households. There were repairs which could not be left half-finished, people who knew her work, a place on the river which she and her partner had already discussed. Nulith would carry letters and return when a route allowed it. Eirvek did not know whether he would return with her.

He said that last part carefully, watching his aunt. She did not contradict him.

Aevra took them to Thaereth so the questions would be written in one place rather than several unreliable notebooks.

For the rest of the afternoon she worked in the room promised to the garden households.

It was not a good room. The floor held damp near the back wall, and one corner smelled of material which had been stored there too long. The person directing the work was a woman with a stiff left arm who knew where every object had come from and was furious with several of its owners. She gave Aevra a scraper and told her to clear the lower plaster before deciding how much could be retained.

Aevra worked on her knees. Behind her, two neighbors dragged shelves into the light. They argued about a piece of cloth found behind one shelf, whether it had been stolen or lent and never returned. The woman with the stiff arm told them to argue while carrying.

The plaster came away easily where the damp had reached it, in small soft flakes which stuck to the scraper. Higher up it held. Aevra followed the line, making it uneven rather than taking sound material to produce a tidy edge.

A young worker watched her for a while, then began doing the same beside her. They worked without speaking until the worker asked why she had come down.

“To look at the old road.”

“Why?”

“Something came up in the river. We thought it had been repaired recently.”

“What?”

“A bell.”

The worker stopped scraping. “A little one? With a line round the shoulder?”

“Yes.”

“My uncle lost one from the lower boat. He said the rope broke. His wife said he tied it badly.”

Aevra sat back. She described the bell’s mark, its clapper, the repair washer. The worker knew nothing about the mark and thought the clapper sounded right. The uncle was away with one of the first loads. He might be able to tell them when he returned.

She wrote down the household’s location. There was no certainty yet, but she felt suddenly foolish about how much mystery the bell had acquired on the journey. Somewhere below, a person might be annoyed about losing it.

The worker resumed scraping.

“Does he want it back?” Aevra asked.

“He’ll say he does.”

“It’s a long way away.”

“He’ll say that too.”

They laughed. The woman directing the work came to inspect the stripped wall and asked what was funny. The worker told her. She knew the uncle and laughed more loudly than either of them.

When the wall was ready, Aevra helped lay boards over the driest part of the floor for sleeping platforms. The room still smelled unpleasant. It would need time, and air, and work after people began using it. The woman showed her the next tasks without apologizing for their number.

By the time Aevra left, the first household had brought two baskets to the doorway. One contained bedding. The other contained a pot with a lid tied on. The owner asked which corner was theirs. Aevra went to fetch the woman who knew.

That evening Vaurden found Aevra washing plaster from her hands.

“You know how to inspect a bearing?”

“Yes.”

“Come early. The service approach above the old lifting room. We need to move stores through it if the lower steps take water.”

“Is that toward the royal stair?”

“Near it. Uivren says she can look at the connection while you’re there.”

Aevra dried her hands. “How many people do you need?”

“Two who know what they’re looking at. No one stands beneath it while you decide.”

“I’ll ask Drelset.”

Vaurden nodded and went away.

She found Drelset outside the room where they slept, mending the torn sleeve Ivrassa had mocked. His stitches were poor and he knew it. He held the cloth up before she could speak.

“If you tell me to give it to someone else, I’ll make you wear it.”

“There’s work tomorrow.”

“There usually is.”

She explained. He listened, pulled the thread through, and bit it off.

“Will you come?” she asked.

“Yes. After I eat.”

“Of course.”

“Last time you said that we were carrying things before the pot was hot.”

She remembered. “After you eat.”

He tested the seam, frowned, and began unpicking it.

Aevra sat beside him. For a while she held the cloth taut while he sewed.

Chapter 28 — In the work case

Drevik had the lamp wrapped before he admitted what he was doing.

He told himself he was examining the repair. He had taken it down from the shelf while Veirsa was out and turned it over in his hands, tracing the line of solder. Then he had fetched the soft cloth from his case because the oil might mark the bench. Then he had drained the little remaining oil into the household jar.

The lamp fitted the case better than his replica did.

He stood over it, one hand on the lid. From the court came Eirvek’s voice, then Rethven’s, farther away. They were moving something heavy and disagreeing about which end should go first.

Drevik could close the case. He could put the replica on the shelf. Not to deceive her for long; she would know at once. He was not foolish enough to imagine otherwise. But by then he might be gone, and he could send money, or a better copy, or whatever she asked for.

He knew how that sounded. He kept standing there.

His father had held his wrist on the last morning. Drevik remembered the weight of that hand, lighter than it had once been, and the care with which the old man had asked for something he could no longer fetch himself. He had not asked for a report on other people’s claims. He had asked his son for a lamp.

A little girl appeared in the doorway. One of the neighbors’ children; Drevik had seen her twice and not learned her name.

“Did you clean it?” she asked.

He moved his hand from the case.

“What?”

“The light. She said I could clean the little lid next time.”

Drevik looked at the empty hook.

“It isn’t ready,” he said.

The girl came closer. “Can I do it when it is?”

“You’ll have to ask Veirsa.”

“She said.”

“Then yes.”

Satisfied, she went out again. Her feet slapped the court stone. Drevik heard her tell someone that it was not ready yet.

He unwrapped the lamp.

The oil jar had a narrow spout. He poured slowly, spilling a little on his fingers. He wiped the reservoir, replaced the lid, and hung it back on the hook. The bail settled against the worn place in the metal. It looked as it had before.

His replica lay on the bench where he had put it down. He returned that to the case and closed the lid.

Then he sat on the stool and waited.

Veirsa was tired when she came home. She put down a bundle of unfinished fittings, asked whether he had eaten, and began sorting the work before he answered.

“I put your lamp in my case,” he said.

Her hands stopped.

“I put it back.”

She looked at the hook. Then at the case.

“How far?”

“I hadn’t left.”

“How far had you packed it?”

“All the way.”

She turned toward him fully.

Drevik began to explain. She held up one hand.

“I know why.”

He shut his mouth.

“Did you think I wouldn’t notice?”

“No.”

“Then what did you think?”

He could not give her the answer about sending money. Not because it would make him look worse, though it would, but because saying it aloud would require her to hear it.

“I thought I’d go before you could stop me.”

Veirsa’s face changed. She looked away, then back.

“Get out of the workshop.”

He stood.

“Not the house. I am not putting you in the street. But get out of here.”

He took his case and went into the sleeping room. She did not follow.

For the next hour he heard her work. The hammer strokes were no louder than usual. He had expected her anger to remain audible, to give him something to respond to. Instead she worked on the fittings she had promised to finish.

Rethven came in later. He looked at Drevik, set a bowl beside him, and left. Drevik ate it because not eating would have been another thing for them to manage.

At supper Veirsa spoke to Eirvek about his proposed journey. She asked whether he had enough warm clothing, whether Nulith had explained the route, whether he understood that seeing the surface did not mean he would like it. Eirvek answered impatiently, then apologized when Rethven looked at him.

Drevik sat at the end of the table. Nobody asked his opinion.

Afterwards he went to help clear the bowls. Veirsa let him carry them but took the lamp herself when she returned to the workshop.

The next morning he found Aevra in the court and told her.

She listened with her hands still around the cup she had brought out. When he finished, she looked toward Veirsa’s doorway.

“Does she want you elsewhere?”

“She said I could stay.”

“Then stay if you can bear it.”

“I don’t think she wants to look at me.”

“She doesn’t have to.”

He flinched. Aevra saw it and did not apologize.

After a while she said, “Have you written to your father?”

“Yes.”

“About this?”

“No.”
