She nodded and went back inside. He had hoped she would ask what he meant to write.

Drevik spent the morning helping Thaurik pack finished vessels. Thaurik knew nothing about the lamp. He talked about firing temperatures, a crack in his kiln, whether he should take a particular shaping tool above if he went. Drevik answered and passed cloth between the pots. The work required enough attention to keep his hands occupied and not enough to keep his thoughts quiet.

At midday a vessel slipped. He caught it against his chest. Thaurik swore, then thanked him. Drevik nearly cried.

He went back to Veirsa’s house before the meal. She was at the bench, adjusting a little clasp beneath the original lamp.

“My copy leaks,” he said from the doorway.

“I know. You told me.”

“I was going to repair it before I came. I didn’t because I thought it wouldn’t matter once I had this one.”

Veirsa put down the clasp.

“Bring it.”

He fetched the case. She examined the seam, pressed the base, and asked what solder he had used.

He told her. She made a dissatisfied sound.

“That’s what I had.”

“I can see the result.”

He let that pass.

They worked separately at first. She cleaned the old seam while he cut a new piece for the worn hinge. He reached for a file and she moved it away before he touched it, then handed him another. He could not tell whether the first was unsuitable or whether she did not want him using it.

After a while she asked him to hold the reservoir steady. Their hands were close enough that he could see the little burn scar on her thumb flexing as she worked.

“I am still angry,” she said.

“I know.”

“Don’t make that sound patient.”

He looked up. “I’m not being patient.”

For the first time since the previous day, she almost smiled.

When the seam was finished, they filled the copy with water and stood it on clean cloth. It would need to remain there long enough to show a slow leak. Drevik watched the cloth until Veirsa told him watching would not improve the joint.

He asked whether he could take an impression of the original lid.

She considered it.

“Not with anything that sticks in the hinge.”

“No.”

“And I do the lid.”

“All right.”

She brought out a fine molding compound used for small fittings. He prepared a shallow bed in a scrap tray. She pressed the lid into it, working carefully around the thumb depression and the old dent, then lifted it clear.

The hollow held the lid’s worn marks in reverse. Drevik bent over the ridge where his father’s drawing showed a dent.

He had to sit down.

Veirsa took the tray away from the edge of the bench. She did not touch him. For a while he sat with both hands over his face, ashamed of the sound of his breathing and unable to stop it.

Outside, Eirvek called for Rethven. A neighbor knocked. Veirsa answered the door and dealt with the customer. Drevik stayed where he was until he could work again. Veirsa showed him how to dust the hollow before pouring the fine casting mixture. He steadied the tray while she filled it.

The cloth beneath the replica was still dry.

Chapter 29 — Houses being emptied

The first thing removed from the lower house was a door which its owner had spent eleven years meaning to replace.

He would not leave it.

“It fits,” he told Aevra, while she and Drelset worked the hinge pins free.

“You said it didn’t,” Drelset answered.

“It fits badly. That’s different from another one.”

They carried it to the waiting boat. By then his wife had decided to leave the table, which meant bringing back the boxes they had already put beneath it. The doorway stood open behind them. Inside, a lighter rectangle showed where a hanging had protected the wall from smoke.

Aevra had begun recognizing rooms by the things no longer in them.

For several days the lower terraces had been moving in pieces. Boats took people and goods to dry rooms along the downstream reach. Some returned half-empty because the destination could not yet receive another household. Some came back with boards, food and arguments. Ivrassa had arranged places for Veirsa and Rethven with people she knew, but they were still finishing work for neighbors and would not leave until the next freight departure.

Aevra spent mornings in the service approach and afternoons carrying. Her shoulders ached when she woke. Drelset complained that she had found a way to make a holiday in a lost kingdom resemble every bad job they had ever taken together.

“This is a holiday?” she asked.

“I’ve slept in four different beds.”

“Three floors and a bench.”

“I’m broadening myself.”

He was repairing the carrying chair as he spoke. Baerun had persuaded him to widen the upper crosspiece and change the position of the handholds. Eulva had tried it, pronounced it uncomfortable, and then admitted it was less uncomfortable than walking the whole way. Thaurik would carry her when he could; nobody pretended he could do it alone.

The proposed upper company had twelve names after Drelset, Aevra and Thaereth counted them, then thirteen when Uivren agreed to guide the first stages. Seven original Aeler, Uivren, Ausk, Nulith, Eirvek, Eulva and Thaurik. The number made Aevra uneasy. It also represented people who could each name a reason for going.

Uivren intended to return below before the higher galleries. She had said so twice. Aevra had written it down.

They took Ausk to the mark on the third morning of the surveys.

The service approach climbed behind the old lifting room, a place where loads had once been raised between river level and the royal stores. The lifting gear was gone. Deep grooves remained in the floor where its feet had stood. Above the chamber, a passage crossed behind the crown room and divided: one branch returned to inhabited terraces, the other rose toward the royal service stair.

The divided mark stood over that second branch.

Ausk stopped beneath it. For a moment their face seemed occupied elsewhere.

“The seam,” they said.

Aevra held the lamp higher.

Two signs had been cut on either side of a joint in the stone. One was partly hidden by later plaster. A shallow hook survived beneath them.

“That’s it?” Drevik asked.

Ausk did not answer immediately. They put a hand against the wall, withdrew it, then looked toward the bend.

“I think so.”

“Warm stone?”

“Farther.”

Uivren went ahead alone. Aevra waited with the others at the junction until the surveyor returned. There was warmth beyond the bend, she said, but the remembered stair had been altered. A later landing crossed above part of it. She could see a way to the next reach, not all the way through.

“Can we bring the chair?” Aevra asked.

“Not without changing where you carry it.”

Drelset measured the chair with a cord. It would pass the narrow length turned on its side; Eulva could not remain in it. Thaurik asked how far she would have to walk. Uivren sketched what she had seen, leaving the later climb unfinished until they could inspect it from below. He kept the board.

Ausk remained under the mark. Drevik stood beside them without speaking.

Aevra looked back once and saw Ausk rubbing the outside of their right foot through the boot.

The service approach had its own trouble.

On the left as one came up from the lifting room, a long facing wall had begun to lean into the passage. Behind it lay an old packed void. No one knew exactly how far it extended. Water had found a way through the fill, leaving dark runs at the joints and a scattering of fine grit each morning.

Drelset put three short witness pegs across the widest cracks and marked their positions. Aevra checked the ceiling where it met the wall. The roof stone itself appeared sound. The facing beneath it was not.

They could not rebuild it with the materials and time available. They could keep the passage usable for the immediate work by supporting the projecting section and restricting loads.

Vaurden listened to their proposal. “How long?”

“We don’t know,” Aevra said. “We look at it each time before anyone goes through.”

“No carts.” Drelset pointed to the inner edge of the floor. “Nothing striking here. Carry one at a time.”

Vaurden agreed. He moved the store crews to the other branch where possible and had a barrier placed below the approach when no one was working.

Uivren disliked the air behind the wall. She found a small opening near floor level and held fibers beside it. Sometimes they hung still. Sometimes they lifted toward the passage. She asked that no flame be carried close to it and that the lights be the new covered growth lamps.

“Can you tell when it changes?” Thaereth asked.

“Sometimes.”

Aevra watched the fibers.

They set the temporary shore with two borrowed beams, their feet blocked against sound floor on the opposite side. A crosspiece spread the pressure across the facing. It left a space beneath for a person to pass bent at the waist, with a wider opening at the uphill end. Aevra disliked the shape of that opening. Drelset adjusted one beam until its head bore more squarely.

“That’s better,” she said.

“It is.”

He did not sound pleased with himself. He had taken his coat off to work, and the repaired sleeve hung over a clean stone beyond the dust.

When they finished, Aevra touched the upper beam. It was still. She made herself take her hand away.

Veirsa’s house was half-packed when Aevra came to ask for a fitting for the chair.

The original lamp had been moved to a different hook because its usual place stood above a bench already dismantled. Drevik sat beside the replica, working a new piece of braid around its bail. He looked up and smiled, but only after checking Veirsa’s face.

Aevra saw that and did not ask.

Rethven was arguing with Nulith about how much food Eirvek should carry. Nulith wanted less weight. Rethven wanted his son not to be hungry. Eirvek sat between them holding a pair of boots which no longer fitted, waiting for someone to notice the more immediate problem.

Aevra noticed. She asked him to try Baerun’s spare right boot and one of Drevik’s old ones. The sizes marked inside did not match, but both boots nearly fitted. Eirvek walked across the room and looked offended by how useful the arrangement was.

“I can find you matching ones later,” Drevik said.

“You don’t know that.”

“No. I can look.”

Veirsa gave Aevra the chair fitting and told her to return the file when she had finished with it. Aevra promised. It was a small promise and she was glad of it.

On her way out, Veirsa caught her sleeve.

“Nulith knows him,” she said quietly. “When he says he’s all right, ask her too.”

“I will.”

“And don’t let him carry things just because he’s ashamed to say they’re heavy.”

Aevra nodded.

Veirsa released her. “He’ll hate me for saying that.”

“For a little while.”

“Good. Let him.”

The last inspection before the planned departure took place with Vaurden and two store workers. The facing had shed more grit. One witness peg had moved a little; Drelset reset it and put the old position beside the new in charcoal.

“Not the same mark,” he told a worker who offered to wipe the first away.

Aevra checked the beam heads. The lower one had pressed into the soft mortar and needed a thin wedge. Drelset fitted it. Uivren stood well back, watching the fibers beside the opening.

“We should finish moving this level today,” Aevra said.

Vaurden looked at the remaining stores. “We can.”

“Not tomorrow.”

“I heard you.”

He fetched more people. They moved food jars, rolled matting, a pair of tool chests, a dismantled household screen. The loads went down to the lifting room and out by the safer branch, then along the terraces to the boats. Nothing passed beneath the shore without Drelset or Aevra watching.

Near the end of the shift, Drelset’s stomach growled loudly enough for the nearest worker to laugh.

“You were promised breakfast,” Aevra said.

“I had breakfast. I’d like another.”

She went to fetch food while he stayed with the last loads. When she returned, he had put his coat on and was showing a young worker how to lift a chest without trapping a hand beneath the handle.

They ate sitting on the steps below the barrier. Drelset broke his bread in half, inspected the filling, and gave Aevra the larger piece because it had less filling in it.

She objected. He pretended not to hear.

Chapter 30 — The work that would not hold

The fibers lifted before the wall made a sound.

Uivren caught Aevra’s wrist and drew her back from the opening. They were at the lower end of the approach, just above the steps to the lifting room. Drelset stood uphill of the shore with two workers and the last chest. A third worker was passing beneath the crosspiece, empty-handed, coming down.

“Back,” Uivren said.

Aevra raised her voice. “Leave the chest. Come down.”

The worker under the shore straightened too early, struck a shoulder, swore and ducked again. Drelset put a hand on the crosspiece while the other two moved toward the wider uphill opening.

From behind the wall came a soft continuous pouring sound.

Aevra had heard loose fill running before. This was more of it than she had imagined could fit in that void.

“Drelset.”

“I hear it.”

He pushed the nearer worker ahead of him. The facing bulged. A line of mortar fell out at waist height, spilling across the floor in pale grains. The upper beam gave a sharp wooden crack but held.

The worker nearest Aevra stumbled out. She caught his arm and sent him down the steps. Behind him the second worker bent beneath the crosspiece. Drelset stayed uphill, one hand holding the light, the other against the man’s back.

A breath of warm, sour air came through the opened joint.

Uivren shouted something in her own language, then, “Out. Both.”

The second worker was through. Drelset turned sideways to follow.

The floor shifted under the foot of the upper shore.

It did not move far. Aevra saw the blocking stone slide, saw the beam tilt, saw the crosspiece twist against the facing. Then the wall came forward at the top and the wider opening narrowed to a triangular space.

Drelset vanished behind the beam.

Aevra ran three steps before Uivren caught the back of her coat.

“Wait.”

She pulled free. “Drelset!”

“I’m here.”

His voice came from low down, just beyond the fallen crosspiece. Aevra knelt. She could see one hand against the floor and the side of his face in the growth light which had fallen with him. A block lay across his lower legs. Above it, the beam had wedged between the advancing wall and the opposite side of the passage.

“Can you move?”

“My foot. No.”

“Which?”

“Both. One worse.”

He was breathing quickly. She could hear it beneath the continuing trickle of grit.

Aevra looked at the beam. It was carrying part of the wall now in a direction it had not been set to carry. Its lower end had lodged against a step in the floor. The gap beneath it was barely wide enough for her shoulders.

“Don’t come in,” Drelset said.

“I’m looking.”

“I can see you looking.”

Uivren had gone down the steps. She returned with Vaurden and Thaereth before Aevra had decided where a second support could go. The store workers were bringing another timber from the lifting room.

“Longer,” Aevra said. “We need one that reaches that shoulder.”

She pointed to sound stone uphill of the damaged section. Vaurden understood and sent one man back. Thaereth crouched beside her.

“How far is he?”

“You can see.”

“I can’t from here.”

Aevra moved enough to let her look. Drelset lifted his free hand. There was dust in his beard.

“Sorry,” he said.

“Don’t,” Aevra answered.

He closed his mouth.

They brought the longer timber. Getting it into position took time because the passage below the damage was too narrow to turn it flat. Aevra and Vaurden raised one end, fed it under the leaning beam, and tried to bring its head against the sound shoulder. It struck the trapped chest instead.

The chest they had left.

Aevra could see its corner beyond Drelset. She asked whether he could push it away.

He tried. His face changed. “No.”

“Don’t move your legs. Just—”

“I know.”

She stopped speaking.

Vaurden fetched a hooked pole. Together they caught the chest’s iron handle and dragged it sideways a few inches. Its weight scraped against Drelset’s boot. He made a sound Aevra had never heard from him.

They stopped.

“Again,” he said after a moment. “Do it.”

Aevra looked at his face. He nodded once.

The second pull cleared enough room. The new timber went past the chest and found the stone shoulder. Vaurden held it while Aevra set the foot. They had no proper socket. They built one from a block and wedges and hoped the floor beneath it was what it appeared to be.

Thaereth sent the unneeded workers out of the lifting room. Their voices diminished below.

Aevra put weight on the new shore with a wedge. It tightened. The first beam stopped making its little rubbing noise.

She lay on her stomach and reached through.

Drelset’s hand was warm. He caught hers too hard, then loosened his grip.

“I need to see the block,” she said.

“It’s long. On the right. The left one’s caught under it.”

His left boot. The one she could not see.

She pushed the light farther in with a short stick. The block crossed both shins at an angle, but most of its weight rested on rubble beyond him. If they could raise the nearer end a little, he might draw his legs back. The space would not admit a second person beside him.

She asked for a lever.

They had the narrow hammer from Hesh’s settlement, useless for this, and a borrowed iron bar too short to reach from outside. Aevra would have to go in to place it.

Uivren said her name.

“There’s no other place to put it.”

“The air is changing.”

“I know.”

“No. You are hearing me. That isn’t—”

Aevra crawled forward before the sentence ended.

The gap scraped her back. Dust entered her mouth. She could smell Drelset’s sweat and the sour air from the wall. She put the bar’s end beneath the block and found a piece of stone for a fulcrum. Her elbow had no room to move. She shifted, pressing one shoulder against the underside of the beam.

“Don’t touch that,” Drelset said.

“I know.”

She lifted.

The bar moved. The block did not. She reset the fulcrum, trying to give herself more travel. Drelset had begun to shake. He pressed his lips together and watched her hands.

Behind them someone coughed. Uivren was telling Thaereth to move back.

Aevra lifted again. This time the block rose a little.

“Now.”

Drelset dragged his right leg toward him. His boot came clear. The left remained caught.

“Again,” he said.

She could not hold the angle. Her hand slipped on the bar. The block settled, not back where it had been but lower on the rubble beyond. Drelset cried out and struck the floor with his free hand.

Aevra tried to reset the bar. The space beneath the block had closed.

“I can get another stone,” she said.

The new shore moved.

She felt it through the floor before she heard it, a short harsh vibration. Above her, the first beam rolled against the wall. Grit struck the back of her neck.

“Aevra,” Thaereth shouted.

Drelset pushed at her shoulder.

“Out.”

She held the bar. It seemed important not to lose the bar. She could not afterwards remember why.

Hands caught her ankles. She kicked once, trying to keep her place, then the beam lowered enough to press her coat against her spine. She let go of the iron and pushed backwards with both palms.

They pulled her clear as the foot of the new shore broke through the thin floor crust beneath its blocking stone.

The timber dropped. Its head slipped off the shoulder. The first beam took the changed weight for perhaps a breath, then split lengthwise with a report which filled the passage.

Aevra rolled against the opposite wall. Stone struck where her head had been. The triangular gap closed to a black slit. The light beside Drelset vanished.

She got to her knees.

Uivren stood in front of her with both hands against her chest.

“No.”

“Move.”

“No.”

Aevra tried to go around her. Vaurden caught one arm and Thaereth the other. She shouted Drelset’s name. No answer came through the falling grit.

The sour air thickened. Someone below was coughing continuously. Uivren pushed all three of them toward the steps. Aevra resisted until her foot found empty space where the edge of the tread had broken. Then she went down because they were going down and because she could no longer draw a full breath.

They waited in the outer court while the air cleared enough to approach again.

Aevra sat on the floor. Her hands kept closing. Kouven washed her eyes and made her drink. She asked him where the bar was. He told her it was not important. She nearly struck him.

Drevik arrived and knelt beside her. She looked at him without knowing what he had said.

Vaurden and Uivren returned to the lower steps with covered lights. Thaereth went with them. Aevra stood to follow, and Kouven caught her sleeve.

“They’ll call you.”

“They don’t know where he is.”

“They were there.”

She pulled away, then stopped at the doorway because her legs had begun to tremble too much to carry her up the first step.

When the others came back, Thaereth had dust along one side of her face.

The passage was filled above the waist. More material was moving behind the broken facing. They could not hear anything. They could not reach the place where he had been without taking away stone which was holding the rest.

Aevra asked whether they had tried calling from the upper branch.

Vaurden said the upper branch could be reached by the long circuit, but its opening into the damaged approach was buried too. He would send people to listen from there.

She wanted to go with them. Uivren said she would go. Aevra looked at her with such hatred that the surveyor’s face tightened, but she went anyway.

They listened from both sides. They called. The answer was water somewhere behind the fall and small intermittent movements of stone.

By the time darkness would have fallen above, if their count of days was right, the barrier below the approach had been moved farther back. Vaurden posted two people there to keep anyone from entering alone.

Drelset’s coat was in the court. He had taken it off after the first load that morning. The sleeve they had mended together lay folded beneath it.

Thaereth found Aevra at the washing place.

She had been cleaning the borrowed tools. She could not stop feeling grit under the handles.

“We had a moment before the second support went,” Thaereth said.

Aevra put down the brush.

“To do what?”

“I don’t know. Pull harder. Shift the block from above.”

“There wasn’t room.”

“I know what you said.”

“What I saw.”

Thaereth’s face was gray with fatigue. “I keep seeing his hand.”

Aevra looked back at the tools.

“So do I.”

For a while neither spoke. Then Thaereth said, “If I’d brought the longer timber first—”

“The floor went.”

“Later.”

“It was already going.”

“You don’t know that.”

Aevra turned on her. “Neither do you.”

The words echoed from the basin. Thaereth stepped back. Aevra wanted her to answer, to become someone she could be angry with without having to change the subject of her anger. The officer only looked down at her own dirty hands.

“Give me the brush,” she said.

Aevra did.

They washed in silence. Thaereth could not get the dust out of one crease beside her thumb. She scrubbed until the skin reddened. Aevra took the brush away and set it on the ledge.

Later, Drevik brought food. He put three bowls down and sat beside them. No one ate until it had gone nearly cold.

Drelset’s share of the next day’s work was still marked on Vaurden’s slate. Aevra saw the mark when the keeper came to ask about the chair. He had already begun to rub it away and stopped when he noticed her looking.

“I’ll do the fitting,” she said.

Vaurden nodded. He did not ask about the rest.

She picked up Drelset’s coat. The mended sleeve came unfolded, showing the uneven little stitches. She folded it again, this time on top where they would not be crushed, and carried it to the room where they slept.

Part V — The stair of returning

Chapter 31 — What stays lit

The cast would not come out of its bed.

Drevik had loosened all four edges and turned the tray over a folded cloth. Nothing happened. He tapped the back once, lightly. Veirsa caught his wrist before he could tap again.

“Wait.”

He put the tray down. Across the room, Eirvek was trying to close a bag around a bundle which was plainly too large for it. Nulith watched without helping. The workshop had been emptied to the point where each small noise returned from the walls. Veirsa’s tools lay in two open boxes. The original lamp stood between them, lit for the last work before packing.

They waited. The casting compound had dried at the edges and remained damp beneath. Drevik had known that when he began. He had wanted it ready because everything else seemed to be leaving.

Rethven came in carrying the straps for the tool boxes. He asked Veirsa which boat had their places. She told him again. He nodded before she had finished.

Outside, someone called for a missing lid.

Eirvek swore at his bag.

“Take it out,” Nulith said.

“I can fit it.”

“Then fit it where you can carry it.”

The boy undid the cord. Inside the bundle was a little jointed model of a lifting frame, made of thin rods and wooden wheels. Drevik had seen it on a shelf without asking who had made it. One wheel had been painted blue.

“Does it work?” he asked.

“It did before he packed it,” Veirsa said.

Eirvek looked at her. She turned away to wrap a file.

Drevik lifted the model. It was lighter than it looked, awkward because of its shape rather than its weight. The arm folded if one pin was removed. He showed Eirvek how to place the pin inside a twist of cloth rather than leaving it loose in the bag. Together they wrapped the folded frame and fitted it against the boy’s spare shirt.

Nulith shook her head but said nothing. Drevik had his own case beside his feet.

Aevra arrived with the packing list and stood in the doorway as if she had forgotten why she had come.

Veirsa put a hand against her back and moved her gently out of the way of Rethven’s box. Aevra apologized. Nobody answered the apology.

They were leaving the next morning. The lower boats would go first, while the river remained manageable. The upper party would take the long circuit behind the terraces, avoiding the buried service approach. Vaurden had sent two people ahead to clear the connection to the royal stair. They had returned, wet and tired, with news that it could still be reached.

There were twelve on Aevra’s list. She read the names once without looking up. Drevik heard the space where Drelset had been.

Supplies had been laid out in Nulith’s room: six days of food with another day’s reserve divided among three packs, water vessels to be filled at known points, two newly purchased lengths of rope, slings for the chair, a compact block and tackle borrowed from the boat crews. Heavy tools had been pared down to what Aevra and Thaereth believed they could carry. Uivren would guide them to a lower station she knew, where they could replenish before the final climb if the stores keeper had anything to spare.

“If,” Baerun had said when told.

“Yes,” Uivren had answered.

Drevik was to carry his case and a food packet. He offered to take more. Aevra looked at his shoulders, then said she would decide after seeing him walk with what he had.

“You don’t have to make it equal,” Veirsa told him when she had gone.

“I wasn’t.”

She raised one eyebrow. He went back to the cast.

It came free shortly before the meal. The lid’s dent showed clearly, as did the worn place beside the thumb depression and the edge of the old repair. Drevik held it toward the light. Veirsa inspected a thin corner and told him to support it there. They made a little backing board and tied the cast to it with cloth strips.

The replica had burned through a full test without a leak. Its new wick needed trimming. Veirsa let Drevik do that while she wrapped the original. He watched her lower it into a box with the household’s cups, padding the bail so it would not strike the lid.

“Will it travel well?” he asked.

“It has before.”

He nodded.

She put a hand on the box, not yet closing it.

“Write when you reach him.”

“I will.”

“Even if it takes a long time to send.”

“Yes.”

She closed the box and tied it. He did not tell her again that he was sorry. He had already told her, and he did not want to ask her for a different answer on their last evening.

They ate with the door open because the room was warm from bodies and packing. Neighbors came and went. Someone brought a dish which no one had room for until it was set down, and then everyone found room. Eulva complained about the salt and took another helping. Thaurik had clay beneath his nails from a last visit to his kiln. He ate without talking much.

Afterwards Eirvek asked Drevik to sing the surface verse. Drevik began, lost a line, and heard Nulith supply a different one. It fitted badly. Rethven tried to make it fit, dragging the tune out until Veirsa laughed and told him to stop.

Aevra sat by the door with a bowl in her hands. Drevik could not tell whether she was listening. When the song came round again, her lips moved on a few words.

Later, while they cleared the dishes, she asked him whether he had space for Drelset’s coat.

“Yes.”

“Keep it dry.”

“I will.”

She handed it over folded inside another cloth. It weighed very little. He put it above the lamp case, where he could reach it without opening everything else.

At the landing in the morning, Veirsa embraced Nulith first. They spoke into each other’s shoulders, too quietly for Drevik to hear. Then she took Eirvek’s face between her hands. He bore it, embarrassed, until she let go; then he hugged her hard enough to make her shift her feet.

Rethven had been checking a strap which needed no more checking. He came over and put both arms around his son. They stood that way longer than either appeared to have intended.

Drevik looked toward the river. The lower boats were already loaded. In one, the workshop boxes lay beneath a stretched cover. He knew which contained the lamp.

Veirsa turned to him.

“Come back if you can,” she said.

He had prepared several things to say. None survived that.

“I’d like to.”

She embraced him. He held her carefully at first, then less carefully when she tightened her arms.

“Look after that seam,” she said when she let go.

He laughed, and the laugh hurt.

Ivrassa called for the last passengers. She had found room for a packet of their messages with a trader going toward an eastern junction; the trader promised to hand them on, not to deliver them personally. Thaereth kept copies. The pilot’s oil debt had been paid from the remaining common money before he took his boat downriver. Drevik watched both transactions because he wanted to know that some small things had been finished.

The boats moved out. Eirvek walked along the landing until the stone ended. Nulith waited beside him. Aevra did not call them back until they turned of their own accord.

Vaurden waited at the first turn with a packet wrapped in oiled cloth. He gave it to Thaereth, then asked her to repeat the address of the upper office to which she meant to take it. She did. He corrected the name of his own office on the outside; she had written the old royal designation from the book, and the people now keeping the records used another.

Drevik helped him retie the packet.

The young worker from the room Aevra had repaired was there too. The uncle had come back on the previous boat, and the bell was his. Not his by inheritance, the worker explained when Thaereth asked, but his boat’s bell, bought from a store which sold old fittings. He had described the cracked lip and the ugly washer without being shown their drawing.

“He wants it returned?” Aevra asked.

“He wants to know whether you mean to pay for keeping it.”

Thaereth blinked. Vaurden laughed, then covered the laugh with a cough when he saw the officer’s expression.

“We haven’t stolen it,” she said.

“No one said you had,” the worker answered. “But he’s bought another. He doesn’t need two.”

Drevik saw Aevra begin to smile and then stop. Drelset would have enjoyed this.

Thaereth wrote the claim down, including the price of the replacement, to be verified later. The worker asked her to read the price back. Then he thanked her and went to catch his boat.

Vaurden shook each hand he could reach. When he came to Aevra, he used her right.

“I’ve marked the long way in white,” he said. “Not the red work marks. Those lead back to the lifting room.”

“I remember.”

“I know. I’m saying it anyway.”

She squeezed his hand. He held hers a moment longer, then released it.

At the turn, Drevik looked back for the household boats. They had gone beyond the reach visible from the terraces. The river was still full of work: a load being lowered, an empty boat turning, two people disputing a rope. He adjusted the case against his hip and followed the others.

They climbed through the terraces with twelve people, the chair empty for the first easy stretch. Behind them the landing voices faded into the sound of water.

Chapter 32 — A road remembered

Ausk stopped beneath the warm wall and looked up.

“There should be a landing.”

There was a landing. It stood well above their heads, its underside carried on two beams of stone. The stair which ought to have reached it had been cut away. In its place ran a shallow channel full of old mineral deposits.

Aevra set down her pack. Eulva lowered herself onto a sound step and began unfastening the shawl she had tied around her waist. They had been climbing for nearly an hour. The warmth from the wall made the air close, though Uivren said there was no reason to hurry through this part.

“How high?” Aevra asked Ausk.

They held a hand out at waist level.

“From where we stand?”

“Yes. I think.”

Drevik said, “Could the floor have been lower?”

“Higher,” Aevra said. “Or the landing replaced.”

Ausk pressed their fingers against their eyelids.

“Don’t force it,” she added.

“I am trying to separate what I see from what I have.”

She left them to that and examined the stone. The cut stair was older than the channel. Tool marks remained beneath the deposits where someone had dressed the broken ends smooth. The landing above belonged to the later work.

Uivren went along the channel to its far end. Aevra could hear her testing the floor with a short rod. When she returned, she pointed toward a low opening behind a projecting slab.

“Up there. Then across.”

“For all of us?”

“We will see.”

Aevra went with her, leaving Thaereth to keep the others clear of the channel. The opening led into a narrow rising space between two walls. There were footholds cut into one side, little worn hollows which might once have served maintenance workers. At the top, a gap opened onto the back of the landing.

Aevra climbed until she could see over its lip. Sound stone. Enough room to stand. A short continuation of the stair beyond it, turning out of sight.

She came down slowly. Her hands left pale prints on the wall.

“People can climb,” she said. “The chair can’t.”

“Not as it is,” Uivren agreed.

They dismantled the chair where Eulva could watch.

“I’ve only just got used to it,” she said.

“We’ll put it back.”

“That’s what Thaurik said about my shelf.”

Her son looked up. “I did put it back.”

“Lower.”

“So you could reach it.”

“I could reach it before.”

He continued undoing the fastening. Aevra let them quarrel.

The two long side pieces could be passed up separately. The seat and back went in a sling. Eulva would need to climb the first few footholds with help, then sit on a short board secured across a level section while they helped her through the final gap. Baerun could manage the rise if someone took the weight off his injured leg when he turned.

Aevra described it before moving anyone. Eulva asked to see the footholds. Nulith brought the light closer. The old woman looked, put a hand against the wall, and tried the first step.

“Not with everyone looking,” she said.

The unnecessary watchers moved away. Thaurik stayed. She did not ask him to go.

They raised the packs first. Eirvek climbed above with Uivren and passed each load back from the edge. Drevik followed with the lamp case, then came down again to help. Thaereth checked the sling knot, loosened it, tied it once more. Aevra watched without saying that the first knot had been good.

Eulva’s ascent took a long time. Her knee would not bend enough for one foothold, and the alternative required her to turn her body toward the wall. She grew angry with the wall, then with Thaurik, then with Aevra for suggesting a pause.

“I am already paused.”

“I mean sit here.”

“There isn’t here.”

There was, but Aevra had failed to show her. Nulith guided the old woman’s hand to the board, let her feel its breadth, and waited until she trusted it enough to lower her weight.

After that they moved more slowly and spoke less.

When Eulva reached the landing, she lay back on her folded shawl and shut her eyes. Thaurik knelt beside her. She pushed his hand away once, then caught it before he withdrew it entirely.

Baerun came next. At the turn, his foot slipped from the damp stone and he struck his shin against the wall. Kouven caught his hip while Aevra held the sling. The old wound did not open, but he was shaking when they got him seated.

“No more today,” Kouven said.

They still had to find somewhere to sleep. Aevra looked along the landing. Uivren was already going ahead to look.

The shelter was a former work recess beyond the next bend. It had no door and a floor just wide enough for their bedding if the packs went against the back wall. There was clean seep water in a basin Uivren inspected before allowing them to fill bottles. No one called it comfortable, but no one objected.

They rebuilt the chair. Thaurik insisted on doing most of it himself. Eirvek handed him the pieces in the wrong order until Eulva began directing from her cover.

Ausk sat apart, looking at the turn they had passed.

Aevra brought them food.

“I said waist height,” they said.

“Yes.”

“I could feel the hand resting on it.”

“It might have been there.”

Ausk took the bowl. “You believed me.”

“I believed you remembered it. We still had to look.”

They ate for a while without answering. Then they asked whether the changed landing meant the mark was wrong too.

“No. The mark is there.”

Ausk nodded. Aevra went back to the others.

Uivren returned from her survey carrying a small fragment of pale stone. She used it to draw their next route on the floor: down to a bypass, across the hot reach, into a cooler passage, then up toward the lower station. The upper continuation of the royal stair was cut off from this landing by a fall which she would not ask them to enter.

“So we go down again,” Thaurik said.

“For a while.”

He looked at his mother’s chair and said nothing else.

The crossing interval came from the river crews’ observations. Uivren knew the approach and the places to wait; she did not control what moved through the fire. They would rest here, travel the next stages, and decide at the actual crossing whether it remained usable.

Aevra listened until she could follow the route without the drawing. She repeated it for Thaereth, who had been helping Kouven. Ildret asked about light near the hot reach. Uivren answered, then made her repeat the answer because she had omitted the part about covering it.

They spent the next day learning how slowly twelve people could move through a route which one person could inspect in an hour.

The descent from the recess included three short drops, each too high for Eulva and awkward for Baerun. At the first they argued about whether to dismantle the chair again. At the second no one argued; they had learned the time it took. At the third Eulva asked to be allowed to try the steps before they began undoing everything.

Thaurik disliked the idea. She disliked his dislike. Aevra stayed out of it until the old woman put a foot down and the knee would not hold. Then she helped her sit without announcing who had been right.

They ate in a passage lined with pale mineral ribs. Water had shaped them into folds so fine that the light showed through the thinnest edges. Drevik held the lamp at different angles until a whole length of wall changed from gray to amber. Eirvek went quiet. Even Uivren waited while they looked.

Farther along, the ribs thickened and joined, making the passage seem roofed by the inside of an enormous shell. Their footsteps traveled strangely through it, neither echoing nor dying away, but spreading into a soft continuous murmur. Aevra ran her right hand along one smooth curve. It was cold enough to numb her fingertips.

She had no useful thing to say about it. She was glad no one asked.

The next shelter was smaller than the first. Baerun and Thaurik had to sleep with their feet beneath a projecting shelf. Ausk offered to take the awkward place, but the hauler said he was already there and would not get up to be grateful. It sounded enough like his earlier self that Kouven smiled.

Aevra checked the food with Nulith. They were using it faster than the walking distances suggested. Carrying, stopping and waiting all required meals. They agreed to cook the packet which had become damp rather than risk another day’s spoilage. Nulith made it taste better than Aevra expected, adding a small quantity of something from her own bag.

“What is that?”

“Mine.”

Aevra laughed. “I meant the food.”

“So did I.”

Nulith gave her another spoonful before telling her its name.

Later, after they had eaten, Eirvek sat beside Drevik and took out the little lifting model. One joint had loosened in the climb. Drevik held it while the boy repaired it. The model’s blue wheel turned, stopped, turned again.

Aevra watched from her bedding. Drelset would have told them the angle was wrong. She could hear exactly how he would have said it, amused and unable to leave the thing alone. She pulled her cover up and closed her eyes before either of them noticed her looking.

Chapter 33 — The crossing interval

They waited above the fire for half a day.

The waiting place was a hollow behind a rib of stone, high enough that the worst heat passed below it. From there Aevra could see only the middle of the crossing: a shelf descending toward a narrow bridge of black rock, then rising again into vapour. Uivren had gone to inspect both approaches. She returned with dust on her sleeves and said the far handholds were sound.

Aevra asked about the ones she could not see.

“Those are the ones I mean.”

She wanted to go and look. Her legs still trembled from the descent to the hollow. She stayed with the chair.

Eulva had fallen asleep. Thaurik sat beside her, one hand resting on the chair’s upper rail. He had a small box of tools beneath his knees, chosen after rejecting and repacking several others. Aevra could hear them shift when he moved.

The rest of the loads lay against the wall. They had taken two iron fittings recovered near the stair because Vaurden wanted upper workers to see the difference between the old linkage and the later one. Thaereth had labeled each. Now she moved them from her pack to a separate cloth and looked at Aevra.

“Too much?”

“Across that, yes.”

Thaereth touched one of the labels. Then she put the fittings in a dry recess and copied their dimensions onto the page already carrying the marks.

No one offered to take them instead.

The air slowly cooled. Not enough to be comfortable; enough that the sweat on Aevra’s neck stopped running. Uivren rose, watched the vapour for a long time, and called them forward.

They moved in the order agreed. Uivren first to the far anchor, with one rope. Aevra and Thaereth next to establish the near end. Then the chair, carried by Thaurik and Nulith with Drevik and Eirvek helping on the broader stretches. Ildret controlled the lights. Ausk and Kouven stayed with Baerun until the chair was across. Nobody would overtake.

The first descent was easier than it looked from above. There were steps cut into the shelf, worn at their centers. Aevra kept her left hand on the inner wall and her eyes on the next place for her feet. Beyond the edge, red light moved under a dark crust. Heat rose around her calves.

At the anchor Uivren had indicated, she found an iron ring set deep in stone. Its surface had scaled but the buried neck held when she tested it. Thaereth joined her. Together they took up the slack of the rope Uivren had carried across and secured it at chest height.

The surveyor stood on the far rise and made a short downward motion with her hand.

“Lower?” Thaereth asked.

Aevra lowered it. Uivren shook her head and pointed beyond them.

“Bodies,” Aevra said. “Keep low.”

They repeated the signal back. Uivren nodded.

The chair came carefully, Eulva gripping its arms. Thaurik had wrapped cloth around the lower rail where it bit his hand. Nulith walked backwards over the first two steps, then turned when the shelf widened. Aevra went beside the chair with a short safety sling passed around the frame and over the handline. It would not lift the chair. It might keep it from going over if someone slipped.

Halfway across, the red beneath them brightened.

Aevra heard a noise from the far bank, a sharp rhythmic striking. Uivren had begun hitting the stone with her rod.

“Keep moving,” Aevra said.

Thaurik looked down.

“Look at her,” she told him. “Not there.”

He fixed his eyes on his mother’s face. Eulva was staring straight ahead, her mouth open a little, breathing with care.

The brightness traveled along a seam below the bridge. Farther out, a shape rose where no shape had been. Aevra could not tell whether it had emerged or become visible because the surrounding stone had darkened. It was moving toward the span, not toward them.

Uivren struck the rock again. Then she shouted, “Left. Under.”

The route bent left beneath a projection which hid the far steps. Aevra could not see whether the chair would fit. She passed the instruction on. Nulith obeyed first, pulling the forward end into the shadow. The chair tilted. Eulva cried out, and Thaurik stopped.

“Go,” Aevra said. “I have this side.”

She did not have enough of it. She had a sling and one hand on the frame, but he moved because she said it, and the projection passed above Eulva’s head with scarcely room for the folded shawl.

A gust hit the exposed shelf behind them. Drevik bent double. Eirvek caught the back of his coat and stayed there, his own face turned into his sleeve.

The thing crossed below. Aevra saw a long jointed limb touch the dark surface and leave it shining. She felt the movement in the handline, through the anchor, through stone which had seemed immovable a moment before.

The chair reached the far rise. Uivren caught its forward rail and helped lift it clear. Aevra unfastened the sling. Her fingers were clumsy. She had to force herself to do one movement at a time.

Baerun was still on the near side.

She turned back.

Kouven and Ausk had brought him to the bridge. He held the handline with both hands, his stick tied to his pack. Ildret followed, the light closed. Thaereth stood at the near anchor, waiting for them to pass before she freed the rope.

“Leave it,” Aevra shouted.

Thaereth looked across.

“Leave it.”

The officer nodded. She put a hand behind Baerun’s shoulder and followed him onto the bridge.

The next gust came lower. It struck their legs. Baerun lost his footing and hung on the line, dragging Kouven sideways. Ausk dropped to one knee and caught the hauler’s belt. For a moment all four stopped in a shape Aevra knew could not hold. Baerun’s pack dragged at his shoulders. Thaereth cut its strap while Kouven held him; the load struck the shelf and slid out of sight, taking his stick with it.

Aevra went back far enough to throw the short sling. Kouven caught it. They passed it under Baerun’s arms. Aevra pulled from the far rise while Ausk and Thaereth got his good foot beneath him.

“Walk,” Kouven said. “One more.”

The hauler moved. Then again.

Ildret came last, so close behind Thaereth that their shoulders touched. The officer left both near fastening and rope where they were. On the far side Uivren cut the handline clear of the path when it began to drag loose stone from its heated anchor. There was no time to recover the length.

They climbed until the light beneath them disappeared.

The first water they found was too warm to drink pleasantly and clean enough that no one cared.

Kouven made them drink slowly. Eirvek ignored him and was sick against the wall. Nulith held his hair out of his face, then gave him a little more water and waited before giving him another mouthful. The boy began to cry with anger at his own body. She let him.

Aevra counted twelve twice.

Thaurik had a blister across the base of one thumb where the carrying rail had grown hot. Eulva’s cheek was reddened. Drevik’s eyebrows smelled scorched, though he insisted nothing hurt. Baerun sat with his head between his knees until Kouven persuaded him to lean back.

Thaereth stared at the place where the rope had been tied to her pack.

“We have the other length,” Aevra said.

“Not as long.”

“I know.”

The smaller rope remained. Two chair slings were abraded, one scorched. The compact tackle had gone with a pack they abandoned when Baerun fell; it had been lashed to his load to balance another bag and no one had thought to move it before the crossing. Aevra remembered approving that arrangement. She did not say so yet.

Ausk unfastened their coat and found a burn along one forearm. It was shallow, but long. Kouven cleaned and dressed it while they watched his hands.

“You held him,” Baerun said.

“Yes.”

“Thank you.”

Ausk nodded, then hissed as the water touched the raw skin.

Uivren went ahead again. Aevra wanted to call her back, to make everyone stay in sight until the shaking stopped. She watched the surveyor disappear round the bend instead.

Drevik sat beside her and put a piece of food in her hand. She chewed without tasting it.

After a while he said, “My sleeve.”

She looked. Eirvek had torn the seam holding him beneath the projection. Three finger marks remained in the dust.

“I can mend that,” she said.

“I wasn’t asking.”

He folded the torn part inward and finished his food. Aevra ate the rest of hers. When Uivren returned, there was still no safe place to sleep nearby. They stood, lifted the chair and went on.

Chapter 34 — The thing behind the load

The cooler passage smelled of wet stone and something animal.

Aevra noticed the second smell before Uivren stopped. It was sourer than the river, with the close rankness of a den. Along the inner wall lay small pale bones, some old and dry, others clean enough to shine. Tiny movements withdrew from the light ahead of them.

Uivren raised a hand. Everyone stopped except the back of the chair, which Thaurik lowered a moment later with a muttered apology to Eulva.

“Stay close,” the surveyor said. “Light down.”

Ildret covered the brighter lamp. The passage changed shape as shadows widened. Aevra could hear claws or hard feet moving somewhere beyond the bend, several little sets, then silence.

“They’re going away,” Eirvek whispered.

“Let them,” Nulith said.

They continued at a pace Baerun could manage. The passage opened into a chamber crossed by a raised ridge. On the far side, the route climbed over a broken lip into a higher crawl. Uivren had expected an easier step. Recent fragments lay beneath it, their edges bright.

Aevra examined the climb. They could get people up, but the chair would need to be passed separately. There was no time to take it fully apart. They could remove the side rails, lift the seat upright, and turn it through.

Behind them, something exhaled.

She turned her head slowly.

The wyrm occupied the mouth of the passage they had left. Its head was low. A line of pale plates ran above its eyes and down the neck. The body behind it was thicker than a person, close to the floor, moving with a slow muscular ripple which drew the hind limbs forward. It stopped where the light reached its face.

No one spoke.

Aevra thought of the trained animal in the market, the handler taking food out of its path, the instant attention to a shifting pot. There was no collar here and no person it watched for a signal.

“Up,” Uivren said softly.

Drevik helped Eirvek reach the lip. The boy climbed and turned to receive packs. Thaereth followed. They began passing loads with a haste which made the buckles rattle.

Ildret moved the lamp behind a projection so its light would not fall directly into the animal’s eyes. The wyrm lifted its head, tasting the air. Small creatures scattered beneath it, and it snapped at one so quickly that Aevra saw only the closing jaws.

That gave them a few moments.

Nulith and Thaurik helped Eulva stand. She looked at the lip, then at the animal, and made no complaint about being lifted. Aevra put her good shoulder beneath the old woman’s hip while Thaurik raised from the other side. Above, Drevik and Thaereth took her arms. It was awkward and hurtful and succeeded.

The chair caught when they turned it upright.

“Leave the rail,” Aevra said.

Thaurik pulled the pin. The rail came free and fell with a crack against the floor.

The wyrm turned toward them.

Ausk tore open a food packet and flung handfuls toward the far side of the chamber. The animal followed the movement, stopped, then lowered its head to the scattered strips. It ate one. The next vanished with a brief crunch.

“Baerun,” Aevra said.

He was already moving. Kouven went ahead to help from the lip. The shorter remaining rope was too short to make the lifting arrangement she would have preferred. They looped it beneath his arms and used hands on the other end. Baerun pushed with his good leg, reached, slipped, and swore at them to pull.

They pulled.

Aevra saw the blood begin at the edge of his dressing when the bad shin struck stone. Then he was up, dragged clear by Kouven and Drevik.

Uivren climbed next. Ausk followed, the dressed forearm held awkwardly away from the rock. Ildret was still below with Aevra, protecting the light.

The animal had finished the food.

It moved toward the ridge. Aevra took the loose chair rail from the floor and held it across her body. She had no sensible use for it except to place something between its mouth and herself.

“Ildret. Go.”

The lamp worker set the closed light on the lip and climbed. Her boot slipped, found a hold, rose. Aevra backed toward the wall.

The wyrm came faster over the ridge. Its belly scraped stone. She thrust the rail at its face and the jaws closed on the end with a force which tore it sideways out of her grip. The broken end struck her left hand. She felt something give beneath the skin.

She had no time to look.

Above her, Uivren shouted. Aevra turned and jumped for the lip. Her left hand closed and failed. The pain was immediate and white. She hung from the right until Drevik caught her wrist.

A pressure struck her boot. Teeth scraped leather. She kicked with the other foot and felt the sole connect with something hard. The wyrm jerked back, dragging the boot halfway off. She let it go.

Drevik and Ildret pulled. Her ribs struck the edge. She could not get a knee up. Then Nulith had the back of her belt and they hauled her over, scraping her stomach along the stone.

They dragged her beyond the lip before letting her turn.

Below, the animal struck the wall once with its head. Stone rattled into the chamber. Its body was too broad for the angle of the climb, but Aevra could see its forelimbs searching for purchase. They did not wait to learn what it could manage.

Her hand had begun swelling before they found a recess.

Kouven cut the glove away. Two fingers were bent at angles which made her look elsewhere. Blood ran from a split over the knuckle. He asked her to move them; she told him what she thought of the request.

“I need to know.”

“I can’t.”

He nodded and stopped asking.

They washed the wound with water they could ill afford. He supported the fingers, bound the hand so it could not close, and made a sling from the inside of a spare shirt. Aevra watched a drop of blood reach her wrist and gather there until Nulith wiped it away.

Her left boot was gone. Her sock had been torn but the foot beneath was only scraped. Eirvek offered one of his mismatched spare shoes, then remembered he was wearing them. Thaurik unfastened a soft outer shoe he used around the kiln and gave it to her. It was too large. They packed cloth around her toes and tied it firmly.

The chair was missing a side rail. They could carry it with a substituted pole if they found one, or use it on the flatter stretches with a short sling taking the load. Aevra began explaining, then stopped because Kouven was looking at her.

“Someone else can.”

“I know how it fits.”

“So does Baerun.”

The hauler sat pale and angry while his own dressing was changed. He lifted his head.

“Give it here.”

They brought him the chair.

Aevra lay back. Pain reached from her fingers to her elbow in waves which did not quite leave between arrivals. She could hear everyone moving, eating, repairing. The work went on without her hands.

After a while Thaereth crouched beside her.

“Can you walk?”

“Yes.”

“All of us?”

She looked at Eulva wrapped in the shawl, at Baerun bending over the chair, at the remaining packs, opened and rearranged.

“I don’t know the next part,” she said.

Thaereth stayed beside her. She did not ask the question differently.

They had to rest longer than Uivren wanted. The next passage required hands on the rock and Aevra could not bear even the motion of standing. Kouven gave her something bitter from the little bottle he had saved through every lost pack and spoiled dressing. It did not remove the pain. After a while it made the pain less able to occupy every thought.

Nulith sat beside her and repaired the torn sock. The scrape on Aevra’s foot had been cleaned and covered; the sock needed a patch so the dressing would not work loose in the oversized shoe. She sewed without asking Aevra whether she minded the color of the thread.

Aevra watched the needle. At home she would have taken it and finished the job herself rather than sit idle. Here even accepting the mended sock required Nulith’s help to get it on.

“Thank you,” she said.

Nulith nodded. “Tell me if it rubs.”

Across the recess, Eirvek had begun to shake. He was trying to conceal it by looking into his bag. Drevik noticed and sat beside him. Aevra could not hear their conversation. After a while the boy handed him the little model and went to sit near his aunt without pretending he had another reason.

Thaurik and Baerun finished the chair repair. The substitute was ugly: a shortened piece of packing wood, a crossed sling, the remaining sound rail taking more weight. They tested it with a pack before asking Eulva to sit. The first attempt tilted. Baerun swore at himself, unfastened the sling and began again.

Aevra held still. It was harder than she expected not to give an opinion.

The second attempt held. Eulva settled into it, complained about the angle, and then said it would do. Thaurik closed his eyes briefly.

Only after they had packed did Aevra discover that Ildret had kept the broken chair pin. She asked why.

“The metal’s sound,” Ildret said. “We might need it.”

It was an answer Aevra would once have given without thinking. She looked at the small object in the lamp worker’s palm and asked her to wrap the sharp end.

When they moved, Aevra carried no pack. Drevik had taken it without discussion. She wanted it back until she tried to stand and found how much of standing she ordinarily did with both hands.

They traveled for less than an hour before stopping again.

Aevra hated the halt because she had caused it. Her borrowed shoe turned on a sloping patch and she had put out the injured hand without thinking. Nothing had struck it. The sudden movement was enough. She stood bent over the sling, unable to speak, while the others lowered their loads.

Eulva looked at her from the chair and said, “It’s a poor seat, isn’t it?”

Aevra stared.

“Needing everyone to stop.”

She would have disliked the remark from anyone else. From Eulva it made her sit down.

They were on a broad shelf with a low ceiling. No animal smell reached it, only damp cloth and the food bag Ausk had opened for the diversion. One wrapper still held a few strips pressed into its folds. Nulith picked them out carefully and shared them between the people carrying the chair.

Thaurik ate standing. His burned thumb had begun bleeding where the sling rubbed it. He noticed Aevra looking and turned the hand away.

“Kouven,” she began.

“He’s doing it when we stop properly.”

“We’ve stopped.”

“For you.”

The words came out harder than he seemed to intend. He looked at the floor. Eulva closed her eyes.

Aevra said, “Then use it.”

He sat beside Kouven and held out the hand. Neither looked at her again for a while.

Drevik had put down both packs. One strap had cut a red line along his neck. He rolled his shoulder and winced. Aevra asked for her bag back.

“No.”

“You could give me something from it.”

“Later.”

He sounded like Maeven when she had decided an argument was finished. Aevra almost said so, then thought how much she would give to hear Maeven being unreasonable about anything at all.

They changed the loads instead. Ildret took food from Drevik’s pack, Nulith took the little tool roll, and Ausk carried the damaged light so it would not be crushed beneath the heavier things. Aevra held the loose ties while they worked. It was a task she could do sitting. Nobody thanked her for it.

When Thaurik’s hand was covered again, he came to inspect her shoe.

“The knot’s twisted.”

“I couldn’t get it round.”

He retied it, turning the soft upper so the seam no longer lay beneath her heel. He had worked with his injured thumb held clear, using the other fingers awkwardly.

“Try that.”

She put weight on it. “Better.”

He nodded and went back to his mother.

At the place they finally slept, there was room to lie flat but not to keep the packs beside them. Those had to be stacked near the entrance. Drevik objected until Uivren showed him where water had once run along the inner wall; the drier sleeping floor was the safer place for bodies, and the packs could be lifted quickly if necessary.

He put the lamp case on top of the stack, then moved it down because the height made it liable to fall. Aevra watched him search for a position which would settle his mind. She had no kindness to spare for it until he checked the cloth around Drelset’s coat and laid the separate bundle above the case.

Kouven checked the bindings by a very small light. He was saving the brighter one for work which needed it. Eulva wanted to wash. There was insufficient water for more than face and hands. She sat with the damp cloth on her lap, staring at it, then washed behind her ears with great care.

For a time the only sounds were the little sounds of bodies being made ready to rest: a buckle set down, a cough, the scrape of a shoe pulled from a swollen foot. Aevra could not get comfortable. Every position pressed somewhere which had been scraped against the rock. Her left hand pulsed inside its wrapping.

Drevik lay nearby, still awake.

“Talk,” she said.

“About what?”

“Anything.”

He told her about the court, though she had been there for part of it. He described the musician’s rattling strings and Rethven taking away the cup. When he reached Drelset’s game, he stopped.

“Go on.”

“He said he’d won enough to pay for supper.”

“Had he?”

“He hadn’t counted the first three games.”

Aevra laughed once. It hurt her ribs. Drevik smiled in the little light.

“He was going to ask the woman how to make a board.”

“Of course he was.”

“He said the pegs were wrong.”

She laughed again, then put her right arm over her face. Drevik did not continue. After a while she asked him about the musician, and he tried to describe the instrument in more detail than he knew.

She fell asleep while he was explaining how the metal tongues might have been fixed. When she woke, the light was still low and he was asleep too. Somewhere near the entrance Uivren moved a pack away from a drip. Aevra listened until the sounds settled again, then lay still rather than getting up to check.

Chapter 35 — The voice above

Uivren made them practice before the ledge.

There was a length of safe floor beside the entrance. She put Aevra’s right hand on the back of her belt and told her to follow without watching her feet. Aevra tried and immediately looked down.

“Again.”

“I heard you.”

“Then do it.”

Aevra shut her mouth. The pain in her left hand had made every instruction sound like criticism. She followed the surveyor across the safe floor, feeling each change in pace through the belt.

At the ledge they would keep contact where the passage narrowed. No one was to turn toward a voice from another height. The rock returned sound badly, Uivren said. A person ahead could seem behind; a call from below could arrive from the roof. If someone needed to stop, they were to tell the person touching them, who would pass it along.

“What if we hear someone else?” Eirvek asked.

“We keep our feet where we know they are.”

The answer satisfied neither of them. It was the answer available.

The chair would not go along the narrowest stretch with Eulva in it. She would walk supported between Thaurik and Nulith, stopping at each wider place. The chair, partly dismantled again, would be passed ahead. Baerun would follow with Kouven. Thaereth would come last. They agreed the order aloud because no one trusted a nod they could not see.

Ildret opened the less damaged light a little. Its greenish glow caught the wall and left the edge dark.

They began.

The ledge curved into a chamber whose far side could not be seen. Aevra felt it rather than saw it: cold space against her cheek, the absence of returning light, the faint movement of air from below. Her borrowed shoe caught on a rough patch. She stopped. Uivren stopped with her. The halt passed backwards in quiet words and small tugs.

Aevra freed the shoe and went on.

Somewhere behind, Nulith began singing.

It was the descent tune, slow enough that the words fitted between steps. Thaurik joined for a line. Eulva, breathless, supplied a word when he forgot it. The sound gave Aevra a sense of where they were until another version returned from above, thinner and half a beat late.

Nulith stopped.

The echo continued briefly after her.

Aevra kept her hand on Uivren’s belt. The leather was warm from the surveyor’s body. Ahead, the wall bent outwards. Uivren turned sideways, and Aevra followed with her injured hand held tight against her chest.

“Aev.”

She stopped breathing.

It came from above the bend, familiar and impatient. Drelset shortened her name that way only when she had failed to notice something obvious. He had done it in workshops, across noisy rooms, once from the top of a scaffold while she searched for a tool he was holding out.

“Aev, here.”

She lifted her head.

Uivren’s belt moved beneath her fingers. The surveyor was still taking the next step. Aevra had to move or release her.

She moved.

The voice came again. There was a little cough in it, the cough Drelset made after laughing with food in his mouth. Aevra could see his face so clearly that for an instant the rock ahead seemed to have been placed in front of it.

She wanted to answer. She wanted, with a force which had nothing to do with believing, to hear what he would say next.

Behind her Drevik’s fingers tightened on the back of her coat.

She took another step.

A voice farther back said, “Mother?”

It was Thaurik. Eulva was ahead of him, but the sound he had answered came from lower down. A shuffle followed, then Nulith’s sharp, “Here. Your hand. Here.”

The company stopped.

Aevra did not turn. She felt Drevik standing still behind her. Uivren waited, one foot on the next piece of stone, her body balanced in the gap between movements.

Nulith spoke again, quietly now. Thaurik answered. Eulva said something cross and ordinary about being pulled like a sack, and Aevra almost sobbed at the sound.

They moved.

The next wider place held only four people at once. Uivren guided Aevra into it, then turned to help Drevik pass the chair pieces on. Aevra stood against the wall while the others came through in pairs. She counted faces, not voices.

No one asked whom anyone had heard.

When the ledge narrowed again, she offered her right hand backwards before Drevik reached for her coat. He took it for the first few steps, then changed to the belt when she needed the wall. His palm was damp. She held it firmly enough to hurt him and knew he would not say so.

The refuge had a low ceiling and a floor sloping toward the entrance. They put packs along the lower edge before anyone lay down. Uivren checked the air, then sat with her back against the wall and closed her eyes.

Aevra remained standing until Kouven told her there was nothing left for her to inspect.

“I wasn’t inspecting.”

“Then sit anyway.”

She sat.

Nulith unfastened the sling and let Kouven look at the hand. The swelling had tightened the bandage. He loosened it carefully. Aevra stared at the ceiling while he worked. Water had left a thin shining line there, catching the light in little separate beads.

When he finished, she began crying.

It was not quiet at first. She tried to make it quiet and could not. Her breath caught in her throat, each attempt to draw it bringing another sound she did not recognize as her own. She bent forward around the hurt hand.

Drevik sat beside her. He did not touch her until she leaned against him. Then he put an arm around her shoulders and held on.

Across the room, someone turned the light a little away.

She said Drelset’s name once. Drevik answered, “I know,” though she had not said anything for him to know.

For a long while the others went about the little tasks which remained. Water was passed. The chair fittings were put where they would not be lost. Eulva asked for her shawl, then apologized for asking; Nulith told her not to be foolish and brought it.

Aevra cried until there was no strength left in it. Afterwards her face hurt. Drevik gave her a damp cloth. She held it over her eyes with the hand which could still close.

Later Thaurik sat near the entrance and said to no one in particular, “She was right beside me.”

Eulva looked up.

“I am right beside you.”

“I know.”

She reached over and took his sleeve. They stayed that way while he ate.

Uivren said they would rest until everyone had slept. No one argued about the time.

Chapter 36 — Where Uivren stops

Drevik knew they had reached a station when he saw a complaint scratched beside the door.

He could not read the language, but someone had crossed out the last word and written a longer one beneath it. A reply followed in a different hand. The argument occupied a patch of plaster which had been washed around, carefully, for some time.

The door opened before they knocked.

A Mazereth with a gray scarf looked at Uivren, then at the people behind her, and made a sound of dismay. Uivren answered with what Drevik suspected was a shortened account. The keeper stepped aside.

The room smelled of stored food and hot stone. There were benches, hooks, a basin with a plug that fitted, and a cupboard standing open on folded bedding. Drevik wanted to kiss the cupboard.

Instead he helped lower Eulva into a chair which was meant to remain on a floor.

Uivren spoke to the keeper while the others drank. They knew each other well enough to interrupt. The keeper inspected her cracked sleeve, touched the bag containing the replacement lens, and asked a question which made her laugh without pleasure.

Aevra sat with her hand supported. She looked younger with exhaustion, or perhaps less defended. When Uivren came over, she began to thank her.

“Tomorrow,” the surveyor said. “Eat first.”

They stayed two nights.

The keeper had food to sell, though less than Uivren had hoped. Thaereth spent most of the remaining common coin and took a written debt for part of the rest. Water was plentiful. There were spare dressings, a length of cord, and a bundle of soft shoes which none of them fitted well. Aevra kept Thaurik’s borrowed shoe and paid to have its tie repaired.

Kouven slept for half the first day. An older worker at the station looked at the wounds while he slept, then discussed them with him when he woke. Neither pretended the hand could be made right there. It needed setting and time, and perhaps would never close quite as it had before. Drevik heard that last part from the far bench and watched Aevra’s face. She had heard it too.

On the second morning Uivren laid out her own supplies separately.

Aevra approached her. “We can find you a room above.”

“I have a room.”

“I mean if you came with us.”

“I am not.”

Drevik looked down at the strap he was mending. He had known this conversation would happen despite everything Uivren had said.

“It wouldn’t have to be exposed,” Aevra said. “There are lower rooms. Covered courts.”

The surveyor put down the food she was wrapping.

“I know there are rooms.”

Aevra stopped.

“My work is here,” Uivren said. “People expect me. I have already been away longer than I intended.”

“I wasn’t asking you to give it up.”

“What were you asking?”

Aevra looked at the separate supplies. “I don’t know.”

Uivren resumed wrapping them. After a moment she said, “You can send a message here. The keeper has carriers to the market. From the market, ask for the survey tables on the east side. Not the rope tables. Those move.”

Drevik took out his notebook. “Will they know your name?”

“They will know what I am owed.”

He laughed. Aevra did too, reluctantly.

They arranged the message route with the keeper. Nothing about it was quick or certain, but it involved places and people who actually carried things. Uivren copied a mark onto Drevik’s page so her name would not depend entirely on his pronunciation.

She had enough food for the known return leg to the next staffed place. A local carrier was going that way in two days, and she would travel with them for the first stretch. She had no intention of taking the blind ledge or the damaged hot crossing again if a lower route remained available. She described the alternative to the keeper, who argued about one bend and then agreed.

Drevik listened with envy. She knew where she wanted to arrive. His own destination had become a room he was afraid would be empty.

Drevik went with the station keeper to collect the stores.

The store room lay through a passage lined with hooks, each carrying a different sort of pack. Some were broad and flat, others narrow enough to pass through places Drevik would not have tried to enter. A little rack held spare fastenings. The keeper stopped there to replace a broken catch on the food bag before putting anything in it.

Drevik offered to do the repair. The keeper watched him test the spring and handed him a tool without asking whether he knew how to use it.

For ten minutes he worked. The metal was different from the fittings he used at home, softer at the bend and harder at the pin. He had to change his first plan. When the catch closed cleanly, the keeper tried it twice and put the tool away.

They filled the bag together. The keeper measured food against the number of people and the likely time above, adding a little when Drevik explained the chair. Two packets were returned to the shelf after an uncertain pause. One was put back in the bag.

Drevik asked whether messages ever came from the upper side.

“Occasionally.”

“Recently?”

The keeper shook their head. “Sounds. Workers. No letters.”

He had hoped for a letter. He carried the bag back with care.

In the main room, Uivren had spread her recovered instruments on a cloth. She was cleaning the unequal calipers, using a little brush to work grit from the hinge. The replacement lens lay in its own soft wrap. Beside it was the empty place in her bag where the long measure should have fitted.

“Will you replace it?” Drevik asked.

“When I can pay someone to make it.”

He sat down. “Tell me what to write if I find someone above.”

She looked at him skeptically, but took his notebook. The description involved more measurements than he expected and a material he had never worked. She drew the joint and then crossed it out because the drawing was misleading. Her second attempt was clearer.

“Don’t promise,” she said.

“I haven’t.”

“Good.”

He kept the page. As he got up, Uivren caught the corner of his sleeve.

“Stay a little.”

He sat again. She put the brush down beside the calipers. For a while they watched the keeper moving between the benches.

“I keep hearing the chair behind me,” she said.

Drevik looked toward the passage where its pieces stood.

“Not now,” she said. “When I close my eyes.”

He left his notebook shut on his knee. After a while her hand loosened on his sleeve. He stayed until the keeper brought food, and Uivren reached for her share.

Later, when she was speaking to the carrier who would take her back, he copied the measurements onto another sheet in case the first was damaged. He left a margin for the maker’s questions.

Before they left, he took out the lamp and checked the seam.

It had held. The cast had lost a tiny piece from one corner of its backing, not from the impression itself. He wrapped it again, more carefully.

Uivren stood beside him.

“Still there?”

“Yes.”

“Good.”

He looked up. “Thank you.”

“For looking at it?”

“For waiting for us. At the river. After.”

She considered him. “You helped with my things.”

“It wasn’t the same.”

“No.”

He smiled despite himself.

She touched the repaired seam with one finger, then withdrew it. “Your father may be annoyed.”

“He will.”

“Then he can tell you.”

Drevik had to look away.

At the door, Aevra embraced Uivren awkwardly, keeping the injured hand between them. The surveyor tolerated it, then adjusted the knot in Aevra’s sling.

“Too low.”

“I know.”

“You keep saying that.”

This time Aevra laughed properly.

Uivren went through the first turns of the remaining route with Thaereth. The royal stair continued above the station through a passage whose lower air she disliked. She would come no farther than its threshold. The keeper had heard work beyond the upper connection some days before, but could not say whose.

Eleven people assembled their loads.

Drevik counted them and felt the missing twelfth as a physical space near the door. Uivren stood beside the keeper, her bag already packed for a different journey.

She called after them before the first bend.

“Drink before the climb.”

Baerun lifted his bottle in a gesture which might have been obedience or complaint. Drevik heard her laugh once behind them.

Chapter 37 — Behind the royal wall

The stair ended at a door without a handle.

It was broad, metal-faced, and set into a stone frame which had darkened around its lower joints. Beside it stood a narrow service panel, partly hidden by deposits from an old leak. Above the panel, a royal mark had been cut away and another fitted over it. Aevra could see the earlier lines at the edges.

Thaereth put down her pack.

“Here.”

She sounded certain for the first time since the ledge.

The receiving chamber was larger than the station below, but colder. A channel ran across its floor toward a grated opening.
