On the other side of the channel, raised stone benches offered places to sit. They put Eulva there and helped Baerun settle beside her. The final climb had taken most of the day. He had stopped counting steps aloud after the first hundred.

Ausk stood beneath the old mark.

“I don’t have this,” they said.

“You got us to the stair,” Drevik answered.

“That isn’t this.”

“No.”

Thaereth took a small hammer from the tool roll. She struck the service panel in a pattern, paused, and repeated it. The sound went into the wall with an odd flatness. Aevra felt it beneath her right palm.

They waited.

No answer came.

Thaereth struck again, then moved to another point where the metal rang more clearly. Aevra knelt beside the lower joint. The channel carried a little water, clean-looking but not tested. A rusted rod disappeared through the wall above it. The rod’s near end had once connected to a lever. The lever was gone.

“Don’t move that,” she said when Eirvek leaned forward.

He drew back without argument.

She followed the rod with her light. It passed through a packed collar into darkness. Beside it a smaller pipe had been stopped with a wooden plug swollen hard in place. This was the old hydraulic linkage. Somewhere above, moving the wrong part might admit water rather than people.

Thaereth tapped once more.

A reply came so faintly that Aevra thought she had imagined it.

The officer raised a hand. Everyone stopped moving.

Three taps. A pause. Two.

Thaereth answered. Her shoulders dropped a little.

The first voice reached them through the service pipe after someone on the far side opened a speaking shutter.

“Names.”

Thaereth gave hers, her office, the expedition’s authority. The voice asked for the names of two people who could confirm her identity. She gave those too.

“How many?”

“Eleven here.”

A pause.

“Your party was eight.”

“Zerla separated at the control river. Drelset is dead. Six of the original party here, five joining from below.”

Drevik sat down suddenly beside the chair. Aevra did not look at him. Hearing Drelset’s name passed through a wall made it sound newly official.

“What people?”

“Ten Aeler and one Trow.”

Aevra counted in her head and almost corrected her before realizing the number was right. Six original, four from Outhren, Ausk.

The voice withdrew. Another voice spoke beyond it, too far away for words to carry. Then the first returned.

“Any fever?”

Kouven answered. He described Baerun’s wound, Aevra’s hand, the burns, the absence of current fever or vomiting. He did not say everyone was well. He gave his work and the place where he had served before departure.

The reply was that they must wait.

“How long?” Eulva asked, loudly enough that the pipe might carry it.

No answer came for her.

Aevra used the waiting to examine the chamber. It helped until she began trying to do things with the injured hand. Kouven told her to sit. She told him she needed the position of the drain. He came with her and held the light, irritated enough that she stopped asking him to move it unnecessarily.

The drain led under the door, not away from it. A raised lip around the grating suggested it had once been meant to take overflow. The level marks on the wall were old, but one wet line lay over dust which had not yet settled hard.

She called Thaereth.

The speaking shutter opened again after another series of taps. A different voice asked what she had found.

Aevra described the rod, the plugged pipe, the wet line and the direction of the channel. She gave measurements using the widths of the stone courses because she could not get a rule into every place she needed.

The upper worker asked whether the rod was free.

“We haven’t touched it.”

“Good. Don’t.”

She nearly laughed.

He asked about two smaller openings. She found one. The other had been filled, perhaps long ago. She scraped the deposit beside it with a borrowed tool held in her right hand while Kouven complained about the dust.

Beyond the wall came more conversation, then a heavy metallic sound. The upper worker told them that part of the supply had been isolated but a second connection remained under pressure. They were finding the valve.

“Zerla told you?” Aevra asked.

“She told us enough to listen. We didn’t know which end you could reach.”

“She’s alive?”

“Yes.”

The word traveled through the chamber. Baerun covered his face. Ildret sat down beside him. Aevra put her forehead against the cool wall and left it there for a moment.

Then the worker asked her to repeat the position of the filled opening, and she lifted her head.

The waiting lasted long enough for Eulva to fall asleep and wake convinced they had already been admitted.

Thaurik explained where they were. She listened, looked at the door and asked for water. He brought it without correcting the rest of what she said. Aevra watched him crouch beside her so the cup could be held at a comfortable height.

Drevik had taken out the replica and was checking the wick. There was no need to light it; Ildret’s lamps were enough. He trimmed it anyway, catching the little black piece in his palm rather than letting it fall into the oil. She nearly asked him to trim hers, although it had not been lit.

From behind the wall came a succession of knocks, then a long pause, then a scraping which seemed to pass over their heads. The sounds did not arrive where she expected. After the ledge, each misplaced noise tightened her body before she could remember where she was.

Thaereth asked her to listen to the rod.

Aevra put her right hand against its exposed end. A small vibration came through it. She told the upper worker, who asked whether it had begun before or after the scraping. She thought after. She said she was not certain.

“That will do. Tell me if it changes.”

She kept her hand there until her arm tired. Nulith replaced her. Then Eirvek, solemn with the responsibility, took the next turn. The worker above spoke to him directly, asking the same question he had asked Aevra. The boy answered without looking for her approval.

Ausk sat beside the outer wall, their burned forearm resting on the bag. They had not slept. Drevik brought them a piece of food and remained beside them. Aevra could hear them discussing the memory’s warm wall, whether it had been heated by the same water which now barred the way. Neither claimed to know.

Aevra moved to the bench and tried to rest her head. The stone was cold through her coat. She thought of the first receiving chamber, the one whose door had opened easily and whose air had almost killed them. She wanted to tell the upper workers about it again, then remembered she already had.

She let them work.

They heard the argument because someone above had forgotten to close the speaking shutter.

“The identified party first.”

“And the lower households?”

“Pending confirmation.”

“There’s a boy and an elder.”

“The identified Aeler. The other stays until—”

Thaereth struck the panel once, hard.

“We can hear you.”

Silence.

Ausk stood beside the packs, face unreadable. Drevik moved nearer without touching them.

Thaereth said, “All eleven have traveled the same route. All eleven will be examined. I have given their names.”

“That is not the only consideration.”

“Then put the consideration in the record with your name.”

The voice did not answer immediately. Aevra disliked the pleasure she felt at the silence. There were people beyond the wall doing dangerous work to let them in, and someone among those people was also willing to keep Ausk here. She could not turn all the sounds into one enemy.

Another worker opened the shutter farther.

“We’re still draining the lower pocket. Nothing opens until that’s done. Keep everyone on the raised side.”

They moved the packs. Water began entering the channel in a steady stream. Beads gathered on the cold metal of the grating. Aevra watched the level. It rose to the lip, held, then fell as a hidden outlet took it away.

Thaurik put an arm around his mother’s shoulders. She let him keep it there.

Above, someone shouted for a wedge. A hammer answered. The door remained closed.

Chapter 38 — Eleven through

The first opening was too small for a person.

A square shutter lifted in the service panel and a face appeared behind it, lit from the other side. The worker was an Aeler woman with a cloth tied over her hair and a cut across one cheek. She looked into the chamber, counted without moving her lips, and asked Aevra to bring the light nearer the drain.

Aevra did. The woman watched the water, then lowered the shutter and spoke to someone behind her.

When it opened again, she passed through a bottle.

“Drink that. More coming.”

Nulith took it to Eulva first. Nobody argued about the order.

The woman gave her name as Vaedra. She was responsible for the inner works, she said, not for deciding where anyone would sleep afterwards. She would open the service leaf into a little transfer space. They were to send two people, no more, wait while the lower leaf closed, then move through the upper one. The main door would remain shut until the water supply was properly isolated.

“The chair?” Thaurik asked.

“Bring it to the opening. We’ll see.”

They brought it. Vaedra inspected the width through the shutter and told them to remove one side rail. Aevra began to explain the missing piece and the sling repair. Vaedra listened, then called for someone who knew carrying chairs.

A man arrived. He looked at Baerun, looked at the frame, and asked which one had designed the handholds.

“Me,” Baerun said.

“They’ll do.”

The hauler smiled for the first time that day.

Eulva went first with Thaurik.

The service leaf opened inwards with a grinding sound which made everyone lean away. Beyond it lay a stone-floored space just large enough for the chair turned sideways. The upper door remained closed. Vaedra stood inside wearing boots wet to the knees.

They passed the old woman through with care. She caught Aevra’s sleeve briefly as she went by, then released it to take Vaedra’s offered hand. Thaurik followed with the chair pieces. The lower leaf shut.

The sound of the upper door opening came later, distant and heavy. Then voices, the scrape of the chair, the upper door closing again.

Aevra had to keep telling herself that the delay was part of the process.

Baerun and Kouven went next. The hauler insisted on stepping over the threshold with help rather than being carried. He got his good foot through, paused for breath, and asked the upper worker not to pull his bad arm. The worker changed grip immediately.

The door shut again.

Drevik stood with the lamp case against his chest. Aevra watched him look from it to Eirvek, who was staring at the opening.

“Give it ahead,” she said.

He nodded. When the leaf opened, he passed the case to Vaedra, named it, and asked her to keep it upright. Then he turned back for Eirvek’s pack. The boy began to protest that he could carry it. Drevik said he knew and took it anyway, freeing the boy’s hands for the awkward step.

Nulith waited until she had seen Eirvek inside, then closed her eyes for a moment. She went with Ildret on the next cycle.

That left Aevra, Thaereth and Ausk.

The chamber seemed suddenly larger. The packs which remained made isolated little shapes on the bench. The water continued running beneath the floor.

When the leaf opened, Vaedra said, “Two.”

Aevra looked at Thaereth. The officer nodded toward Ausk.

They stepped forward together.

Ausk paused at the threshold. Beyond Vaedra stood another official, dressed more cleanly than the workers. He held a board and looked first at Ausk’s face, then at the name written there.

“Provisional admission,” he said.

Ausk answered, “I heard.”

Vaedra moved him aside so they could enter.

The transfer space smelled of wet iron. Aevra stood close to Ausk while the lower leaf shut behind them. For a few breaths neither door was open. She could hear herself swallowing.

Ausk looked up at the ceiling. “Not fond of this room.”

“No.”

The upper door opened.

The receiving room beyond held lamps bright enough to hurt.

Aevra blinked. People were moving everywhere: workers with tools, carers with blankets, a warden trying to keep the way clear. Eulva sat wrapped in something blue. Baerun lay on a bench while Kouven spoke to an upper healer. Eirvek had a cup in both hands. Nulith was taking off his wet outer coat despite his objections.

Aevra counted them. Nine including herself and Ausk, then realized she had not counted Ildret standing behind a worker. Ten. Thaereth still below.

She remained beside the door until the final cycle brought the officer through.

“Eleven,” she said.

Thaereth heard and nodded. Vaedra shut the upper leaf behind her. The last grinding turn of the wheel seemed to continue in Aevra’s bones after the wheel stopped.

A carer approached her hand. Aevra pulled it back before she could prevent herself.

“I’ll tell you before touching it,” the woman said.

“Sorry.”

“Sit here.”

She sat. The bench was ordinary wood. Its edge had been rounded by use. Aevra ran the fingers of her right hand along it, feeling the grain.

The carer loosened the sling. She asked when the injury had happened, whether Aevra had felt the fingers since, what Kouven had done. Aevra answered. Her voice sounded strangely formal. When the woman began unwrapping the hand, she stopped answering and watched the lamp instead.

Across the room, someone brought food.

The smell reached her before the bowl: onion, fat, the particular grain used in Kharveth’s kitchens. It was so familiar that she did not recognize it as a smell at first. She thought of Maeven leaving a spoon in the pot, of a table crowded with another person’s supper, of being irritated because the wrong food had been cooked on the wrong day.

The carer asked a question. Aevra had not heard it.

“Again,” she said.

The woman repeated it patiently.

Drevik found his case beneath a dry bench. Vaedra had put it upright as he asked. He touched it, then left it there while he helped Eirvek undo a knotted bootlace.

No one had to leave again that night.

Part VI — The room prepared

Chapter 39 — The receiving days

The hand was set on the second morning.

Aevra remembered being told what would happen. She remembered agreeing. She did not remember the words she used while it happened, though afterwards Kouven would not meet her eyes and the healer said she had heard worse.

The fingers were splinted, the wound cleaned, the whole hand wrapped in a shape which no longer resembled part of her. She was to keep it raised. She was not to test the grip. She was not to decide, because the pain had lessened, that the injury had become a workshop inconvenience she could work around.

The healer had met masons before.

Aevra lay on a bed in the observation room and tried to obey.

There were rules for the receiving days. Three mornings of examination, meals and water kept separate until the carers were satisfied there was no shared illness, dressings changed where the wounds could be properly seen. No one was locked alone. The rooms opened onto an enclosed court where people could sit in the cooler air.

Kouven thought most of it sensible. He objected when a warden proposed separating Ausk from the others because of the appearance of the burned arm.

“It’s a burn,” he said. “I dressed it.”

“We have to establish—”

“Then ask the healer to look. That’s what she’s here for.”

The healer looked and agreed with him. Ausk remained in the room.

Aevra heard the exchange from her bed and began to get up. Nulith put a cup on the table beside her.

“It’s finished.”

“I know.”

“Then stay there.”

Aevra did, resentfully. A little later she realized Nulith had gone out without telling her where. The realization troubled her until she heard herself beginning to ask Eirvek, and stopped.

During the second night Aevra woke to the sound of a door being closed and tried to sit up before she knew where she was.

The sling caught against the bed. Pain brought the room back: the screened lamps, the basin, Baerun asleep on the far side. A carer stood in the doorway with folded cloths.

“Only me,” she said.

Aevra nodded. Her heart would not slow.

She lay down again, then got up because lying down made it worse. In the enclosed court the air was cool. A lamp above the drainage grate showed that its cover was properly seated. She found herself checking the hinges on the outer door.

Ausk sat beneath the wall, awake.

“Can’t sleep?” she asked.

“I was asleep. The bell woke me.”

Aevra had not noticed a bell.

“They ring it when the watch changes,” she said.

“I worked that out.”

She sat beside them. The court had a narrow opening high above, admitting colder air from a shaft. Ausk looked up at it and then away.

“Too bright?”

“Sometimes. It’s the changes.”

Aevra considered the steady lower lights, the way their eyes had adjusted so slowly in Hesh’s house. She wanted to offer advice and could think of none which would not begin by telling Ausk to become accustomed to something she herself found ordinary.

They stayed until the carer came to fetch her. Aevra protested that she was resting. The woman pointed out that her hand was hanging down instead of raised.

Back in bed she watched the light reach a small flaw in the plaster. She knew who had probably done that work. She could picture the trowel which left the mark. For a while the familiarity helped. Then a sound from the corridor became Drelset clearing his throat, and she had to begin again.

At breakfast Baerun complained that the room was too quiet. Eulva said it was too noisy. They argued amiably about bells, footsteps and the clatter from the kitchen. Aevra ate while listening to them disagree about the same room.

Zerla came on the third day.

She stood in the doorway wearing a clean coat Aevra had never seen. One cheek was thinner than she remembered, or perhaps the whole face was. For a moment neither moved. Then Zerla crossed the room and embraced her so carefully that Aevra understood someone had warned her about the hand.

“I heard the wheel,” Zerla said into her shoulder.

“We got out.”

“I know. Now.”

They held each other until Zerla stepped back and looked round the room. She greeted the others, stopped at Baerun’s bed, laughed at his shoe and then cried without warning. He took her hand and told her it had been fashionable below.

Later she described the upper bank.

The maintenance marks had led her to a stair which was partly blocked but passable alone. She had slept once in a dry cupboard because she could not make herself go farther in the dark. At the first staffed works, they had not understood which control river she meant. She had drawn it, argued, slept again, and been taken to someone who knew the old connections.

The warning had reached Kharveth. Searchers had inspected the original route and found enough destruction to stop them going down it. Listeners had been posted at several possible upper ends. Some had been withdrawn when days passed without an answer. Vaedra had kept workers near this one because the water had changed in a way Zerla described.

“Did they say we were dead?” Aevra asked.

“Some.”

“Maeven?”

Zerla shook her head. “Not to me.”

Aevra looked away.

Zerla had already heard about Drelset. She asked where he had been standing. Aevra described the approach, the chest, the two workers who got through. Zerla listened without interrupting. At the end she asked whether his coat had come back.

“Drevik has it.”

“His sister will want it.”

“I know.”

Zerla stayed until the visiting bell.

Maeven brought stockings, a clean shirt, Aevra’s own comb and a packet of food which the carers inspected before allowing it through.

Aevra saw the bag first and knew it was hers. Then Maeven came round the door and the bag ceased to matter.

She stood too quickly. Pain caught her hand. Maeven dropped the bag on a chair and crossed to her.

For a while there was no conversation. Aevra pressed her face into the familiar place beside Maeven’s neck. Maeven held the back of her head with one hand and the shoulder clear of the sling with the other. She smelled of metal dust and soap.

“You’re thin,” she said at last.

“So are you.”

“I’m not.”

Aevra laughed against her coat.

They sat together on the bed. Maeven unpacked the bag, naming things unnecessarily. The shirt had been mended at the side seam. The stockings were not the pair Aevra preferred because those had worn through and Maeven had thrown them away. The food was from her aunt, who had wanted to come and been told there were too many visitors already.

Aevra watched her hands.

“Stay,” she said when Maeven began folding the empty bag.

“I have to get back.”

“It’s only—”

“I know how long it is.”

Aevra stopped.

Maeven put the bag down again. “There are fittings cooling. Someone is watching them for me. I can’t leave them there all afternoon.”

“Of course.”

“I’ll come tomorrow.”

“I know.”

Maeven looked at her, tired suddenly. “Do you?”

Aevra reached with her right hand. Maeven took it.

“I want you here,” Aevra said.

“I want you home.”

They sat for another few minutes. Then Maeven kissed her, picked up the bag and went back to the work which had continued while Aevra was away.

On the fourth morning a clerk arrived with a proposal to extend the observation period for the lower travelers.

“For what symptom?” the healer asked.

“The duration of their residence below.”

“That isn’t a symptom.”

“We cannot know—”

“You can ask me what I have found.”

The clerk did. The healer answered for each person separately. Eulva needed rest and assistance on stairs. Thaurik’s burned thumb was healing. Eirvek had no illness. Nulith had strained a shoulder carrying. Ausk’s burn required dressings. None of those findings justified keeping them behind an observation boundary after the others left.

The clerk took notes. It was clear he would rather have been given a different answer. He did not receive one.

Release did not provide rooms. Thaereth spent the afternoon finding temporary accommodation, while Nulith made her own inquiries and came back with better information about two of the places offered. Eulva refused a room up three flights. Thaurik backed her refusal before anyone suggested she was being difficult.

Aevra tried to assign who would carry which bags. Nulith let her reach the third instruction before saying, “We’re going different ways.”

Aevra looked at the packs. Drevik’s was already beside the door, the lamp case in his hand.

“Right,” she said.

She sat down and let him leave for his father.

Chapter 40 — The father’s wick

His father was awake.

Drevik had prepared himself for finding the room empty, for finding strangers putting away the bedding, for being told that someone had sent a message which failed to reach him. He had not prepared for the old man looking up from his chair and saying, “You’re late.”

He laughed, then put down the case before he dropped it.

His father held out a hand. Drevik crossed the room and knelt beside the chair. The hand on his head was very light.

“I know,” he said.

The old man touched his hair, then his cheek, as though checking whether both belonged to the same person. Drevik stayed still until the hand withdrew.

The room had changed. The bed stood nearer the stove. A second chair had been moved beside it for whoever sat through the night. Bottles occupied a shelf which had held his father’s tools. One unfinished piece remained on the table, covered with a cloth.

His father saw him looking.

“I’ll finish it.”

Drevik nodded.

“Not today.”

“No.”

A woman from the neighboring rooms brought water and asked whether he had eaten. She had been helping with the nights, she said. His father complained that she counted how often he drank. She told him someone had to, and went out to give them time alone.

The case stood beside the door.

His father looked at it. Drevik rose to fetch it, then remained standing with one hand on the fastening.

“It’s our lamp,” he said.

The old man’s expression did not change at first.

“The copy. I repaired it.”

“You didn’t find the other?”

“I found it.”

“Then?”

Drevik opened the case. The familiar reservoir caught the light, its new seam clean, the braid around the bail still pale. His father looked at it for a long time.

“I asked you for the other.”

“I know.”

“Was it broken?”

“No.”

“Too dangerous to reach?”

“No.”

The answers left no shelter between them. Drevik set the lamp on the table and explained.

He began with Veirsa because the history was too long. Her grandmother had taken the lamp from the crypt and repaired it. Her mother had worked by it. It stood above the bench in a house where people cooked, argued, made things and waited for neighbors. They were relatives. He described the line they had traced through the papers, including the duplicated name in their own account.

His father interrupted at that.

“No. That name was—”

Drevik showed him the copy Veirsa had made. The old man read it, frowned and put it aside without conceding anything.

“So she would not give it to you.”

“No.”

“And you accepted that.”

Drevik looked at the floor.

“Not at first.”

He told him about putting it in the case. His father’s fingers moved against the chair arm. Drevik could not tell what the movement meant.

“But you put it back.”

“Yes.”

The old man closed his eyes.

Drevik waited. From the next room came the sound of water being poured into a basin. Somewhere outside a cart wheel struck the same uneven stone twice.

At last his father said, “I would have liked to see it.”

“I brought something.”

He took out the cast.

The old man held it with both hands.

Drevik had expected him to touch the dent first. Instead he followed the line of the hinge repair. He asked whether the original pin remained. Drevik said no. He asked what metal had replaced it. Drevik told him, and for a few minutes they spoke about a repair as they had spoken about hundreds before.

Then his father said, “Who did that?”

“Veirsa thinks her grandmother. Some of the work is later.”

“Good hand.”

“Yes.”

He turned the cast toward the window. The light reached the shallow thumb depression, showing the worn edge.

“She works metal?”

“Household things. Handles, lamps, fittings. Her partner brings work and does other jobs. Her son has come up with us.”

“Here?”

“In the city.”

His father looked up. “How old?”

Drevik told him. The old man asked whether the boy worked. Drevik described the rods, the model lifting frame, the curiosity about surface birds which had sometimes turned into teasing.

“Bring him,” his father said.

“When you’re rested.”

“I’m rested now.”

“You’re tired.”

His father’s mouth tightened. Drevik heard Veirsa’s mother in the room, hating the same question. He changed it.

“I’ll ask whether he wants to come.”

The old man accepted that, grudgingly.

Drevik lit the replica before evening.

He filled it at the table, checked the base and trimmed the wick. His father watched every movement. The first flame was too high.

“Lower.”

Drevik lowered it.

“A little more.”

He turned the wick down until the light lay in the familiar shape across the table. The repaired seam held. He put a clean scrap beneath it anyway and his father gave him a look which was almost amused.

“I checked it.”

“Then why the cloth?”

“Because you would look.”

The old man smiled. It passed quickly, but Drevik saw it.

They ate together. His father managed little and objected when Drevik noticed. Afterwards he wanted to move to the bed. The transfer required help from the neighbor. Drevik learned where to put his hands, how long to wait between standing and turning, which side hurt if he gripped too tightly.

His father apologized once.

“Don’t,” Drevik said too sharply.

The old man looked away. Drevik wished he had let the apology stand and answered it kindly.

That night was bad.

There was coughing, then a long interval in which his father could not find a position that let him rest. Drevik changed the folded cloth behind his shoulders, brought water, opened the window a little and closed it when the draught became too much. Near dawn the old man mistook him for someone else and asked whether the cart was ready.

Drevik said it would be.

When his father slept, he sat beside the lamp and unfastened his own collar. He had imagined coming home as an end to being afraid. Now he listened to every breath, counting the spaces despite knowing that the counting could do nothing.

The neighbor came at first light and sent him to lie down.

He resisted until she said, “If you want to help tonight, sleep now.”

The phrase was familiar enough to make him obey.

On the afternoon after the bad night, his father wanted the table moved.

“Nearer the window.”

“It catches the draught there.”

“Then close the window.”

Drevik moved it. He had been arranging the room according to what was easiest for care: water within reach, tools put away, the lamp where it could not be knocked. His father wanted to see the passage outside and the little patch of brighter stone opposite. They shifted the chair too. By the time it was done, both were tired and neither would admit the effort had been excessive.

A neighbor stopped outside to ask about a repair. Drevik began to explain that his father was not working. The old man interrupted with a question about the object. The neighbor brought it in.

It was a clasp on a box, worn at the catch. His father examined it in his lap and told Drevik what to fetch. Drevik fetched it. For half an hour he was a boy again, handing over tools, anticipating one request correctly and another too soon.

The neighbor left the box to be mended. His father looked pleased.

“You could have asked me,” he said after the door closed.

“I thought you were too tired.”

“I am tired.”

Drevik waited.

The old man looked out at the passage. Someone was carrying a covered tray past the window. They could hear the faint clink of whatever lay beneath the cloth.

“I don’t want every person who comes through that door to have decided what I can do before I speak.”

Drevik sat on the other chair. He had reasons for the table, the tools, the visitor. He did not give them.

“All right.”

His father nodded, still looking outside.

Later he asked Drevik to finish the clasp because his fingers had begun to tremble. Drevik did it without remark. The old man watched and corrected the angle once, correctly.

When evening came, they left the table by the window. Drevik closed the shutter halfway against the draught. His father said that was better.

Eirvek came two days later with Nulith.

He had washed his hair and was trying not to look nervous. Drevik’s father inspected him with an attention the boy bore surprisingly well. Then he asked to see the lifting model.

Eirvek had brought it wrapped in a cloth. He set it on the table and demonstrated the blue wheel. The old man watched, asked about the lower pin, and made a suggestion which Eirvek disputed at once.

Drevik stood in the doorway, listening.

Nulith looked at him and raised her eyebrows. He smiled. Neither went in to rescue the conversation.

After the visitors left, his father slept for a long time. When he woke, he asked Drevik whether Veirsa’s workshop had a window.

“Onto a court.”

“Good light?”

“Not much. She used the lamp.”

The old man nodded. He did not say anything about the original after that, though once Drevik found him holding the cast in his lap, his thumb resting on the old dent.

Chapter 41 — The account of the work

They held the inquiry in a room Aevra had repaired six years before.

The window still stuck in wet weather. She had told the owner then that the frame needed replacing, and the owner had paid for the cheaper repair. Now a clerk struggled with the latch while the people at the table waited. Aevra nearly offered to help before remembering her hand.

Thaereth sat beside her with the copies arranged in order. The officer had slept badly. Aevra could tell by the little red patches beside her nose and the care with which she set each page square to the next.

The inquiry had three tasks, the chair said: establish what had happened to the expedition, assess the continued danger of the connection, and recommend provision for those admitted through it. There would be a separate account of the expenditures. Baerun made a noise at that which might have been a cough.

Zerla spoke first.

She described the separation and her route to the upper works. Vaedra followed with the listening stations, the changed water, the isolation of the supply and the opening of the service leaf. Aevra listened to the parts of her own survival which had happened elsewhere.

Then Thaereth presented the route and the evidence from Outhren.

One of the officials interrupted at the word inhabited.

“How many?”

Thaereth explained that they had not conducted a census. She gave the number of households Vaurden had recorded for the threatened terraces and made clear that it did not include the whole settlement. She named workshops, boat services, gardens and the places receiving the lower relocation.

“People awaiting return,” the official said, making a note.

Aevra leaned forward.

“Some. Others are moving along the river.”

“Because they have no upper route.”

“Because their work and families are there.”

He looked at her. “They said that?”

“Yes.”

She gave Veirsa’s name, Rethven’s, Ivrassa’s connection to the household. The official crossed something out. Aevra could not see what he wrote instead.

The upper record keeper had found two documents.

One confirmed the original failure and the loss of the main road approximately a hundred and eighty years before. The other, much later, recorded a request to examine a possible lower connection and an expense for tools. No result had been filed with it. There might be another bundle, he said. The records had been moved twice after water damage.

Thaereth laid the copied lower account beside them.

The dates were close enough to matter. They did not identify every worker or establish who had refused to return. The chair asked whether the expedition had brought the original lower book.

“No,” Thaereth said. “It belongs to the people using it.”

“Then we have a copy.”

“With the copyist named and the place it can be examined.”

The record keeper nodded. He seemed less offended by that than the official asking the questions.

Aevra showed the drawing of the relief opening and the unknown water connection. Vaedra moved her chair closer. The two of them spoke for some time about the direction of pressure and the later alterations. The rest of the table listened with decreasing comprehension. Aevra found this easier than the historical questions: a proposed intervention could be described, tested, rejected for a reason she could point to.

A permanent receiving route would require more work. A staffed post could be maintained in the meantime, with the water isolated and the inner door operated under supervision. Vaedra named the workers and shifts it would take. The chair asked for fewer. She refused, explaining which task would be left unattended.

Thaereth placed both recommendations on the table and read the earlier one aloud. A survey, followed by closure if the connection threatened inhabited galleries. Aevra remembered how little there had seemed to dispute when they set out.

“Has the danger diminished?” the chair asked.

“No.”

“Then why withdraw this?”

Thaereth rested her hand on the earlier page.

“We have found inhabited galleries on the other side.”

“And if maintaining access puts these at risk?”

Vaedra’s list of shifts lay between them. Thaereth drew it closer.

“Then we need these workers. And a survey party with people who know the lower connection.”

The chair did not reach for the list. Thaereth waited, her hand still on the page she had withdrawn.

“What of the other lost holds?” the record keeper asked.

The chair looked tired. “This inquiry concerns Outhren.”

“There is an account here of another.”

“Record it.”

Nothing more was promised. Aevra watched the keeper set aside a separate sheet rather than bury the name beneath the current papers.

Nulith was called before the inquiry turned to the failed shore.

She had brought no family tree. The clerk asked for one, and she said she could give the names she knew if they would explain what the names were supposed to establish.

“Your connection to this kingdom.”

“Which kingdom?”

“Kharveth.”

“I came through its door with your officer. Before that I lived in Outhren.”

The clerk looked toward the chair. The chair asked for her household’s address below, the route by which she had arrived and her intended stay. Nulith answered all three. She wanted work and a place for a season, perhaps longer, and a means of corresponding with her sister’s household. She did not ask the inquiry to declare her an upper resident by ancestry.

The record keeper wrote down the address. He asked how it would be recognized if the household moved again. Nulith described the carrier contact and gave Ivrassa’s name.

“Household sister?” he asked, checking the relationship.

“Of Veirsa. Not mine by birth.”

He altered the entry without making her explain why the distinction mattered.

Thaurik spoke next. He wanted to be certain that accepting a room did not bind his mother to remain above. The answer was that no such condition was intended. He asked for that in writing. After a short pause, the clerk wrote it.

Aevra turned to the page where she had written Nulith’s questions. Nulith was already answering one, correcting the clerk’s account of where she meant to live. Aevra folded her hands beneath the table, caught the splint against her sleeve and winced. Nulith saw, shifted the water jug nearer, and continued speaking for herself.

Drelset’s death took longer to describe than the passage in which it had happened.

Aevra drew the shore and the two approaches. Thaereth gave the order of movements. Uivren was absent, but her account had been written at the station before they parted, with the keeper witnessing the mark she used. Kouven described Aevra’s condition after being pulled out and the air which had driven everyone down the steps.

The chair asked whether the support had been adequate.

“For the load when we set it,” Aevra said. “We thought so.”

“And after?”

“The floor beneath it failed.”

“Could that have been discovered?”

Aevra saw the timber with its load on it. She had to look down at her splint to remember that the load was gone and the question was being asked afterwards. Since returning, she had woken twice with her right arm braced against the bedframe.

She looked at the drawing.

“Perhaps with more time and the right access. We did not discover it.”

Thaereth did not rescue her answer.

They explained the attempts from both sides, the loss of contact, the continuing movement, the body left beyond the fall. A clerk asked for the location to be marked precisely for the death record. Aevra marked it. Her right hand made a line less steady than she wanted.

No one asked her to call the death necessary. She had been braced for that question and found its absence exhausting.

The decision was narrower than the room’s arguments.

There would be a staffed receiving post for an initial season, supplies assigned from two upper stores, and a properly equipped survey of the connection with local knowledge sought from below. Letters could be left at the post for carriers as routes became available. The admitted households could remain while ordinary residence arrangements were made. Ausk’s temporary admission would be reviewed with those arrangements, not treated as a continuing medical restriction.

The funding came partly from work postponed elsewhere. One official named the postponed work and made sure the cost entered the record. Vaedra did not dispute it.

Aevra left with a copy of the decision.

Outside, a young clerk asked whether the rumor about a speaking Dragon was true. Baerun said it was and that the creature had better manners than some employers he could name. The clerk laughed, then realized no further story was coming.

At the door, Thaereth touched Aevra’s sleeve.

“I’ll send the final copy.”

“Thank you.”

“I didn’t mean that as—”

“I know.”

They stood awkwardly for a moment. Then Aevra asked whether Thaereth was going home.

“To the office.”

“Eat first.”

The officer smiled faintly. “Uivren got to you.”

Aevra looked down the passage, where familiar lamps marked every turn. “Apparently.”

Chapter 42 — Those who waited

Drelset’s sister opened the door with flour on her hands.

Aevra had imagined several versions of the visit. In none of them had she needed to wait while someone finished kneading.

“Come in,” the woman said. “Sit. I’ll be a moment.”

Her name was Hauren. Aevra had met her before, at a meal years ago, and remembered very little beyond an argument about who had borrowed a pan. Now she sat at Hauren’s table with Drelset’s coat on her knees and watched her scrape dough from between her fingers.

An older man sat near the stove. He looked at the coat and then away. Hauren introduced him as their mother’s brother. Aevra nodded. He did not offer his name and she did not ask.

She set the coat on the table.

Hauren dried her hands and unfolded it. The mended sleeve showed at once.

“He did that?”

“Yes.”

“Badly.”

“I held it for him.”

Hauren pressed a finger along the stitches. “He never would bring things round before they tore.”

Aevra sat very still.

The older man asked how he had died.

She told them. She began with the stores being moved, the known damage, the air changing. She described the workers he helped through and the block across his legs. She did not describe him as calm. He had been frightened and in pain. He had spoken clearly when he could.

Hauren asked whether he had known he was dying.

“I don’t know.”

“Did he say anything for us?”

“No.”

Hauren looked at the coat again.

“He was trying to get out,” Aevra said. “We were trying to get him out.”

The older man leaned forward. “You went in?”

“Yes.”

“And came out.”

“Yes.”

He did not accuse her. She heard the accusation anyway and made herself wait before answering a question he had not asked.

Hauren wanted the exact order: when the first support shifted, what the second timber did, whether his right leg was free, how close Aevra had been. Aevra used two spoons and the edge of the cloth to show the angles. Her injured hand lay useless in her lap.

When she had finished, Hauren put the spoons back beside the bowl.

“I keep thinking if he’d moved first.”

“So do I.”

“He would have said someone had to hold it.”

Aevra nodded.

“That doesn’t help.”

“No.”

They sat while the dough rose beneath its cloth.

Later the older man asked whether the people he helped were alive. Aevra gave the two workers’ names and the place where they had been sent. One had asked her to tell the family that Drelset had put a hand between his shoulder and the beam as he passed, keeping him from striking it. She had written the words down so she would not improve them in remembering.

Hauren read the little note and folded it into the coat.

Aevra told her about the breakfast, the sleeve, his failed attempt to flirt with Ivrassa. Hauren laughed once at that, sharply, then covered her mouth. The older man smiled at the floor.

When Aevra rose to leave, Hauren asked whether she would come again if they thought of more questions.

“Yes.”

“Not tomorrow.”

“No.”

At the door, the smell of bread had begun to fill the room. Aevra carried it with her down the passage until the cooler air took it away.

Aevra walked home by the longer route.

It took her past the workshop where Drelset had first worked for her. Someone else rented it now. New shutters covered the front, and a different name had been painted above the door. The step was the same. He had sat there eating whenever she objected to crumbs on the bench.

She stopped across the passage, pretending to inspect a fastening on a public lamp.

An apprentice came out carrying waste in a bucket. He was younger than Drelset had been when she took him on, and already had the same impatient way of setting down a burden. He nodded to her and went back inside.

Aevra wanted, absurdly, to tell him something. To warn him, or to ask whether he knew what the room had been, or to say that the step was slippery when wet. He turned sideways on the wet step without looking down. She let him pass.

At a food stall she bought two portions of the meal Maeven usually liked. The seller asked about the hand. Aevra gave the short answer. The seller had heard a much larger story involving a Dragon and a destroyed city. Aevra corrected the city. She did not have the strength to correct everything else.

“Alive down there?” the seller asked.

“Yes.”

“Well.”

She put more food into the packet than Aevra had paid for. Aevra thanked her and carried it home with the cord hooked around her good wrist.

Maeven was still working. The packet stayed on the table until the food had cooled. When she came in, she looked surprised that Aevra had brought it, then sat down without washing the last metal stain from her fingers.

They ate quietly. Aevra said she had visited Hauren. Maeven asked whether she wanted to talk about it. She said not yet.

“All right.”

For a little while they sat at the table with nothing being asked of either of them.

The argument with Maeven began over a cupboard.

It had been moved from the kitchen to the little room beside the workshop. Aevra went to fetch a bowl and found an empty wall. She asked where the cupboard was. Maeven told her. Aevra said it had been perfectly well placed where it was.

“It wasn’t,” Maeven said.

Aevra had turned toward the cupboard’s old place all morning. Knowing where it stood now had not changed the turn. The missing bulk behind her shoulder kept drawing her attention from whatever Maeven said. Maeven, meanwhile, moved through the cleared space with an ease Aevra had never seen in the kitchen before.

“I could reach everything.”

“You weren’t here.”

Aevra turned.

Maeven put down the fitting she had been cleaning. “I needed the space. Your aunt came twice a week. The neighbor brought food. People stood in the doorway asking whether there was news. I moved it.”

“I didn’t say you shouldn’t.”

“You said it was perfectly well placed.”

Aevra looked at the empty patch on the wall. It was lighter than the rest.

“I just wanted a bowl.”

“Then get one from the other room.”

She did. With one hand, it took longer than it should have. By the time she returned she was angry enough to make a careless remark about how many things had changed.

Maeven stared at her.

“Yes,” she said. “Things changed.”

Aevra set down the bowl. “I know you were afraid.”

“No. You know you would have been.”

She could not answer that.

Maeven went to the window and opened it. Cool air entered, moving the loose papers on the table.

“I went to work,” she said. “I paid for things. I told people the same thing until I couldn’t bear hearing myself say it. Then someone would come with a rumor and I’d have to begin again.”

Aevra sat.

“I wanted you here every day,” Maeven said. “Now you’re here and you keep asking where I’ve put things.”

Aevra looked at her hand, the clean wrapping, the fingers she had been told not to test.

“I’m sorry.”

Maeven nodded without turning. She did not come over to embrace her. After a while she went back to the fitting.

That evening Aevra learned where the bowls were. After the meal she carried them past the old cupboard mark and put them away, one at a time. Maeven was still talking from the table.

Ausk’s room fell through two days later.

The owner had agreed to a short tenancy, then discovered who would occupy it and decided the room needed repairs. There were repairs needed; Nulith had seen the loose shutter. There had been repairs needed when the money was accepted too.

Ausk came to Aevra’s door carrying their bag.

“I can return to the receiving house tonight,” they said. “I need somewhere after that.”

“Stay here.”

Maeven, behind her, made a small sound.

Aevra closed her mouth.

“Come in,” Maeven said to Ausk. “Sit while we talk about it.”

They discussed the rooms, the hours of work, the light Ausk needed and the amount they could pay. The little room held the moved cupboard and several boxes. It could be cleared, but not by pretending those things occupied no space. Ausk offered to help. Maeven said the heavy box was not to be lifted by anyone until its contents were removed.

Aevra listened.

The arrangement was for two weeks, then another conversation. Ausk would have a curtain and a key to the outer door, use of the basin, and no obligation to perform memories for visitors. Maeven said the last condition before Ausk asked for it.

They looked at her for a long moment.

“Thank you.”

“People will ask,” she said. “They ask me to look at broken fittings at supper.”

Ausk smiled, then looked down at the key.

“Could we have the next conversation before the two weeks ends?”

“How much before?” Maeven asked.

“Three days.” They rubbed the key’s edge with a thumb. “Longer, if you already know.”

Maeven named a day. Ausk asked her to repeat it, then set their bag down beside the chair. They had kept it across their knees throughout the conversation.

That evening they moved the boxes. Aevra carried small things with her good hand. Maeven and Ausk took the cupboard once it was empty. It fitted against the other wall, leaving room for a narrow bed.

Aevra found herself wanting to say that it had been better in the kitchen. She took the last bowl downstairs instead.

Chapter 43 — A lamp at the wake

For a week his father improved.

He ate more, complained more, and insisted on finishing the piece beneath the cloth. Drevik brought the tools to the table and sat beside him while he worked. The old man’s hands tired quickly. When they did, he gave instructions and criticized the speed with which Drevik followed them.

It was a little catch for a household box. Nothing ceremonial. The customer had said there was no hurry and apparently meant it. His father finished it on a morning when the room was bright enough that they did not need the lamp.

“Good,” Drevik said, testing it.

“I know.”

He delivered it himself and brought back the payment. His father counted the coins, accused the customer of overpaying, then remembered he had raised the price before leaving the surface town. They laughed about that. Drevik remembered the laughter afterwards with a greed which frightened him.

One morning Drevik found his father with the family papers spread over the blanket.

The old man had been trying to follow Veirsa’s copy without admitting that the small writing was beyond him. Drevik offered to read it. His father refused, then asked which name came after the third line.

They went through it together.

At the duplicated name, his father insisted again that the old account was right. Drevik fetched the separate sheet showing the two marriages. The old man studied it, moved a finger from one line to another and finally said, “Your aunt will hate that.”

Drevik laughed. “Shall I let you tell her?”

“I’ll write.”

He dictated two sentences. The second was sufficiently unkind about the aunt’s record-keeping that Drevik refused to put it down without warning him how it would sound.

“I know how it sounds.”

“Then you can say it when she visits.”

His father considered this and substituted a more restrained sentence which was somehow more insulting. Drevik wrote it.

They sent neither version. The paper remained beside the lamp, and during the next visit his father forgot to mention it. The aunt brought a dish he liked and complained about the cost of oil. He complained with her. Drevik watched them sit together, their quarrel waiting comfortably among many others which had not needed finishing.

Later, while his father slept, he copied the corrected names onto a clean sheet for the household box. He left the old version behind it. He could not bring himself to destroy his father’s careful lines because some of them were wrong.

He marked the wrong junction plainly, put both sheets away and, for the first time that morning, forgot the box.

For several hours after the payment his father had seemed stronger. He asked to see the coins twice. Drevik kept them in a separate dish because he did not want them disappearing among the household money before the pleasure had gone from them.

The improvement did not continue.

There were more bad nights. The neighbor stayed when she could; other relatives took turns. Drevik learned to sleep through the ordinary cough and wake at the different one. Sometimes he woke when there had been no sound at all and sat listening until dawn.

His father asked about Veirsa again. He wanted to know whether the lower house had been moved, whether Eirvek had found work, whether the cast could be sent to someone who would make a metal impression without damaging it. Drevik answered what he knew. He had no news yet from below.

“Write,” his father said.

“I have.”

“Tell her about the hinge.”

“I did.”

The old man nodded. A few days later he asked the same thing, and Drevik answered again without reminding him.

He died near the end of the third week after Drevik’s release from the receiving rooms.

The night had been no worse at first than others. Drevik had brought water, changed the cloth behind his shoulders, trimmed the lamp. Near morning the breathing altered. The neighbor fetched the healer. Drevik remained beside the bed holding the old man’s hand, which no longer closed around his.

There was no final question. Once his father opened his eyes, but Drevik could not tell whether he saw him. He spoke anyway, saying that he was there, that there was no need to get up, that the lamp was lit.

Afterwards he kept holding the hand until the healer touched his shoulder.

The room became busy around him. Quietly busy, with people asking where things were, what should be brought, whom to send for. He answered badly. The neighbor answered when he could not.

He wanted a few minutes in which nothing was required and hated every minute in which no one spoke to him.

At some point he noticed the lamp had burned low. He stood, trimmed the wick and added oil. The seam remained dry beneath his fingers.

The wake filled the room and spilled into the passage.

Relatives came whom Drevik knew well, relatives he knew only by the way his father had described them, neighbors who had sat through nights and customers who brought stories about work delivered late or repaired without charge. One person brought a box with the new catch on it. Drevik touched the catch and had to leave the room for a moment.

When he returned, Eirvek was helping serve food. He had found the spare cups without asking and was carrying them in a little stack he could manage. Nulith moved behind him with the pot. She spoke to the neighbor as if they had known each other for years.

Aevra and Maeven arrived together. Aevra’s hand was still splinted. She put her right arm around Drevik and held him without trying to find a useful sentence.

The replica stood beside the place prepared for his father. Its flame was low, as the old man preferred. The cast of the original lid lay nearby on its backing board.

An older cousin asked when he had decided not to bring the real lamp.

Drevik looked at him. The question had been asked quietly, perhaps kindly. It still hurt.

“It belongs to Veirsa’s household,” he said. “She uses it.”

“But your father—”

“I know what he wanted.”

The cousin glanced toward the body, then back. “I didn’t mean—”

“I know.”

Drevik did not know. He was too tired to decide.

Later another relative asked about the cast, and he could answer that. He showed the dent and the old hinge repair. Eirvek overheard and added that his grandmother had once dropped a hot spoon into the reservoir by mistake. Drevik had not heard that story. Several people laughed, and Eirvek, encouraged, explained how long the smell had lasted.

No one asked him to stand and describe the lost kingdom. He went back to the cups.

The news arrived the following afternoon, while there were still people in the room.

A carrier from the receiving station had brought a packet handed through several routes. It contained a short account from the lower stores, a note for Thaereth, and a folded letter in Veirsa’s hand. Drevik recognized the mark she had shown him on the family papers.

Her household had reached the downstream rooms. The workshop roof did not leak, which she regarded as an improvement worth mentioning before anything else. Rethven disliked the landing and had already begun discussing alterations with people who had not asked him. The original lamp had survived. One cup had not.

She asked about Eirvek, about the replica seam, about his father. She had written before she could know the answer to the last.

Drevik read the letter twice. Then he took it to Nulith, who read it aloud to Eirvek, stopping once to laugh at a phrase Drevik had not understood.

There was another note passed through Uivren’s station. It did not come from Uivren herself, but said she had left with the carrier as planned and reached the next survey post. Drevik put that aside for Aevra.

The lower relocation had not been easy. Veirsa mentioned a boat turned back, a neighbor who had changed her mind at the last moment, repairs still owed. She did not describe everyone as safe forever. She said who was with her and where a reply could be sent.

That evening Drevik wrote.

He told her his father had died. He told her he had held the cast, asked about her hands and wanted to know more about the workshop. He did not turn those questions into permission which the old man had never given. He described the catch finished in the bright morning and the lamp burning beside him.

Then he wrote about Eirvek, because she had asked and because the boy was alive in the next room arguing with someone about a song.

They sang after the meal.

The tune began with a relative whose voice had once been strong and now needed help on the longer phrases. Others joined. Drevik knew the words she used. At the turning verse, Nulith supplied a lower line under her breath, and Eirvek followed it. The two versions crossed awkwardly.

Someone laughed. The singer stopped, then began the verse again more slowly. Nulith sang hers clearly this time. It was the one about removing boots at the lower door, followed by a line about the person waiting inside being tired of cleaning after everyone.

His father would have liked that, Drevik thought, and for a moment the wanting was so sharp he could not sing.

He listened instead.

The lamp burned through the last verse. Afterwards he carried it to the worktable, set it on a clean cloth and left it lit while people put away the dishes.

Chapter 44 — The curtain

The old pot returned on a day when Aevra had stopped expecting it.

Her sister brought it in both hands, scrubbed cleaner than it had been when borrowed, with something wrapped inside. Maeven looked into it and laughed.

“She’s put the lid back.”

“I always meant to.”

“Which lid?” Aevra asked.

“The right one.”

They looked at each other across the table. Aevra’s sister had brought bread as well. She stayed long enough to ask after the hand, tell them news of the neighbor and criticize the cupboard’s new position. Maeven told her she could put it wherever she liked in her own house.

After she left, Aevra put the pot on the stove.

It had been six weeks since the receiving chamber. Her hand had begun to move again, though two fingers remained stiff and the grip was weak. The healer allowed exercises and forbade lifting. She had returned to the workshop for small tasks which she could do seated, discovering daily how many small tasks were not small to a hand that could not close.

Maeven had stopped asking whether it hurt. Aevra had stopped answering that it was fine.

Earlier that week she had gone to the receiving post with Thaereth.

The journey took most of a morning by maintained passages. Aevra found herself angry at how easy some of them were: the clean steps, the rails fixed at a useful height, the lamps attended before their oil ran low. Then a worker carrying a ladder asked her to move aside, and she did, embarrassed by an anger which had no fair recipient.

At the post, Vaedra showed them the new stores allocation. There was not enough of anything to look generous. There was enough to keep the room staffed while the next survey went down. Two workers were testing the speaking line. A third was cleaning the drainage grate, complaining about material left by the last shift.

Aevra stood where she had sat on admission. The bench had been scrubbed. She could find no mark which proved she had been there.

Thaereth handed over a packet of letters. The station keeper entered each destination in a book and told her which could go with the next carrier and which would have to wait. One of Drevik’s letters was among those waiting. Aevra asked whether the route might open sooner.

“It might.”

She accepted the answer.

An older woman waited behind them with a folded sheet, opening and closing it along a crease already soft with handling. When the keeper asked for its destination, she said Outhren, then tried Vouthra.

“Which household?”

“I don’t know who’s there.” She opened the sheet again. “My mother had a drawing of a court with a tree in it. They used to hang the washing round it. She said her grandfather remembered that.”

The keeper asked to see what she had written. There was a long pause.

“I can send the description. I can’t promise anyone will recognize it.”

The woman nodded. She kept a finger on the paper while he took it, then let go.

Aevra waited beside Thaereth until the keeper had wrapped the letter. She found herself wanting to go after the woman, though she knew nothing about a tree. By then the woman had passed the turn.

On the way out, the keeper gave her a receipt for the bell. Its owner had agreed to payment rather than return, after the repair marks were copied and the purchase recorded.

Thaereth folded the receipt carefully.

“I’ll put it with the original survey.”

“Not in the story about it,” Aevra said.

“What story?”

“The one everyone is telling.”

Thaereth smiled. “This will be a much duller file.”

They stopped for food before going home. Aevra chose something she had disliked as a child and found she still disliked it. Thaereth offered to exchange bowls. She scraped the last of the sauce into Aevra’s bowl before they exchanged.

The evening meal had been planned for several days. Eulva and Thaurik were coming from their new rooms, which were on one level and near a court where Eulva could sit without being carried. She had found an upper cousin through the names in an old letter. They had met, compared two incompatible accounts of the family, and apparently liked each other despite it. Thaurik had found temporary work at a kiln. He still intended to go below again when a reliable route opened. Eulva had not decided whether she would go with him.

Nulith had work repairing packing slings for a carrier. She wanted to send money and letters downriver, then travel herself when the next staffed route could be arranged. Eirvek wanted to remain for a season. He had said so to everyone who asked, including people who had not asked.

Ausk still occupied the little room. The two weeks had become another two weeks, then a longer arrangement with rent and a share of the household work. They had begun doing accounts for a trader who valued their careful hand more than the stories other people hoped to buy. Once, a visitor had asked whether they could recover a dead person’s memory. Ausk said no. Maeven changed the subject before the visitor could explain why an exception should be made.

Aevra had taken the inquiry’s final copy to Drelset’s sister. Hauren had found two errors in the spelling of the workers’ names and asked that they be corrected. The corrections were made. She had not asked Aevra to visit again yet.

There were days when Aevra wanted to knock anyway. She did not.

Drevik arrived early with the lamp.

“You don’t need to bring it every time,” Maeven said.

“I know.”

He put it on the side table. Aevra saw the new braid beginning to darken where he carried it. The seam remained bright.

He had returned to work on a few small commissions. His father’s tools were still in their old order. He said he would move them when he knew where he wanted them. No one at the table advised him to hurry.

He brought a copy of his reply to Veirsa for Nulith to add to. There was space at the bottom. Aevra wrote a few lines for Uivren on a separate sheet, saying the hand was improving and the receiving post remained staffed. She asked whether the replacement lens was still sound. It seemed a better question than asking whether Uivren missed them.

Vaedra had sent word that a survey party was preparing to inspect the lower connection with a guide met through the station. There would be no casual reopening of the old water passage. The work had a list of materials, named shifts and a date which had already moved once. Aevra read the note and put it with the others.

She would have liked to go. She was relieved not to be asked.

The meal was one she had loved since childhood: dark grain cooked slowly with fat, onions, smoked roots and the sharp fermented paste her aunt insisted had to be added at the end. It filled the kitchen with a smell Aevra associated so closely with being fed that she had not considered anyone might dislike it.

Eulva entered and stopped just inside the door.

“Oh,” she said.

“Too warm?” Aevra asked.

“No.”

Thaurik helped her to the chair nearest the cooler room. Nulith came behind them carrying a dish of pale cakes made from something she had found at the lower market stalls. Eirvek immediately reached for one and was told to wait until they were on a plate.

They settled awkwardly at first. The table was too small for all the serving dishes. Drevik moved the lamp. Maeven fetched another stool. Ausk came down with a cloth for the side table and put it where the pots would stand without scorching the wood.

Aevra served the grain. She watched Eulva take a spoonful, pause and swallow with care.

“Not enough salt?”

Eulva looked at the bowl. “No. It’s—”

Thaurik said, “Mother doesn’t like the paste.”

“I can speak.”

“Then speak.”

Eulva set down the spoon. “It’s very strong.”

Aevra felt a small, ridiculous hurt. The pot had come back; the room was full. She looked toward Maeven, who was busy making room for Nulith’s dish and did not rescue her.

“There are cakes,” Nulith said.

“And broth,” Aevra remembered. Eulva had asked for it when they arranged the meal. She had made it and left it at the back of the stove.

She fetched a clean bowl. It was in the cupboard in the other room. She could reach it easily with her right hand now that Maeven had moved the shelf down.

When she returned, Eulva had begun telling Drevik about her upper cousin. Their grandmothers, it seemed, had both claimed ownership of the same silver comb. Neither branch possessed it now. Drevik asked who had sold it. Eulva looked delighted that someone had asked the sensible question.

Aevra set the broth beside her.

“Thank you,” Eulva said, and tasted it at once.

Across the table Eirvek had taken too much of the dark grain and was trying to hide the evidence beneath a cake. Nulith saw and told him he did not have to like everything. He said he did like it. His eyes watered.

Ausk laughed softly.

The brighter light from the court had begun to catch their eyes. They glanced toward the curtain between the kitchen and the little room.

“May I?”

“Of course,” Aevra said.

Ausk drew it halfway, leaving the doorway clear. The light at their end of the table softened. Maeven went into the next room and opened the window a little. Cooler air moved through the house, carrying away some of the heavy smell from the pot.

Drevik passed Eulva the bread. Thaurik began describing a kiln which had been built, in his opinion, by someone who had never fired a pot in his life. Maeven disagreed about the draught and asked him to draw it.

Aevra cleared a space between the bowls. Thaurik took the offered chalk and leaned over the table. Eulva ate her broth while they argued about the chimney.

Appendix — Names and terms

Optional name-finding notes for the expedition and the communities below. Outhren and Vouthra are retained as different usages rather than silently standardized. This reference does not replace the lower households’ accounts of themselves.

Dramatis personae

Aevra — An Aeler passage mason of Kharveth. Maeven is her wife; Drevik is her cousin.

Maeven — A maker of metal fittings, sharing Aevra’s household and a working life of her own.

Drevik; his father — A memorial craftsman raised in a mixed surface town, and the ill father he has accompanied to Kharveth. Their household preserves a drawing of an ancestral lamp.

Thaereth — The officer responsible for the expedition’s authority and the safety of inhabited galleries.

Drelset — A shorer who has worked with Aevra for years.

Baerun; Kouven — The expedition’s hauler and medical orderly.

Ildret; Zerla — Its lampworker and runner.

Uivren — A Mazereth surveyor with routes, colleagues and commitments below the upper holds.

Hesh; Vorrin; Seth — Mycothere residents: the first two share a household; Seth tends a nearby growing bed.

Ausk — A Trow memory-carrier. Uses they/them pronouns. An acquired recollection is not necessarily a reliable guide to the present.

Ivrassa — A Lethai-ar ferrykeeper whose connections reach households along the river.

Veirsa; Rethven; Eirvek; Nulith — A metalworker, her partner, their son, and Eirvek’s aunt.

Eulva; Thaurik — A dyer and her adult son, a potter.

Vaurden; Vaedra — A lower water-record keeper and an upper works mason, respectively. They belong to different communities.

Hauren — Drelset’s sister.

Places and terms

Kharveth — The Aeler hold from which the expedition sets out.

Outhren; Vouthra — Upper and local names associated with the kingdom the expedition seeks.

Orrvek’s porch — The entrance to a family burial place recorded in Drevik’s inherited papers.

The hearth lamp — A bronze lamp with paired wick lips and a hinged carrying bail. The original and the household replica have different dents and repairs.

The Under Realms — Inhabited depths with distinct peoples, routes, economies and dangers. The upper holds’ knowledge of them is partial.

Aeler; Mazereth; Mycotheres; Trow; Lethai-ar — Different peoples encountered in the novel. Shared ancestry does not give the Aeler one kingdom or a single regional way of life.

Drakes and wyrms; Dragons — The former are dangerous animals. Intelligent Dragons are rare; their interests are their own.

A shore — A temporary support for unstable work. Witness pegs record movement across a crack. A receiving chamber controls passage between routes or occupied works.
